Sequences and Infinitary Intersection Types
Pierre Vial

Abstract
. We introduce a new representation of non-idempotent intersection types, using sequences (families indexed with natural numbers) instead of lists or multisets. This allows scaling up intersection type theory to the infinitary λ \lambda -calculus. We thus characterize hereditary head normalization , which gives a positive answer to a question known as Klop’s Problem . On our way, we use non-idempotent intersection to retrieve some well-known results on infinitary terms.



1.
Presentation

Intersection types, introduceed by Coppo and Dezani
[
CDC78
,
CDC80a
,
MC81
]
, are a powerful tool to describe and characterize the semantic properties of programs. For instance, compared to simple types, they type more terms, such as
\Delta:=\lambda x.x\,x
, the self-application, and they allow building models
[
BCDC83
,
ABD06
,
PPR17
]
for the
\lambda
-calculus. They also allow capturing observational equivalence (see
[
Man17
]
for instance).
Various sets of
\lambda
-terms have been characterized with (a form of) typability within intersection type systems.

The question was naturally raised to know whether some sets of programs defined in terms of infinitary behaviors (for instance, as captured by Böhm trees), such as the set of hereditary permutators (TLCA Problem # 20) could be characterized in such a system.
In particular, this led Klop and Dezani to formulate the following question
[
Tat08a
]
: can the set of
hereditary head normalizing terms
(
i.e.
, the terms whose Böhm tree does not contain
\bot
) be characterized by means of an intersection type system? This question was known as
Klop’s Problem
.
One may perhaps undestand better the definition of hereditary head normalizing terms by interpreting them as a generalization of weakly normalizing terms (terms which have a
\beta
-normal form)
in that,
t
is hereditary head normalizing if, at any fixed depth
d
,
t
can be reduced to a normal form up to subterms which occur below depth
d
. Such a term may not have a normal form: this occurs whenever redexes cannot be completely eliminated by reducing
t
, even if the redexes (of the reducts) of
t
keep on increasing in depth. For instance,
\mathtt{Y}_{f}:=\Delta_{f}\,\Delta_{f}
(with
\Delta_{f}=\lambda x.f(x\,x)
) a variant of Curry fixpoint that will be the case study of this article, satisfies
\mathtt{Y}_{f}\rightarrow_{\mathtt{h}}f(\mathtt{Y}_{f})
(it operational semantics is given in Fig.
1
). Thus, after
n
steps of reductions,
\mathtt{Y}_{f}
gives
f^{n}(\mathtt{Y}_{f})
(shortening
f(f(\ldots(\mathtt{Y}_{f}))))
with
n
occurrences of
n
), which, on one hand, shows that
\mathtt{Y}_{f}
is not weakly normalizing, since its reducts all have a (unique) redex. On another hand, the only redex of
f^{n}(\mathtt{Y}_{f})
occurs at depth
n
, which means that, above depth
n
,
f^{n}(\mathtt{Y}_{f})
is an normal form. Asymptotically, when
n
converges towards
\infty
, the computation of
\mathtt{Y}_{f}
outputs an infinite
\lambda
-tree (corresponding to a infinite application of
f
, which we call
f^{\infty}
) which does not contain any redex: in other words, an infinite normal form. And this is the core reason why hereditary head normalization pertains to an infinitary behavior.

\mathtt{Y}_{f}
\char 64\relax
f
\mathtt{Y}_{f}
\char 64\relax
f
\char 64\relax
f
\mathtt{Y}_{f}
\char 64\relax
f
\char 64\relax
f
\char 64\relax
f
\mathtt{Y}_{f}
\char 64\relax
f
\char 64\relax
f
\char 64\relax
f
\char 64\relax
f
\mathtt{Y}_{f}\longrightarrow_{\beta}
f(\mathtt{Y}_{f})\longrightarrow_{\beta}
f^{2}(\mathtt{Y}_{f})\longrightarrow_{\beta}
f^{3}(\mathtt{Y}_{f})\longrightarrow^{*}_{\beta}
f^{n}(\mathtt{Y}_{f})

Figure 1.
\mathtt{Y}_{f}
, a Hereditary Normalizing Term

However, Tatsuta
[
Tat08a
,
Tat08b
]
proved in 2008 that “infinitary behaviors” could not be characterized in an inductive, finitary setting. In particular, the answer to Klop’s Problem is negative in the inductive case, as Tatsuta showed by:

•

making the observation that, in an inductive type system, the set of typable terms is recursively enumerable.

•

proving that the set of hereditary head normalizing termsis
not
recursively enumerable.

In turn, this result raised the
question of whether such a characterization could be obtained by means of an
infinitary/coinductive intersection type system
.

In this article, we prove that it is possible and we give a type-theoreric characterization of hereditary head normalization using
non-idempotent intersection
. Non-idempotent intersection type systems were introduced in 1980 by Coppo, Dezani and Veneri
[
CDC80b
]
before being rediscovered by Gardner
[
Gar94
]
and de Carvalho in his PhD
[
Car07
,
dC18
]
, which emphasized they quantitative aspect. They have been since extensively studied (read
[
BKV17
]
for a survey).
Non-idempotent intersection
\wedge
does not satisfy the equality
A\wedge A=A
as the set-theoretic intersection does (
X\cap X=X
). In its simplest form of this system, non-intersection is represented with
multisets
(the multiset
[A,B,A]
stands for the intersection
A\wedge B\wedge A
) and gives a typing system with only three rules, which is recalled in Sec.
3.4
.
Then, reduction inside a derivation almost boils down to
moving
parts of the initial derivation (see Fig.
13
and discussion in Sec.
5.4
) and the proof of soundness (if a term is typable, then it is head normalizing) becomes trivial, since reduction decreases a positive integral measure on derivations.

To solve Klop’s Problem, we will use a quantitative, resource-aware type system.
However, it turns out that a direct coinductive adaptation of
infinite multisets
cannot work for two major reasons:

•

It would lead to the possibility of typing some unsolvable terms
1
1

1

We actually proved that the coinductive version of
\mathscr{R}_{0}
types every term can typed in
\mathscr{R}
, the coinductive version of
\mathscr{R}_{0}

[
Via18
]
, like
\Omega
. That is why a validity criterion is needed to discard irrelevant derivations, as in other infinitary frameworks
[
San01
]
. This validity criterion relies on the idea of
approximability
(every valid infinite derivation must be, in some sense, a limit of finite derivations), which we informally develop in Sec.
2.4
,
3.6
,
3.7
and formally define in Sec.
6
.

•

Moreover, as it turns out, multisets are not fit to formally express such a notion, because they do not allow pointing, meaning for instance that we cannot distinguish the two occurrences of
\sigma
in
[\sigma,\tau,\sigma]
.
This motivates the need for rigid constructions: multisets
are then (coinductively) replaced by
sequences
,
i.e.
, families indexed by (non necessarily consecutive) natural numbers called
tracks
(
e.g.
,
(2\cdot\sigma,3\cdot\tau,9\cdot\sigma)
instead of
[\sigma,\tau,\sigma]
). We explain all this in Sec.
2.4
below.

This leads us to define a type system that we call system
\mathtt{S}
, in which intersection is represented by sequences of types (intersection is said to be sequential). Tracks, which act as
identifiers
, constitute the main feature of system
\mathtt{S}
presented here.
With those pointers, any type can be tracked through the rules of a whole typing derivation, and the notionsof approximation and of limit of a family of derivation can be defined.

This contribution was presented in
[
Via17
]
with some proof sketches. Here, not only we provide full proofs (which are detailled in appendices), but in the core of the paper, we detail a crucial
argument of soundness
(if a term is suitably typable, then it has a finite or infinite normal form). We also explain in more details how infinite normal forms can be suitably typed.

The expert reader on intersection types may be surprised by the following elements: while the subject reduction property (in its usual form) remains simple to prove, soundness (typability implies normalization) and (infinitary) subject expansion are quite more technical than in the usual, finite non-idempotent case.
The use of a validity criterion constrains us to prove subject reduction while considering residuals (Sec.
5.3
and
5.5
). Approximability (derivation are limits of finite ones) raises a problem with the left-hand side of judgments, which can be overcome by showing that they can be, in some sense, ignored (Sec.
6.2
). Typing normal forms
soundly
is also technical, because we must check that it can be done while respecting approximability (Sec.
7.1
to
7.3
).
At last, infinitary subject expansion is a subtle process, based on cutting infinite derivations into finite ones, proceeding to a substitution in a reduction path of infinite length followed by a finite number of expansion stepsn, and then considering a suitable join of the underlying constructions. It is incrementally explained in Sec.
2.3
,
3.6
and
7.4
.

Structure of the paper.

This article is organized as follows: in Sec.
2
, we recall the mechanisms of intersection types on which we will base our approach and we present the main difficulties we shall have to overcome, along with a sketch of the tools we develop in this article. In Sec.
3
, we formally recall the
infinitary

\lambda
-calculus and the
finitary
non-idempotent intersection type system of Gardner-de Carvalho (system
\mathscr{R}_{0}
) which use multisets to represent intersection. Sec.
3.4
is partly informal: we explain how we will be able to perform later in this article infinitary subject expansion (bring a typing backwards along an infinite sequence of reduction steps) and why this actually cannot be done with multiset intersection. In Sec.
4
, we formally define system
\mathtt{S}
and sequence types. In Sec.
5
, we study the
one-step
dynamics of system
\mathtt{S}
: we define residuals and we prove a deterministic subject reduction property and the subject expansion property
Approximability is defined in Sec.
6
and the infinitary subject reduction property and the normalization of typable terms are proved. Sec.
7
is devoted to typing infinite normal forms and proving the infinitary subject expansion property. This concludes the study of Klop’s Problem and of hereditary head normalization.

This brings us to the main contribution of this article, Theorem
4
: from Sec.
3.6
to Sec.
7
, we solve Klop’s Problem: we give a type theoretic characterization of hereditary head normalization. A general presentation of the content of the appendices, in which we give technical arguments that may be missing, is given on p.
Contents of the appendices.
.

2.
Introduction

In this section, we first recall basic notions on normalization, then we present some aspects of intersection types. We then devote our attention to Klop’s Problem and the technical difficulties we had to overcome and the proof techniques we had to introduce to export intersection type theories to an infinitary setting and to characterize hereditary head normalization. Last, we present two main tools that we use in this article: sequences and approximations/truncations of derivations.

2.1.
Normalization

Evaluation strategies

We consider the following restrictions of
\beta
-reduction:

•

The
head reduction
, denoted
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{h}}
, consists in reducing the
head redex

(\lambda x.r)s
of a
head reducible
term
\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{p}.(\lambda x.r)s\,t_{1}\ldots t_{q}
(notice that any term that is not a HNF is head reducible). A term
t
is
Head Normalizing (HN)
if the head reduction terminates on
t
(and necessarily outputs a head normal form).

•

The
leftmost-outermost reduction
, denoted
\rightarrow_{\ell}
, consists in reducing the
leftmost-outermost redex
of a
\beta
-reducible term
t
,
i.e.
, the one whose
\lambda x
is the leftmost one in
t
(seen as string of characters). A term
t
is
Weakly Normalizing (WN)
if leftmost-outermost reduction terminates on
t
(and necessarily outputs a full normal form).

•

The
hereditary head reduction strategy
(formally defined in Sec.
3.3
) also aims at computing a full normal form: for a given term
t
, it starts by applying the head reduction strategy on
t
until it stops (otherwise, it loops). In that case, the current term is of the form
\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{p}.x\,t_{1}\ldots t_{q}
. Then one applies the head reduction strategy on each head argument
t_{1},\ldots,\;t_{q}
until they are all in head normal forms (if it does not loop for one of the
t_{i}
). Then one goes on with head reducing each new argument at constant applicative depth and so on. The strategy stops if a
\beta
-normal form is reached. Hereditary head reduction may be seen as a more balanced variant of leftmost-outermost reduction. Moreover,
when hereditary head reduction strategy does not stop but keeps on reducing deeper and deeper, it may be seen as asymptotically computing
an infinite term, as it was recalled above.

Having a normal form

Normalization may also be associated with reduction paths of unspecified forms (without referring to evaluation strategies). In this article, we mainly consider the two predicates:

•

A
\lambda
-term
t

has a head normal form
if there is a reduction path from
t
to a head normal form.

•

A term
\lambda
-term
t

has a full normal form
if there is a reduction path from
t
to a full normal form.

Notice that having a head normal form seems to be more general/less constrained than
head normalization: the former pertains to the existence of a reduction path (to a head normal form) of an
unspecified
form whereas the head reduction reduces terms in a
deterministic
way.
Thus, if the head reduction strategy terminates on a term
t
, then
t
clearly has a head normal form.
Moreover, it is well-known of that the converse of these two implications is true,
i.e.
, if a term
t
has a head normal form then the head reduction strategy terminates on
t
.

2.2.
Intersection Types

We recall and discuss here some aspects of intersection types that will interest us in this article. All these aspects are illustrated in system
\mathscr{R}_{0}
in the forthcoming Sec.
3.4
.

Normalization (termination) is a
dynamic
property: it pertains to the reduction of
\lambda
-terms,
i.e.
, their
evaluation
. For instance, proving that a term
t
is head normalizing consists (in principle) in giving a sequence of
\beta
-reduction steps starting at
t
and ending with a head normal form.

On another hand, typing is
static
in the following sense: in a given type system, finding a derivation typing a term
t
or checking that a given tree of typing judgments is a correct derivation is done without having to perform reduction steps but just by considering the structure of the tree and verifying that the typing rules are correctly applied.

One of the desirable features of type systems is that some of them
ensure
normalization,
e.g.
, if a term
t
is typable in Girard’s system F, then it is strongly normalizing
[
Gir72
,
GLT90
]
(
i.e.
, no infinite reduction path starts at
t
). Thus, type systems provide
static
proofs of
dynamic
properties. In some systems, when we have managed to type a program
t
, we do not need to execute
t
to know that
t
is terminating.
However, usually, when typability is related to termination,
e.g.
, in polymorphic or dependent type systems, it is a
sufficient
condition for normalization but not a
necessary
one: many normalizing terms are not typable. For instance,
\Delta=\lambda x.x\,x
is not typable in the simply typed
\lambda
-calculus, and
(\lambda z.\lambda y.y(z\,\mathtt{I})(z\,\mathtt{K}))(\lambda x.x\,x)
, although strongly normalizing, is not typable in system F.

The basic idea behind intersection type systems is allowing assigning several types to variables or to terms. They often
characterize
(and not only ensure) normalization properties (head, weak, weak head, strong…): in an intersection type system, typability is usually equivalent with (a notion of) normalization. Since the
\lambda
-calculus is Turing-complete, this makes type inference undecidable in most intersection type systems.
As an exception to this rule, intersection types were famously used by Kobayashi and Ong
[
KO09
]
to prove that Monadic Second Order logic is decidable for higher-order recursion schemes.

Normalization theorems in intersection type theory.
Intersection type systems often give interesting properties pertaining to reduction strategies. To illustrate this, let us imagine for a moment that we have an intersection type system
\mathcal{H}
characterizing head normalization,
i.e.
, that satisfies the following theorem (let us call it
T_{\text{HN}}
):

“
Theorem
T_{\text{HN}}
:
for every
\lambda
-term
t
,
t
is is typable in system
\mathcal{H}
iff
t
is head normalizing ”

In practice, this characterization theorem is proved in two steps:

\Rightarrow

A proof of “If
t
is
\mathcal{H}
-typable (typable in system
\mathcal{H}
), then the head reduction
strategy
terminates on
t
”.

\Leftarrow

A proof of “If there is a reduction path from
t
to a HNF, then
t
is
\mathcal{H}
-typable”.

Soundness

Typability implies

normalization

Completeness

Normalization implies

typability

some reduction strategy normalizes
t

e.g., the head red. strat.
terminates on
t

t
has a normal form

e.g.,
\exists
red. path

from
t
to a HNF

t
is typable

Figure 2.
Characterizing a notion of normalization with intersection types

Observe that for
\Rightarrow
, we actually prove that a typable
t
is terminating for the head reduction strategy, which is
a priori
stronger than just the fact that
t
has a head normal form. This proves that, in some sense, head reduction is
complete
since it computes a head normal form when it exists. Since the termination of head reduction obviously implies having a head normal form, the implication
\Leftarrow
gives us theorem
T_{\text{HN}}
but also the following, non-trivial statement:

“A term
t
is head normalizing

iff it is terminating for the head reduction strategy.”

A remarkable aspect of this equivalence is that it is
not
type-theoretic: it states that a certain reduction strategy is complete for a certain notion of normalization. But it was proved using types instead of syntactic techniques. The structure of this kind of proof is summarized in Fig.
2
. The arrow on the right (typability implies normalization, or soundness) usually relies on subject reduction, whereas the arrow on the left (normalization implies typability, or completeness) relies on subject expansion (typing is stable under expansion).
To the best knowledge of the author, subject expansion is specific to intersection type system, unless it is hard-coded in the typing rules.
Indeed, to perform subject expansion (going from a typing of
r[s/x]
to one of
(\lambda x.r)s
), the variable
x
can be assigned
several
types and thus, be used as a placeholder for
s
, which can occur with several different types in
r[s/x]
.

Partial typings.

The possibility to partially type terms will be crucial in the core of this article, when we need “truncate” infinite derivations typing infinite terms into finite derivations:
many intersection type systems also feature a universal type
U
which can be assigned to every term, so that a judgment of the form
t:U
is meaningless. However, such a meaningless
U
allows
partially
typing a term. Actually, every head normal form becomes typable: if one assigns
U\rightarrow\ldots\rightarrow U\rightarrow A
(with
n
arrows) and types the
t_{i}
with
U
, then
x\,t_{1}\ldots t_{q}
has type
A
).
The possibility to leave some arguments untyped is crucial for type systems to remain sound while ensuring semantic guarantees are less restrictive than strong normalization (
e.g.
, head or weak normalization).
When considering such a universal type
U
, every term becomes typable and thus, typed judgments should be considered sound only when
U
does not have ill-placed occurrences, called positive occurrences (technically, an occurrence is positive when it is nested in an even number of arrow domains).

Non-Idempotent Typing, Reduction and Decrease

Non-idempotent intersection

When intersection operator is not idempotent
[
CDC80b
,
Gar94
,
dC18
]
, the types
A
and
A\wedge A
are not equivalent anymore. This expresses the fact that, from the quantitative point of view, using the type
A
once or twice is not the same. Thus, non-idempotent intersection is closely related to Girard’s Linear Logic
[
Gir87
]
. For instance, non-idempotent type systems keep information on how many times a variable has been assigned a type. A powerful consequence of this is that reduction cause derivations to decrease in size during reduction, meaning that if
\Pi
concludes with
\Gamma\vdash t:\tau
and
t
head-reduces to
t^{\prime}
, then
\Pi^{\prime}
, the derivation reduct of
\Pi
concluding with
\Gamma\vdash t^{\prime}:\tau
, contains stricly less judgments than
\Pi
. This illustrated with Fig.
3
. This entails that it is trivial that a typed term is head normalizing.

2.3.
Solving Klop’s Problem

Now that we have recalled some useful fact about intersection types, we explain in this section what are the difficulties we will meet and overcome in this article to export (non-idempotent) intersection type theory to an infinitary setting.

Soundness in an infinitary setting, and the need of a fine-grained notion of residuation.

In the case of finitary non-idempotent intersection type, soundness is just based on the subject reduction property inducing the decrease of a positive integral measure and is comparatively very simple to prove. In the infinitary case, things are different: the soundness of a derivation
P
is ensured provided
P
is, in some sense, the limit object of
finite
derivations (this is the core of the
approximability
criterion). To be more precise:

•

Defining infinite derivations by just allowing infinitary multiplicites and nestings in multisets is unsound: for instance, the looping term
\Omega
becomes typable. This is why we need the approximability criterion (Sec.
3.7
).

•

If a term
t
is typed with an approximable derivation, which is a possibly infinite derivation that can be written as a limit (or the join) of finite derivations, then
t
is necessarily typable in the finitary, usual setting, which ensures soundness (in the sense that, in the finite case, if
t
is typable, then it is head normalizing).

•

But the necessity of handling the notion of approximability while reducing derivations (
i.e.
, reducing there subjects) constrain us to have a more meticulous description of the subject reduction property. Indeed, we need to show that approximability is stable under reduction. But for this, as it will be made clear in the paper, we need to have a precise, fine-grained notion of
residuation
(Sec.
5.3
and
5.5
).

•

At last, residuation raises a problem of its own.
As we shall see, residuation can be suitably defined only for the right-hand sides
t:T
of typing judgments
C\vdash t:T
(where
C
is the context,
t
the subject and
T
the type), whereas approximability pertains to their left-hand sides too. We explain how to overcome this problem with the crucial notion of
equinecessity
(Sec.
6.2
).

The infinite jump problem, or infinitary subject expansion.

Another problem in the infinitary setting is ensuring
infinitary subject expansion
: actually, proving that typing is stable under one-step expansion is not really more difficult than it is in the finite case. However, a suitable notion of reduction path of infinite length (the so)-called
productive paths
, Sec.
3.3
) naturally arises when we deal with infinite terms and Böhm tree
[
KKSdV97
]
. As a side remark, one-step subject reduction and even infinitary subject reduction along a productive path are also trivial (as long as we do not consider the question of approximability): the latter can be also thought as a limit process of a finite number of reduction steps. In other words, one may say that infinitary subject reduction is an asymptotic phenomenon.
However, the situation is different for infinitary subject expansion, because intuitively there is
a priori
no way to pass
incrementally
2
2

2

To give an idea, if we consider a sequence of rational numbers
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
which converges to a limit
x
(which is rational or irrational), then passing from
x
to any
x_{n}
means going backward on
\infty-n
(which is also
\infty
) many steps! In this respect, this is not incremental.
from a limit object to one of a finite rank (let us call that very informally the
infinite jump problem
). Infinitary subject expansion is not asymptotic in nature. But as, we shall see in Sec.
3.6
, infinitary subject expansion can be performed and we may already give some elements of understanding on the involved techniques: on one hand, our notion of soundness—approximability—specifies that infinite derivations are
static
limits of finite ones (by static, we mean that the definition of approximability is independent from any notion of reduction). On another hand, we have the usual notion of productive reduction paths of infinite length in the infinitary
\lambda
-calculus, which defines a
dynamic
notion of limit of a family of term: this limit is intuitvely the term which is asymptotically outputted after an infinite number of reduction steps, as in the example of
\mathtt{Y}_{f}
on page
3.4
.

Interestingly enough, the core technique used to perform infinitary subject expansion is also based on approximability, and actually, on confronting these two notions (static
vs.
dynamic) of limits: we use the fact that derivations are static limits of finite derivations to (1) perform infinite subject expansion on these finite derivations (for which the infinite jump probem has a solution, Sec.
3.5
) (2) statically build a (possibly infinite) expanded derivation by taking the join of the resulting finite expanded derivations.

Thus, approximability is pivotal in two respects: (1) it is used to define which infinitary derivations are semantically valid and which are not (2) it is used to perform infinitary subject expansion.

The problem of pointing.
A last technical ingredient to adapt intersection type theory to an infinitary setting is what we call
sequences
in this articles: multisets of elements which are decorated with suitable natural numbers which we call
tracks
.
In non-idempotent intersection type theory, it is convenient (but not mandatory) to represent the intersection of types
A_{1}
,…,
A_{k}
with the multiset
[A_{1},\ldots,A_{k}]
: it allows not needing anymore structural rules and simplifying the type system. However, it has two drawbacks for us: it makes it impossible to have a
pointing mechanism
(in the multiset
[A,B,A]
, it is impossible to point formally to one specific occurrence of
A
) and it gives rise to
non-determinism in proof reduction
as it is well-known (Sec.
5.4
).
Both problems, as we shall see, make it impossible to define approximability and ensure soundness. When elements are decorated with tracks (for instance,
[A,B,A]
may become
(2\cdot A,4\cdot B,7\cdot A)
or
(3\cdot A,2\cdot B,9\cdot A)
), we recover pointing and determinism. For instance, in
(2\cdot A,4\cdot B,7\cdot A)
, one occurrence of
A
is on track 2 (
i.e.
, is decorated with 2) and the other is on track 7. Actually, this tracking mechanism enables a fine-grained description of residuation on typing derivation and prove soundness, as we saw just above.

2.4.
A Glimpse at Sequences and Derivation Approximations

Now, let us give a glimpse at the notion of
sequences
(which refines that of multisets) and of approximation of a derivation.

Sequences and tracking.

Naive multisets do not allow defining a notion of position: we say that they do not allow
tracking
. To see this, consider the equality
[A,B,A]=[A,B]+[A]
. There is no way to associate the occurrence of
A
in
[A]
, located in the right-hand side of the equality to one of the two occurrences of
A
in
[A,B,A]
, in the left-hand side. This shows that there is not way to point to a particular occurrence of
A
inside
[A,B,A]
. Actually, if we swap the two occurrences of
A
in
[A,B,A]
, we obtain

[A,B,A]
. If we do not do anything, we also obtain
[A,B,A]
: the possible swap cannot be seen. It has actually no formal meaning.

To retrieve tracking and thus, the possibility to point at an element, we may annotate elements of multisets with pairwise distinct integers, that we call
tracks
. Such a decorated multiset is called a
sequence
and we use the constructor
(\_)
for sequences instead of
[\_]
for multisets.
For instance,
(2\cdot A,3\cdot B,5\cdot A)
is a sequence, there is
one occurrence
of
A

on track
2 and another on track 5. Sequences come along with a disjoint union operator
\uplus
,
e.g.
,
(2\cdot A,3\cdot B,5\cdot A)=(2\cdot A,3\cdot B)\uplus(5\cdot A)
. Thanks to tracks, each occurrence of
A
in one side of the equality are unambiguously associated with another on the other side, and in the left-hand side, we may point to the occurrence of
A
on track 2 rather than the one on track 5. Disjoint union is not defined for two
sequences sharing a same track,
e.g.
,
(2\cdot A,3\cdot B)\uplus(3\cdot B,5\cdot A)
is
not
defined (because the two operands both use track 3, even though the same type
B
occurs): we say that there is a
track conflict
.
Moreover,
\uplus
is a
commutative
and associative, but
partial
operator. Observe that
(2\cdot A,3\cdot B,5\cdot A)
may be written six different ways,
e.g.
,
(5\cdot A,2\cdot A,3\cdot B)
and
(2\cdot A,5\cdot A,3\cdot B)
: if we perform a swap in a sequence, we see it.

Our framework is deterministic,
e.g.
, there is a
unique
canonical way to produce a derivation from another one when reducing a redex, contrary to system
\mathscr{R}_{0}
, and we can characterize infinitary semantics (more details in Sec.
5.4
).

To sum up, in many ways, sequence types work like multiset types,
e.g.
, they give rise to a syntax-directed, strict and relevant typing system with only three rules, but they allow tracking, constructing a suitable pointing mechanism inside derivations and thus, defining approximability, the validity criterion that will help us discard the unsound coinductive derivations. This will enable us characterizing hereditary head normalization.

Approximation.
As we have hinted at in Sec.
2.3
, coinductive type grammar give rise to
unsound
derivations,
e.g.
, derivations typing of
\Omega
,
but we introduce a
validity criterion
allowing to discard them. This criterion is called
approximability
.
Intuitively, an infinite proof/typing derivation is approximable when it is obtained by superposing infinitely many
finite
proofs, growing over and over, as in Fig.
4
: the outer triangle represents an infinite proof/typing derivation
\Pi
and the inner polygons represent finite proofs that “fit” in
\Pi
.
Thus, an approximable derivation may be infinite, but it is asymptotically obtained from finite/sound proofs.
Equivalently, a derivation is approximable when it is the
join
of its
finite

truncations
, also called
approximations
. The crucial intuition behind this notion is that it allows “reducing” infinite derivations to finite ones along with the
soundness properties
that come with them, such as head normalization.

Figure 4.
An Approximable Derivation as an Infinite Superposition

More precisely, derivations need not being infinite to characterize head normalization in the infinite calculus: infinite derivations are required to characterize hereditary head normalization in an infinitary calculus, because we need to type infinite normal forms without leaving any subterm untyped. Moreover, finite derivations allow us to prove operational properties of the infinite calculus in a relatively simple way (more details in Sec.
3.5
). An important idea is that
any term which is approximably typable is finitarily typable and as such, is head normalizing
. The whole proof of correctness of the system (w.r.t. hereditary head normalization) is based on this observation.

Just to give a more precise idea of what is meant by superposition, the derivation fragment
\Pi_{2}
below can be superposed upon
\Pi_{1}
(we omit the typing assumptions and the possible premises of the top judgments):

\Pi_{1}={\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\mathtt{f}:(A\wedge B)\rightarrow A\rightarrow C\hskip 14.22636pt\mathtt{x}:A\hskip 14.22636pt\mathtt{x}:B$}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=147.40492pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 47.43803pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathtt{f}(\mathtt{x}):A\rightarrow C$}}}}

\Pi_{2}={\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\mathtt{f}:(A\wedge B\wedge(B\rightarrow C))\rightarrow(A\wedge C)\rightarrow D\hskip 14.22636pt\mathtt{x}:A\hskip 14.22636pt\mathtt{x}:B\hskip 14.22636pt\mathtt{x}:B\rightarrow D$}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=272.47525pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 96.59755pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathtt{f}(\mathtt{x}):(A\wedge C)\rightarrow C$}}}}

Indeed,
\Pi_{1}
is obtained from
\Pi_{2}
by removing the symbols colored in red:

\Pi_{2}={\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\mathtt{f}:(A\wedge B{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\wedge(B\rightarrow C)})\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(}A\wedge{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}C)}\rightarrow D\hskip 14.22636pt\mathtt{x}:A\hskip 14.22636pt\mathtt{x}:B\hskip 14.22636pt{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathtt{x}:B\rightarrow D}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=264.69766pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 96.59755pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathtt{f}(\mathtt{x}):{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(}A\wedge{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}C)}\rightarrow C$}}}}

Thus,
\Pi_{1}
can be seen as a
truncation
or an
approximation
of
\Pi_{2}
.
Infinite superposition strongly hints at the presence of complete lattices and complete partial orders, which is formalized in Sec.
6
.

With this concept of approximation, we retrieve, in an infinitary setting, the main tools of finite intersection type theory that we sketched in Sec.
2.2
:
subject reduction, subject expansion, typing of normal forms). Actually, following the scheme of Fig.
2
, we will prove that hereditary head reduction (Sec.
3.2
) is
asymptotically
complete for infinitary weak normalization.

3.
Finite Intersection and Infinite Terms

In Sec.
3.2
and
3.3
, we define the infinitary
\lambda
-calculus and Böhm trees.
Gardner-de Carvalho’s system
\mathscr{R}_{0}
is recalled in Sec.
3.4
, in particular how weak normalization is characterized by considering
unforgetful
derivations. In Sec.
3.5
, we explain how system
\mathscr{R}_{0}
can still be applied to infinite terms. We encounter a first use of subject substitution,
i.e.
, replacing the subject
t
of a derivation
\Pi
by another
u
which is equal to
t
in the typed parts of
\Pi
. We use this to perform an
infinite
subject expansion on
finite
derivations.
We present the main ideas to solve Klop’s Problem in Sec.
3.6
and
3.7
: we informally explain (1) how infinitary subject expansion
could
be performed by truncating derivations and taking the joins of directed families (2) how coinduction gives rise
unsound
derivations,
e.g.
, derivations typing the unterminating term
\Omega
and how soundness can be retrieved with
approximability
.

3.1.
Pointing in a
\lambda
-term

Variable
x
x
Abstraction
\lambda x.u
u
0
\lambda x
Application
u\,v

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}
v
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
u
\char 64\relax

Figure 5.
Lambda Terms as Labelled Trees

Lambda terms can be seen as labelled trees following the pattern of Fig.
5
.
We orient these labelled trees as typing derivation usually are: the root is put at the bottom of the figure (and not at the top), because the derivations of the type system to come can be represented as refinements of parsing trees of
\lambda
-terms (see in particular Fig.
11
on p.
11
). We say that
u
is the
argument
of the application
t\,u
.
This representation gives to the standard notions of
positions
in a
\lambda
-term, represented as words on
\{0,1,2\}
(the set of words on
\{0,1,2\}
is denoted
\{0,1,2\}^{*}
), and
support

\mathtt{supp}(t)
of a term
t
(the set of positions in
t
). If
b\in\mathtt{supp}(t)
, then
t(b)
is the label at position
b
in
t
and
t|_{b}
is the subterm rooted at
b
.
For instance, if
t:=\lambda x.x\,y
, then
\mathtt{supp}(t)=\{\varepsilon,0,0\cdot 1,0\cdot 2\}
where
\varepsilon
is the empty word,
t(\varepsilon)=\lambda x
,
t(0)=\char 64\relax
,
t(0\cdot 1)=x
,
t(0\cdot 2)=y
and
t|_{0}=x\,y
. The
applicative depth

ad
of a position
b
in a term
t
is the number of times that
b
is nested inside the arguments of an application. For instance, if
u_{1}\,u_{2}\,u_{3}\,u_{4}
are terms and
t=\lambda x.((y\,u_{1})((y\,u_{2})u_{3}))u_{4}
,
b_{1}=0\cdot 1^{2}\cdot 2
,
b_{2}=0\cdot 1\cdot 2\cdot 1\cdot 2
,
b_{3}=0\cdot 1\cdot 2^{2}
,
b_{4}=0\cdot 1
, then
t|_{b_{i}}=u_{i}
and
\mathtt{ad}(b_{1})=1
,
\mathtt{ad}(b_{2})=\mathtt{ad}(b_{3})=2
,
\mathtt{ad}(b_{4})=0
. In other words, if
b=b_{0}\cdot b_{1}\ldots\cdot b_{n}
(with
b_{i}
integer), then
\mathtt{ad}(b)=\#\{i\in\{0,\ldots,n\}\,|\,b_{i}=2\}
,
i.e.
,
\mathtt{ad}(b)
is the number of 2 that
b
contains.

3.2.
Infinite Lambda Terms

In this section, we present Böhm trees (chapter 10 of Barendregt
[
Bar85
]
, in particular, Theorem 10.4.2.),
a notion that was suggested by the proof of the Böhm Separation Theorem,
and the construction of one of the infinitary calculi introduced in
[
KKSdV97
]
. See also
[
Cza14
,
EHH+15
,
Bah18
]
for alternative presentations.
This infinitary calculus is a natural framework which allows expressing Böhm trees (more precisely, its normal forms are Böhm trees without
\bot
), but it gives us more flexibility. For instance, the computation of an infinite normal form does not need to follow the path given by hereditary head normalization and we have a general notion of
sound
computations of infinite length, that we call here
productive reduction paths
. We shall generalize some notions to the whole infinitary calculus (for instance, hereditary head normalization will pertain both to finite and infinite terms), since it gives a general framework where they can be expressed.

We now generalize some of the notations of Sec.
3
in an infinitary setting.
Let
\mathscr{V}
be a countable set of term variables. The set
\Lambda^{\infty}
of infinitary
\lambda
-terms is
coinductively
defined by:

t,u~:=~x\in\mathscr{V}~\|~(\lambda x.t)~\|~(t\char 64\relax u)

When there is no ambiguity, we usually just write
\lambda x.t
and
t\,u_{1}\ldots u_{n}
instead of
(\lambda x.t)
and
(\ldots(t\char 64\relax u_{1})\ldots\char 64\relax u_{n})
. If
t
is an infinitary term, then
\mathtt{supp}(t)
, the
support
of
t
(the set of positions in
t
) is defined
by
\mathtt{supp}(x)=\{\varepsilon\}
,
\mathtt{supp}(\lambda x.t)=\{\varepsilon\}\cup 0\cdot\mathtt{supp}(t)
and
\mathtt{supp}(t\,u)=\{\varepsilon\}\cup 1\cdot\mathtt{supp}(t)\cup 2\cdot\mathtt{supp}(u)
by
coinduction
. The notations
t(b)
and
t|_{b}
are defined by
induction
on
b
.

Let
t\in\Lambda^{\infty}
. An
infinite branch
of
t
is an infinite word
\gamma
over
\{0,1,2\}
such all the finite prefixes of
\gamma
are in
\mathtt{supp}(t)
. The notion of
applicative depth

\mathtt{ad}(\_)
is straightforwardly extended to infinite branches:

Example 1
.

•

Let us formally define
f^{\infty}
, the Böhm tree of
\mathtt{Y}_{f}
,
by the equation
f^{\infty}=f(f^{\infty})
,
i.e.
,
f^{\infty}:=f(f(f(f(\ldots))))
is
the labelled tree such that
\mathtt{supp}(f^{\infty})=\{2^{n}~|~n\in\mathbb{N}\}\cup\{2^{n}\cdot 1\,|\,n\in\mathbb{N}\}
,
f^{\infty}(2^{n})=\char 64\relax
and
f^{\infty}(2^{n}\cdot 1)=f
for all
{n\in\mathbb{N}}
.
Then,
f^{\infty}
has one infinite branch
2^{\infty}
, which is the infinite repetition of
2
: indeed, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
2^{n}\in\mathtt{supp}(f^{\infty})
. We have
\mathtt{ad}(2^{\infty})=+\infty
.

•

Likewise, the infinite term
{}^{\infty}x\in\Lambda^{\infty}
defined by
{}^{\infty}x=({}^{\infty}x)x
, so that
{}^{\infty}x=(((\ldots)x)x)x
satisfies
\mathtt{supp}({}^{\infty}x)=\{1^{n}\,|\,{n\in\mathbb{N}}\}\cup\{1^{n}\cdot 2\,|\,{n\in\mathbb{N}}\}
, so
\mathtt{supp}({}^{\infty}x)
has the infinite branch
1^{\infty}
(this indicates a leftward infinite branch), which satisfies
\mathtt{ad}(1^{\infty})=0
since 2 does not occur in
1^{\infty}
.

If
b\in\mathtt{supp}(t)
, the subterm (resp. the constructor) of
t
at position
b
is denoted
t|_{b}
(resp.
t(b)
),
e.g.
, if
t:=\lambda x.(x\,y)z
and
b=0\cdot 1
(resp.
b=0\cdot 4
), then
t|_{b}=x\,y
and
t(b)=\char 64\relax
) (resp.
t|_{b}=t(b)=z
).

Definition 1

(001-Terms)
.

Let
t\in\Lambda^{\infty}
. Then
t
is a
001-term
, if, for all infinite branches
\gamma
in
\mathtt{supp}(t)
,
\mathtt{ad}(\gamma)=\infty
. The set of 001-terms is denoted
\Lambda^{001}
.

Once again, the vocable “001-term” comes from
[
KKSdV97
]
. For instance,
f^{\infty}
is a 001-term since its unique infinite branch
2^{\infty}
satisfies
\mathtt{ad}(2^{\infty})=+\infty
, whereas
{}^{\infty}x
defined above is
not
a 001-term since its infinite branch
1^{\infty}
satisfies
\mathtt{ad}(1^{\infty})=0
.

3.3.
The computation of Böhm trees

Let
t,u\in\Lambda^{\infty}
. The notation
t[u/x]
denotes the term obtained from
t
by the
capture-free
substitution of the occurrences of
x
with
u
(a formal definition can be found in
[
KKSdV97
]
in the infinitary setting). One easily checks that if
t,u
are 001-terms, then
t[u/x]
also is a 001-term.
The
\beta
-reduction
\rightarrow_{\!\beta}
is obtained by the contextual closure of
(\lambda x.t)u\rightarrow_{\!\beta}t[u/x]
and
t\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}t^{\prime}
denotes the reduction of a redex at position
b
in
t
,
e.g.
,
\lambda y.((\lambda x.x)u)v\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 0\cdot 1}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}\lambda y.u\,v
. Thus, the relation
t\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}t^{\prime}
is defined by
induction
on
b\in\{0,\,1,\,2\}^{*}
.

\begin{array}[]{c@{\sep}c@{\sep}c@{\sep}c@{\sep}c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 34.3034pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\phantom{t}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=72.21793pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle(\lambda x.r)s\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varepsilon}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}r[s/x]$}}}}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 19.1736pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty t\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}t^{\prime}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=56.89166pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\lambda x.t\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 0\cdot b}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}\lambda x.t^{\prime}$}}}}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 12.94444pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty t_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}t_{1}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=50.60005pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle t_{1}\,t_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1\cdot b}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}t^{\prime}_{1}\,t_{2}$}}}}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty t_{1}\,t_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2\cdot b}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}t_{1}\,t_{2}^{\prime}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=50.60004pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 12.94444pt\hbox{$\displaystyle t_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}t^{\prime}_{2}$}}}}\hfil\hskip 14.22636pt&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 1.56439pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty t\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}t^{\prime}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=21.67325pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle t\rightarrow_{\!\beta}t^{\prime}$}}}}\end{array}

The notion of normal form generalizes to
\Lambda^{001}
,
e.g.
, a
001-Normal Form (001-NF)
is a 001-term that does not contain a redex. Thus, the set of 001-NF, denoted
\text{NF}_{\infty}
, can be defined
coinductively
by:

t,\,t_{i}~::=~\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{p}.x\,t_{1}\ldots t_{q}~(p,q\geqslant 0)

A term
t\in\Lambda^{001}
is
head normalizing
if
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{h}}^{*}\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{p}.x\,t_{1}\ldots t_{q}
(with
t_{1},\ldots,t_{q}\in\Lambda^{001}
), which is a head normal form. A term
t\in\Lambda^{001}
is
has a head normal form
if there is a
finite
reduction path from
t
to a head normal form
t^{\prime}
(
i.e.
,
t\rightarrow_{\!\beta}t^{\prime}
). In particular, the implication “
t
is head normalizing”
\Rightarrow
“
t
has a head normal form” straightforwardly holds. The converse implication (“
t
has a head normal form
\Rightarrow

t
is head normalizing”) also holds, although the proof differs from the finite cas
[
KKSdV97
]
, and we will give a simple semantic proof of this fact in Sec.
3.5
.

Definition 2

(Böhm tree of a term)
.

Let
t
be a 001-term.

The Böhm tree
\mathtt{BT}(t)
of
t
is
coinductively
defined by:

•

\mathtt{BT}(t)=\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{p}.x\,\mathtt{BT}(t_{1})\ldots\mathtt{BT}(t_{q})
if
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{h}}^{*}\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{p}.x\,t_{1}\ldots t_{q}
.

•

\mathtt{BT}(t)=\bot
if
t
is not head normalizing.

For instance,
\mathtt{BT}(\Omega)=\bot
where
\Omega=(\lambda x.x\,x)(\lambda x.x\,x)
, if
t
a 001-normal form then
\mathtt{BT}(t)=t
and
\mathtt{BT}(\mathtt{Y}_{f})=f^{\infty}
.
Intuitively, the computation of Böhm trees is done by a possibly infinite series of head reductions at deeper and deeper levels.
This corresponds to the asymptotic reduction strategy known as
hereditary head reduction
.

More formally, we define
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{hh}}
, a non-deterministic version of hereditary head reduction as follows: if
t
is a reducible 001-term, we define
\mathtt{adr}(t)
, the applicative depth of reduction of
t
as the minimal applicative depth of a redex inside
t
. There may be several such redexes,
e.g.
, if
t:=x\,(\mathtt{I}\,\mathtt{I})\Omega
, then
\mathtt{adr}(t)=1
and both
(\mathtt{I}\,\mathtt{I})
and
\Omega
are redex occuring at applicative depth 1.

We then set
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{hh}}t^{\prime}
whenever
t\rightarrow_{\!\beta}t^{\prime}
and the contracted redex occurs at applicative depth
\mathtt{adr}(t)
, thus, with the same example, we have both
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{hh}}x\,\mathtt{I}\,\Omega
and
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{hh}}t
. We can also define a (deterministic) parallel version of this reduction, by specifying that, when
t
is reducible, all redexes occuring at applicative depth
\mathtt{adr}(t)
are simulateneously contracted:
x\,(\mathtt{I}\,\mathtt{I})\,(\mathtt{I}\,\mathtt{I})\,(x\,(\mathtt{I}\,x))
would give
x\,\mathtt{I}\,\mathtt{I}\,(x(\mathtt{I}\,x))
.

Observation 3
.

Let
t
be a 001-term. Then
t
is hereditary head normalizing iff, for all
d\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a term
t^{\prime}
such that
\mathtt{adr}(t^{\prime})\geqslant d
and
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{hh}}^{*}t^{\prime}
.

Infinitary convergence.

When
t
is hereditarily head normalizing (
\mathtt{BT}(t)
does not contain
\bot
), hereditary head reduction is productive in the sense that, whenever we wait sufficiently many evaluation steps, the term will not change under any fixed applicative depth (Observation
3
). This is actually why hereditary head reduction computes something (in this case, the Böhm tree of
t
). More generally,
some reduction paths of an unspecified form have an infinite length but asymptotically produce a term:

Definition 4

(Productive reduction paths)
.

Let
t=t_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b_{0}}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}t_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b_{1}}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}t_{2}\ldots t_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b_{n}}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}t_{n+1}\ldots
be a reduction path of length
\ell\leqslant\infty
.

Then, this reduction path is said to be
productive
if either it is of finite length (
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
), or
\ell=\infty
and
\mathtt{ad}(b_{n})
tends to infinity (remember that
\mathtt{ad}(\cdot)
is applicative depth).

A productive reduction path is called a
strongly converging reduction sequence
in
[
KKSdV97
]
, in which numerous examples are found. When
\mathtt{BT}(t)
does not contain
\bot
, the hereditary head reduction strategy on a term
t
gives a particular case of productive path.

Lemma 5

(Limit of a productive path)
.

Let
t=t_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b_{0}}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}t_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b_{1}}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}t_{2}\ldots t_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b_{n}}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}t_{n+1}\ldots
be a productive reduction path of infinite length.

Then, there is a unique 001-term
t^{\prime}
such that, for every
d\geqslant 0
, there is
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that, for all
n\geqslant N
,
\mathtt{supp}(t_{n})\cap\{b\in\{0,1,2\}^{*}\,|\,\mathtt{ad}(b)\leqslant d\}=\mathtt{supp}(t^{\prime})\cap\{b\in\{0,1,2\}^{*}\,|\,\mathtt{ad}(b)\leqslant d\}
and
t_{n}(b)=t^{\prime}(b)
on
\mathtt{supp}(t^{\prime})\cap\{b\in\{0,1,2\}^{*}\,|\,\mathtt{ad}(b)\leqslant d\}
.

The existence and the unicity of the term
t^{\prime}
in the statement of Lemma
5
is easy to prove, and
t^{\prime}
is called the
limit
of the productive path. Intuitively, the lemma specifies that, when
t^{\prime}
is the limit of
(t_{n})_{n\geqslant 0}
, then
t^{\prime}
induces the same tree as
t_{n}
at fixed applicative depth after sufficiently many reduction steps, as expected.

Notation 1
.

We write
t{\rightarrow_{\!\beta}^{\infty}}t^{\prime}
if
t\rightarrow_{\!\beta}^{*}t^{\prime}
or
t^{\prime}
is the limit of a productive path starting at
t
.

For instance,
if
\Delta_{f}=\lambda x.f(x\,x)
,
\mathtt{Y}_{f}=\Delta_{f}\,\Delta_{f}
(with
f\in\mathscr{V}
), then
\mathtt{Y}_{f}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varepsilon}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}f(\mathtt{Y}_{f})
, which gives the productive path
\mathtt{Y}_{f}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varepsilon}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}f(\mathtt{Y}_{f})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}\ldots f^{n}(\mathtt{Y}_{f})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2^{n}}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}f^{n+1}(\mathtt{Y}_{f})\ldots
since
\mathtt{ad}(2^{n})=n\longrightarrow\infty
.
The limit of this path, which implements hereditary head reduction on
\mathtt{Y}_{f}
, is
f^{\infty}
,
i.e.
, we have
\mathtt{Y}_{f}{\rightarrow_{\!\beta}^{\infty}}f^{\infty}
. This formalizes observations on the first page on this article. More generally, if
t
is hereditary head normalizing, by Observation
3
, hereditary head reduction on
t
defines a productive reduction path whose limit is the normal form of
t
, whether this path is finite or not. As a counter-example,
t:=x\,\mathtt{Y}_{f}\,\Omega
is not hereditary head normalizing and
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{hh}}
does not stop on
t
, since
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{hh}}x\,(f\,\mathtt{Y}_{f})\Omega\rightarrow_{\mathtt{hh}}x\,(f\,\mathtt{Y}_{f})\Omega
. However, the computation of
\mathtt{Y}_{f}
in
t
gives a productive reduction path from
t
to
x\,f^{\infty}\,\Omega
.

Notice that
\Omega\rightarrow_{\!\beta}\Omega\rightarrow_{\!\beta}\ldots
is not a productive path (although the term does not change) since all reduction steps occur at null applicative depth.

By Observation
3
, a term
t
is hereditarily head normalizing iff
\rightarrow_{\mathtt{hh}}
on
t
defines a productive reduction path (whose limit is necessarily a 001-normal form by construction, whether this path is of finite length or not). Let us now have look at the alternative notion of infinite normalization.

Definition 6
.

A 001-term
t
has
an infinitary normal form
if there is a 001-normal form
t^{\prime}
such that
t{\rightarrow_{\!\beta}^{\infty}}t^{\prime}
(for some productive path).

The reader may keep in mind that, when we say that
t
has an infinitary normal form
t^{\prime}
,
t^{\prime}
can be either finite or infinite.

It is known that
t
has an infinitary normal form iff
t
is hereditarily head normalizing,
i.e.
, iff the Böhm tree of
t
does not contain
\bot
. Notice that, if
\mathtt{BT}(t)
does not contain
\bot
(
e.g.
, when
t=\mathtt{Y}_{f}
), then it is obvious that
t
has an infinitary normal form by Definitions
2
and
4
, but the converse implication is not, especially in the infinite case
[
KKSdV97
]
. The statement is proved in
[
KKSdV97
]
in a syntactical way, but Theorem
4
shall give an alternative
semantic
proof of this fact. Notice that, contrary to the finite case, this does not hold for leftmost-outermost reduction strategy. If
t:=\mathtt{Y}_{f}\,\mathtt{Y}_{f}
, whose Böhm tree
t^{\prime}:=(f^{\infty})f^{\infty}
does not contain
\bot
, the leftmost-outermost strategy asymptotically computes
f^{\infty}\,\mathtt{Y}_{f}
and not
t^{\prime}
: hereditary head reduction can be interpreted as a well-balanced version of l.o. reduction.

Remark 1

(Variants of the infinitary
\lambda
-calculus)
.

The infinitary
\lambda
-calculus has actually seven main variants, which were introduced by Klop and its collaborators
[
KKSdV97
]
. Among those, only three behave well,
i.e.
, satisfy the weakened form of confluence. The infinitary calculus that is considered here is one of these 3 calculi, which is referred to as
\Lambda^{001}
. Its normal forms, as we shall recall in Sec.
3.3
, are the Böhm trees (without
\bot
). The two other well-behaved infinitary calculi are
\Lambda^{111}
and
\Lambda^{101}
and their normal forms respectively correspond to the Berarducci trees
[
BI93
,
Ber96
]
and the Lévy-Longo trees
[
L7́5
,
Lon83
]
.

3.4.
The Finitary Type System
\mathscr{R}_{0}
and Unforgetfulness

In this section, we present a well-known variant system of Gardner-de Carvalho’s using multisets to represent non-idempotent intersection. This system characterizes
head
normalization. We state its main properties (subject reduction and expansion) and then we explain how it also enables characterizing
weak
normalization, using
unforgetfulness
.

System
\mathscr{R}_{0}
features
non-idempotent
intersection types
[
Car07
,
Gar94
]
, given by the following
inductive
grammar:

\sigma,\,\tau\hskip 14.22636pt::=o\hskip 14.22636pt|\hskip 14.22636pt[\sigma_{i}]_{{i\in I}}\rightarrow\tau

where the constructor
[\,]
is used for
finite
multisets (
I
is finite), and the type variable
o
ranges over a countable set
\mathscr{O}
. We write

[\sigma]_{n}
to denote the multiset containing
\sigma
with
multiplicity
n
. The multiset
[\sigma_{i}]_{{i\in I}}
is meant to be the non-idempotent
intersection of the types
\sigma_{i}
, taking into account their

multiplicity
, and intersection is
strict
,
i.e.
, does not occur on codomain of arrows.

In system
\mathscr{R}_{0}
, a
judgment
is a triple
\Gamma\vdash t:\tau
, where
\Gamma
is a context,
i.e.
, a
total
function from the set
\mathscr{V}
of term variables to the set of multiset types
[\sigma_{i}]_{{i\in I}}
,
t
is a (finite or infinite) 001-term and
\tau
is a type. The context
x:[\sigma_{i}]_{{i\in I}}
is the context
\Gamma
such that
\Gamma(x)=[\sigma_{i}]_{{i\in I}}
and
\Gamma(y)=[\,]
for all
y\neq x
.
The multiset union +, satisfying
e.g.
,
[\tau,\sigma]+[\sigma]=[\tau,\sigma,\sigma]
, is extended point-wise on contexts,
e.g.
,
\Gamma+\Delta
maps
x
on
\Gamma(x)+\Delta(x)
. We set
\mathtt{dom}(\Gamma)=\{x\in\mathscr{V}\,|\,\Gamma(x)\neq[\,]\}
, the
domain
of the context
\Gamma
. When
\mathtt{dom}(\Gamma)\cap\mathtt{dom}(\Delta)=\emptyset
,
we may write
\Gamma;\Delta
instead of
\Gamma+\Delta
.
The set of
\mathscr{R}_{0}
-derivations is defined
inductively
by the rules in Fig.
6
:

\begin{array}[t]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 25.64218pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\phantom{.}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=54.06215pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\mathtt{ax}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x:[\tau]\vdash x:\tau$}}}}\hskip 14.22636pt\hskip 14.22636pt{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 4.02779pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma;x:[\sigma_{i}]_{{i\in I}}\vdash t:\tau$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=85.40854pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\mathtt{abs}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\lambda x.t:[\sigma_{i}]_{{i\in I}}\rightarrow\tau$}}}}\\[17.07182pt]
{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash t:[\sigma_{i}]_{{i\in I}}\rightarrow\tau\hskip 14.22636pt(\Delta_{i}\vdash u:\sigma_{i})_{{i\in I}}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=147.87874pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\mathtt{app}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 37.2819pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma+_{{i\in I}}\Delta_{i}\vdash t\,u:\tau$}}}}\end{array}

Figure 6.
System
\mathscr{R}_{0}

We write
\Pi\rhd\Gamma\vdash t:\tau
to mean that the
finite
derivation
\Pi
concludes with the judgment
\Gamma\vdash t:\tau
and
\rhd\Gamma\vdash t:\tau
to mean that
\Gamma\vdash t:\tau
is derivable.

No weakening is allowed (
relevance
). In particular, the
\mathtt{ax}
-rule concludes with

x:[\sigma]\vdash x:\sigma
and not with
\Gamma,x:[\sigma_{i}]_{{i\in I}}\vdash x:\sigma_{i_{0}}

(for some
i_{0}\in I
).
Since contexts are total, if
\Gamma\vdash t:\tau
and
x\notin\mathtt{dom}(\Gamma)
, then
\lambda x.t
is always typable: rule
\mathtt{abs}
gives
\Gamma\vdash\lambda x.t:[\,]\rightarrow\tau
.
Relevance ensures that

\lambda x.x
(resp.
\lambda x.y
) can be typed with
[\tau]\rightarrow\tau
(resp.
[\,]\rightarrow\tau
), but
not
with
[\tau,\sigma]\rightarrow\tau
(resp.
[\tau]\rightarrow\tau
).

\mathtt{ax}

\displaystyle x:[\tau]\vdash x:\tau

\mathtt{abs}

\displaystyle\vdash\lambda x.x:[\tau]\rightarrow\tau

\mathtt{ax}

\displaystyle y:[\tau]\vdash y:\tau

\mathtt{abs}

\displaystyle x:[\tau]\vdash\lambda x.y:[\,]\rightarrow\tau

A straightforward induction shows that if
\Gamma\vdash t:\tau
is derivable, then
\mathtt{dom}(\Gamma)
is finite.
The definition below allows formulating quantitative properties of the system.

Remark 2

(Relevance and redundancy of context)
.

Contexts are actually superfluous in system
\mathscr{R}_{0}
, since, by induction on
\Pi\rhd\Gamma\vdash t:\tau
, for all
x\in\mathscr{V}
,
\Gamma(x)
is the multiset of the types assigned to
x
in axiom rules. All the cases are straightforward. In a non-relevant system, this does not hold, since
x
may have been assigned types
via
the weakening rule. However, we prefer to keep contexts explicit in system
\mathscr{R}_{0}
although they are superfluous, since they make the system easier to formulate.

Definition 7
.

Let
\Pi
be a
\mathscr{R}_{0}
-derivation. Then the
size
of
\Pi
, denoted
\mathtt{sz}(\Pi)
, is the number of judgments in
\mathscr{R}_{0}
.

Observe that system
\mathscr{R}_{0}
is essentially finite (a derivation contains a finite number of judgments, judgments contains finite number of types, types are finite trees), although it may type infinite terms, which can appear as
untyped
arguments of applications,
e.g.
,
f^{\infty}
(
:=f(f^{\infty})
) in:

\Pi^{\prime}_{1}={\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 44.1525pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\phantom{.}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=91.08278pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\mathtt{ax}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle f:[[\,]\rightarrow o]\vdash f:[\,]\rightarrow o$}}}}\enskip\penalty$}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=111.58269pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\mathtt{app}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 16.2915pt\hbox{$\displaystyle f:[[\,]\rightarrow o]\vdash f^{\infty}:o$}}}}

The derivation
\Pi^{\prime}_{1}
is finite: it contains only two judgments, and finite types and contexts.

System
\mathscr{R}_{0}
enjoys both
subject reduction
and
expansion
, meaning that types are invariant under (anti-)reduction (if
t\rightarrow t^{\prime}
, then
\rhd\Gamma\vdash t:\tau
iff
\rhd\Gamma\vdash t^{\prime}:\tau
). Notice the decrement of size in subject reduction statement.

Proposition 8

(Weighted Subject Reduction and Expansion in system
\mathscr{R}_{0}
)
.

Assume
t\rightarrow_{\!\beta}t^{\prime}
.

(1)

If
\Gamma\vdash t:\tau
is
\mathscr{R}_{0}
-derivable, so is
\Gamma\vdash t^{\prime}:\tau
.
Moreover, if
\Pi\rhd\Gamma\vdash t:\tau
, then there is
\Pi^{\prime}\rhd\Gamma\vdash t^{\prime}:\tau
such that

\mathtt{sz}(\Pi^{\prime})\leqslant\mathtt{sz}(\Pi)
, with actually
\mathtt{sz}(\Pi^{\prime})<\mathtt{sz}(\Pi)
when
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{h}}t^{\prime}
.

(2)

If
\Gamma\vdash t^{\prime}:\tau
is
\mathscr{R}_{0}
-derivable, so is
\Gamma\vdash t:\tau
.

Proof.

Both points are proved by
induction
on the position of the reduction, using the following Substitution and Anti-Substitution Lemmas in the base case.
∎

Lemma 9

(Substitution and Anti-Substitution in
\mathscr{R}_{0}
)
.

Let
r
and
s
be two 001-terms.

•

If
\Pi\rhd\Gamma;x:[\sigma_{i}]_{{i\in I}}\vdash r:\tau
and,
\forall{i\in I}
,
\Phi_{i}\rhd\Delta_{i}\vdash s:\sigma_{i}
, then there is a derivation
\Pi^{\prime}\rhd\Gamma+(+_{i\in I}\Delta_{i})\vdash r[s/x]:\tau
such that
\mathtt{sz}(\Pi^{\prime})=\mathtt{sz}(\Pi)+(+_{i\in I}\mathtt{sz}(\Phi_{i}))-\#I
where
\#I
is the cardinality of
I
.

•

If
\Pi^{\prime}\rhd\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash r[s/x]:\tau
, then there are derivations
\Pi\rhd\Gamma;x:[\sigma_{i}]_{{i\in I}}\vdash r:\tau
and
\forall{i\in I}
,
\Phi_{i}\rhd\Delta_{i}\vdash s:\sigma_{i}
such that
\Gamma^{\prime}=\Gamma+(+_{i\in I}\Delta_{i})
.

Proof.

Substitution is proved by structural induction on
\Pi
. Anti-substitution is proved by induction on
\Pi^{\prime}
, with a case-analysis on the last constructor of
r
.
∎

The following result was proved in
[
Car07
]
for
\Lambda
. We have slightly adapted it for
\Lambda^{001}
:

Theorem 1

(de Carvalho)
.

A 001-term is HN iff it is typable in system
\mathscr{R}_{0}
.

Proof.

Assume that
\Pi\rhd\Gamma\vdash t:\tau
. Let
n=\mathtt{sz}(\Pi)
. Then weighted subject reduction entails that the head reduction strategy outputs a HNF in less than
n
steps. Conversely, head normal forms are easily
\mathscr{R}_{0}
-typable: to type
\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{p}.x\,t_{1}\ldots t_{q}
, we just need to assign
[\,]\rightarrow\ldots\rightarrow[\,]\rightarrow o
(with
q
arrows) to the head variable
x
, so that
t_{1},\ldots,t_{q}
are left untyped. We then use subject expansion to conclude that every HN term is typable.
∎

Characterizing finitary weak normalization.

A term
t\in\Lambda^{001}
is finitarily weakly normalizing when leftmost-outermost reduction terminates on
t
and outputs a
finite
normal form
t^{\prime}
(note that if
u
is infinite, then
(\lambda x.y)u
is infinite but reduces to the finite normal form
y
and is thus finitarily weakly normalizing in the sense that we consider here).

To understand how it is done, observe first that system
\mathscr{R}_{0}
characterizes head normalization (and not weak normalization) because the arguments of a head variable can be left untyped: for instance,
x
is assigned
[\,]\rightarrow\tau
, then
x\,t
is typable with type
\tau
for any term
t
– which is left untyped as a subterm of
x\,t
– even if
t
is not head normalizing.

\mathtt{ax}

\displaystyle x:[[\,]\rightarrow\tau]\vdash x:[\,]\rightarrow\tau

\mathtt{app}

\displaystyle x:[[\,]\rightarrow\tau]\vdash x\,t:\tau

As we explained in Sec.
2.2
(Partial typings), the possibility to have untyped arguments is crucial to characterize head normalization because this does not forbid,
e.g.
, the head normal form
x\,\Omega
to be typed.

On the other hand, in order to characterize weak normalization, we must guarantee somehow that every subterm is typed, except the subterms that can be erased in a reduction paths,
e.g.
, the subterm
u
in
(\lambda x.y)u
should not be typed, since
(\lambda x.y)u\rightarrow_{\!\beta}y
. To achieve this,
[\,]
should not occur at bad positions in a derivation
\Pi
. Actually, it is enough to only look at the judgment concluding

\Pi
. We recall that
[\,]
occurs negatively in
[\,]\rightarrow\tau
and that
[\,]
occurs positively (resp. negatively) in
[\sigma_{i}]_{{i\in I}}\rightarrow\tau
if
[\,]
occurs positively (resp. negatively) in
\tau
or negatively (resp. positively) in some
\sigma_{i}
,
i.e.
, the polarity of the occurrences of
[\,]
in a type is preserved in the codomain of arrows and inverted in the domain of arrows.

We say here that judgment
\Gamma\vdash t:\tau
is
unforgetful
when

[\,]
occurs neither negatively in any
\Gamma(x)
nor positively in
\tau
. A term
t
is unforgetfully typable if an unforgetful judgment
\Gamma\vdash t:\tau
is derivable. This is given by the following theorem
[
dC18
]
, which is the non-idempotent counterpart of a similar characterization with idempotent intersection based on unforgetfulness:

Theorem 2
.

A term
t
is finitarily weakly normalizing iff it is unforgetfully typable in system
\mathscr{R}_{0}

A straightforward induction shows that, when
\Pi\rhd\Gamma\vdash t:\tau
is unforgetful, (1) if
t
is reducible, then every redex of minimal applicative depth is typed, and (2) if
t
is a normal form,
then every subterm of
t
is typed in
\Pi
(thus, no part of the normal form of a term
t
is “forgotten” in an unforgetful derivation), which roughly justifies why the above theorem holds. In general, the unforgetfulness criterion gives a characterization of
weak
normalization in any type system which characterizes
head
normalization.

For now (in particular for Sec.
3.6
), it is enough to keep in mind that a
sufficient condition
of unforgetfulness is to be
[\,]
-free
:
t
is weakly normalizing as soon as
\rhd\Gamma\vdash t:\tau
, where
\Gamma
and
\tau
do not contain
[\,]
.
Throughout this article, we will frequently invoke system
\mathscr{R}_{0}
and this section to illustrate or motivate our choices.

3.5.
Infinitary subject reduction and expansion for finite derivations

Now, let us explain why subject reduction and expansion hold for infinite
productive
reduction path (and not just finite ones). The fact that if
\rhd\Gamma\vdash t:\tau
and
t{\rightarrow_{\!\beta}^{\infty}}t^{\prime}
then there is
\rhd\Gamma\vdash t^{\prime}:\tau
(infinitary subject reduction) is actually an easy consequence of the definition of productive paths (Definition
4
). In contrast, the fact that if
\Pi\rhd\Gamma\vdash t^{\prime}:\tau
and
t{\rightarrow_{\!\beta}^{\infty}}t^{\prime}
, then
\rhd\Gamma\vdash t:\tau
(infinitary subject expansion) relies on the finiteness of the derivations in
\mathscr{R}_{0}
and a technical observation that we call subject substitution and that will be a key tool in Sec.
3.6
and
7.4
to obtain full infinitary subject expansion. Let us prove the infinitary subject reduction property.

Proposition 10

(Infinitary suject reduction (system
\mathscr{R}_{0}
))
.

If
t{\rightarrow_{\!\beta}^{\infty}}t^{\prime}
and
\rhd_{\mathscr{R}_{0}}\Gamma\vdash t:\tau
, then
\rhd_{\mathscr{R}_{0}}\Gamma\vdash t^{\prime}:\tau
.

Proof.

Assume
\Pi\rhd_{\mathscr{R}_{0}}\Gamma\vdash t:\tau
and
\Pi=:\Pi_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b_{0}}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}\Pi_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b_{1}}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}\Pi_{2}\ldots
the derivations obtained by subject reduction along the given path
t{\rightarrow_{\!\beta}^{\infty}}t^{\prime}
.
For every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
N_{k}
be such that, for all

n\geqslant N_{k}
,
\mathtt{ad}(b_{n})>k+1
.

Let
\Pi^{\prime}
be the derivation typing
t^{\prime}
defined as follows:
under applicative depth
k
,
\Pi^{\prime}
is the same as
\Pi_{n}
for any
n\geqslant N_{k}
except that the judgments of
\Pi^{\prime}
type
t^{\prime}
and its subterms instead of
t_{n}
and its subterms.

This definition is licit, because, under applicative depth
\Pi_{n}
and
\Pi_{n^{\prime}}
do not differ whenever
n,n^{\prime}\geqslant N_{k}
, except for
untyped
parts of their subjects, which also proves that
\Pi^{\prime}
is correct.

∎

One notices that the proof of Proposition
10
does not use anywhere the finiteness of the derivations (we perform subject substitution but only at fixed depth
k
after a sufficient number of steps
N_{k}
). The statement and its proof shall still be valid in the infinitary system (Proposition
36
, which we prove more formally).

But as we explained in Sec.
2.3
, naively, subject expansion is not an asymptotic process. However, there is a way to by-pass the “infinite jump problem” by using the fact the derivations are finite: one may replace the limit
t^{\prime}
of a productive path with some
t_{n}
when
n
is big enough, without compromising the correctness of the derivation.

Actually, in a derivation
\Pi
whose subject is
t_{1}
, we may replace
t_{1}
with any term
t_{2}
which coincide with
t_{1}
on the typed parts inside
\Pi
. We name this phenonemon
subject substitution
.

Example 2
.

Consider:

\Pi_{1}={\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 45.2844pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=90.56879pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x:[[\,]\rightarrow o]\vdash x:[\,]\rightarrow o$}}}}\enskip\penalty$}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=100.56879pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 3.99066pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x:[[\,]\rightarrow o]\vdash x(y\,z):o$}}}}\hskip 14.22636pt\hskip 14.22636pt\hskip 14.22636pt\Pi_{2}={\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 45.2844pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=90.56879pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x:[[\,]\rightarrow o]\vdash x:[\,]\rightarrow o$}}}}\enskip\penalty$}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=100.56879pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 9.4444pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x:[[\,]\rightarrow o]\vdash x\,\Omega:o$}}}}

We transform
\Pi_{1}
into
\Pi_{2}
by
substituting
the subject
x(y\,z)
with
x\,\Omega
. This is licit because we replace
y\,z
, which is an untyped subterm of
x(y\,z)
in the derivation
\Pi_{1}
.

To formulate subject substitution, we need to define the set of positions of the typed subterms in a derivation
\Pi
:

Definition 11
.

Let
\Pi
a derivation typing a term
t
. We define the set
\hat{\Pi}\subset\mathtt{supp}(t)
of the
typed positions
in
\Pi
by the following induction on
\Pi
:

•

If
\Pi
ends with an axiom rule (
t=x
), then
\hat{\Pi}=\{\varepsilon\}=\mathtt{supp}(t)
.

•

If
\Pi
ends with an
\mathtt{abs}
-rule (
t=\lambda x.t_{0}
) and
\Pi_{0}
is its depth 1 subderivation, then
\hat{\Pi}=\{\varepsilon\}\cup 0\cdot\hat{\Pi}_{{}_{0}}
.

•

If
\Pi
ends with an
\mathtt{app}
-rule (
t=t_{1}\,t_{2}
),
\Pi_{1}
is its left premise (concluding with
t_{1}:[\sigma_{i}]_{{i\in I}}\rightarrow\tau
) and the
(\Pi_{i})_{{i\in I}}
(concluding respectively with
t_{2}:\sigma_{i}
) are its right premises, then

\hat{\Pi}=\{\varepsilon\}\cup 1\cdot\hat{\Pi}_{{}_{1}}\cup(\cup_{{i\in I}}2\cdot\hat{\Pi}_{{}_{i}})

In the
\mathtt{app}
-case, if
I=\emptyset
,
t_{2}
is not typed and
we only have
\hat{\Pi}=\{\varepsilon\}\cup 1\cdot\hat{\Pi}_{{}_{1}}
, as expected. In the typings of
x(y\,z)
and
x\,\Omega
in Example
2
, we have
\hat{\Pi}_{1}=\hat{\Pi}_{2}=\{\varepsilon,1\}
. The applicative depth of a typed position inside
\Pi
cannot exceed the size of
\Pi
(this can be proved by induction on
\Pi
):

Observation 12
.

Let
\Pi
be a
\mathscr{R}_{0}
-derivation. If
b\in\hat{\Pi}
, then
\mathtt{ad}(b)<\mathtt{sz}(\Pi)
.

Lemma 13

(Subject Substitution)
.

Let
t,u\in\Lambda^{\infty}
.
If
\Pi\rhd_{\mathscr{R}_{0}}\Gamma\vdash t:\tau
,
\hat{\Pi}\subseteq\mathtt{supp}(u)
and, for all
b\in\hat{\Pi}
,
u(b)=t(b)
, then there exists a
\mathscr{R}_{0}
-derivation
\Psi
such that
\Psi\rhd\Gamma\vdash u:\tau
and
\hat{\Psi}=\hat{\Pi}
.

Proof.

Straightforward by induction on
\Pi
.
∎

To obtain infinitary subject expansion in
\mathscr{R}_{0}
, the idea is to perform subject substitution in the considered infinite productive reduction path. Indeed, if
\Pi
types
t
and
t
and
u
differ only for positions
b\in\{0,1,2\}^{*}
such that
|b|>\mathtt{sz}(\Pi)
(where
|b|
is the length of
b
), then Lemma
13
can be applied on
\Pi
.

Contrary to infinitary subject reduction, the proof of infinitary subject expansion below crucially relies on the fact that
\mathscr{R}_{0}
-derivations are finite, in order to perform a subject substitution:

Proposition 14

(Infinitary suject expansion (system
\mathscr{R}_{0}
))
.

If
t{\rightarrow_{\!\beta}^{\infty}}t^{\prime}
and
\rhd_{\mathscr{R}_{0}}\Gamma\vdash t^{\prime}:B
, then
\rhd_{\mathscr{R}_{0}}\Gamma\vdash t:B
.

Proof.

When
t{\rightarrow_{\!\beta}^{\infty}}t^{\prime}
in a finite number of steps, this is an obvious consequence of Proposition
8
. Let us then assume that
t=t_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b_{0}}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}t_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b_{1}}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}t_{2}{\rightarrow_{\!\beta}^{\infty}}t^{\prime}
is an infinite path and that
\Pi^{\prime}\rhd_{\mathscr{R}_{0}}\Gamma\vdash t^{\prime}:\tau
. Let
k=\mathtt{sz}(\Pi^{\prime})
.
Let
N
such that, for all
n\geqslant N
,
\mathtt{ad}(b_{n})>k
(such a
n
exists, since the reduction path is productive). By definition of the limit (Lemma
5
), this implies that
\mathtt{supp}(t_{N})\cap\{b\in\{0,1,2\}^{*}\,|\,\mathtt{ad}(b)\leqslant k\}=\mathtt{supp}(t^{\prime})\cap\{b\in\{0,1,2\}^{*}\,|\,\mathtt{ad}(b)\leqslant k\}
and that, on this set,
t_{N}(b)=t^{\prime}(b)
. In particular, by Observation
12
,
t_{N}(b)=t^{\prime}(b)
is well-defined and holds on
\hat{\Pi}^{\prime}
.

By subject substitution (Lemma
13
) applied to
\Pi^{\prime}
and
t_{N}
, there is a derivation
\Pi_{N}\rhd\Gamma\vdash t_{N}:\tau
.

By Proposition
8
applied
N
times, there is
\Pi\rhd\Gamma\vdash t:\tau
such that
\mathtt{sz}(\Pi)\leqslant k
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 3
.

•

One may take a moment to consider the proof of Lemma
14
with that of Lemma
10
. As noticed in Sec.
2.3
, we need to overcome the “infinite jump problem”. Here, it is pivotal that, under some applicative depth
k
, the subject
t^{\prime}
of the derivation to expand is left untyped. This allows us to “jump” from
t^{\prime}
to some
t_{N}
for a sufficiently large
N
which is similar to
t^{\prime}
under applicative depth
k
. Note that no such need arises while proving Lemma
10
, since, for infinitary subject reduction, we define
\Pi^{\prime}
, the derivation typing the limit, incrementally.

•

The lemmas of this section all apply to
\Lambda^{\infty}
(Sec.
3.2
) and not only to
\Lambda^{001}
with minor changes. In particular, for any
t\in\Lambda^{\infty}
, there is a reduction path from
t
to a head normal form iff the head reduction terminates on
t
.
Notice that some
t\in\Lambda^{\infty}
are
headless
,
e.g.
,
{}^{\infty}x
from Example
1
, which has neither a head variable nor a head redex, because of its leftward infinite branch, but such a headless term is not typable in
\mathscr{R}_{0}
.

3.6.
Roadmap to solve Klop’s Problem

Since hereditary head normalization is a form of infinitary weak normalization, we want to adapt the proof of Proposition
2
which gives a type-theoretic characterization of
finitary
weak normalization. To achieve this, we need:

•

Typing (infinitary) normal forms
in unforgetful judgment (in this section,
f^{\infty}
).

•

Using a form of infinitary subject expansion
to obtain a derivation typing the expanded term (here,
\mathtt{Y}_{f}
)

The second point (infinitary subject expansion) is delicate, but we give intuitions about how it may be achieved just below. We will actually follow some of the ideas that were given in Sec.
2.3
.
This will allow us to present the key notions of
truncation
and
approximability
. Fig.
7
illustrates the main ideas of this section.

A complete trial on
\mathtt{Y}_{f}
.
The first point (typing 001-normal forms) is easier to grasp: notice that in a type system characterizing infinitary weak normalization, derivations should be able to type infinite normal forms
fully
,
i.e.
, without leaving subterms untyped. For instance, the following derivation
\Pi^{\prime}
, which fully types
f^{\infty}
, should be allowed (this derivation is also represented on top of Fig.
7
):

\Pi^{\prime}={\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 48.72198pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=97.44397pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\mathtt{ax}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle f:[[o]\rightarrow o]\vdash f:[o]\rightarrow o$}}}}\enskip\penalty\\
\hskip 14.22636pt\Pi^{\prime}\rhd f:[[o]\rightarrow o]_{\omega}\vdash f^{\infty}:o$}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=117.94388pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\mathtt{app}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 16.50179pt\hbox{$\displaystyle f:[[o]\rightarrow o]_{\omega}\vdash f^{\infty}:o$}}}}

\Pi^{\prime}

\mathscr{R}_{0}
that we call system
\mathscr{R}
(system
\mathscr{R}

). System
\mathscr{R}
allows infinite multisets (
e.g.
,

), proofs of infinite depth and also infinitary nestings,
e.g.
, the type
\sigma
defined by
\sigma=[\sigma]\rightarrow o
, so that
\sigma=[[\ldots]\rightarrow o]\rightarrow o
, is legal.

\Pi^{\prime}\rhd f:\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[[o]\rightarrow o]_{\omega}}\vdash f^{\infty}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}o}

\Pi^{\prime}
is an
infinite
derivation typing the
infinite
term
