Figure 7.
Truncation and Subject Substitution

Step 1 (truncations/approximations).

Notice that
\Pi^{\prime}
is of infinitary depth and
fully
types
f^{\infty}
(
i.e.
, no subterm of
f^{\infty}
is left untyped in
\Pi^{\prime}
). Actually,
\Pi^{\prime}
is
[\,]
-free
and in particular, it is unforgetful (recall the discussion surrounding Theorem
2
).

Thus,
\Pi^{\prime}
is the kind of derivation we want to expand, as to get a derivation
\Pi
typing
\mathtt{Y}_{f}
and witnessing that this term is infinitarily weakly normalizing. Since
\mathtt{Y}_{f}{\rightarrow_{\!\beta}^{\infty}}f^{\infty}
(infinite number of reduction steps), we are stuck in the “infinite jumb problem”: indeed, the pivotal argument in Proposition
14
allowing to “jump back” from the limit of a productive path to a term of this path is that the subject is partially typed, which allows performing subject substitution. Here, not such thing occurs, since no subterm of
f^{\infty}
is untyped in
\Pi^{\prime}
.

But notice that
\Pi^{\prime}
can be truncated into the derivation
\Pi^{\prime}_{n}
below, for any
n\geqslant 1
. To define these derivations, we have set
\Gamma_{n}=f:[[o]\rightarrow o]_{n-1}+[[\,]\rightarrow o]
and we write
x:\tau
instead of
x:[\tau]\vdash x:\tau
for
\mathtt{ax}
-rules:

\Pi^{\prime}_{n}={\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 20.05547pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=40.11093pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\mathtt{ax}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle f:[o]\rightarrow o$}}}}\enskip\penalty\hskip-28.45274pt\parbox[b]{113.81102pt}{$\begin{array}[b]{c}\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 20.05547pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=40.11093pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\mathtt{ax}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle f:[o]\rightarrow o$}}}}\enskip\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 28.27765pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=56.5553pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\mathtt{ax}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma_{1}\vdash f:[\,]\rightarrow o$}}}}\enskip\penalty$}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=77.0552pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\mathtt{app}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 16.2915pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma_{1}\vdash f^{\infty}:o$}}}}\enskip\penalty$}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=163.41591pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\mathtt{app}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 59.47185pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma_{2}\vdash f^{\infty}:o$}}}}\\
\vdots\\
\Gamma_{n-1}\vdash f^{\infty}:o\end{array}$}\phantom{aa}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=156.52988pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\mathtt{app}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 55.55025pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma_{n}\vdash f^{\infty}:o$}}}}

Derivations
\Pi^{\prime}_{3}
and
\Pi^{\prime}_{4}
are represented in the middle of Fig.
7
. By
truncation
or
approximation
, we mean, as suggested in Sec.
2.4
, that the finite derivation
\Pi^{\prime}_{n}
can be informally obtained from the infinite one
\Pi^{\prime}
by erasing some elements from the infinite multisets appearing in the derivation. We also informally write
\Pi^{\prime}_{1}\leqslant\Pi^{\prime}_{2}\leqslant\Pi^{\prime}_{3}\ldots
to mean that
\Pi^{\prime}_{n}
approximates
\Pi^{\prime}_{n+1}
. Thus,
\Pi^{\prime}_{n}\leqslant\Pi^{\prime}
for all
{n\in\mathbb{N}}
.

Conversely, we see that
\Pi^{\prime}
is the graphical
join
of the
\Pi^{\prime}_{n}
:
\Pi^{\prime}
is obtained by superposing suitably all the derivations
\Pi^{\prime}_{n}
on the same infinite sheet of paper.

Step 2 (expand the finite approximations).

•

Substituting
f^{\infty}
.
Observe now that, although we do not know yet how to expand
\Pi^{\prime}
, we can expand the
\Pi^{\prime}_{n}
, because the
\Pi^{\prime}_{n}
are
\mathscr{R}_{0}
-derivations (thus, they are
finite
): by subject expansion in system
\mathscr{R}_{0}
(Proposition
8
), for all
n\geqslant 1
, there is a
\mathscr{R}_{0}
-derivation
\Pi^{\prime}_{n}
concluding with
\Gamma_{n}\vdash\mathtt{Y}_{f}:o
. In the particular case of
\Pi^{\prime}
, we can detail the process: (1)
\Pi^{\prime}_{n}
leaves
f^{\infty}
untyped beyond depth
n
and (2) for any
k\geqslant n
,
f^{\infty}
and
f^{k}(\mathtt{Y}_{f})
are similar below depth
n
, so using subject substitution, for any
k\geqslant n
,
\Pi^{\prime}_{n}
yields a derivation
\Pi^{k}_{n}
typing
f^{k}(\mathtt{Y}_{f})
. We have represented
\Pi^{4}_{3}
and
\Pi^{4}_{4}
at the bottom of Fig.
7
.

•

Finite expansions.

Thus,
\Pi_{n}^{k}
types
f^{k}(\mathtt{Y}_{f})
, the rank
k
reduct of
\mathtt{Y}_{f}
, so we can expand it
k
times, obtaining a derivation
\Pi_{n}
. It can easily be observed that
\Pi_{n}
does not depend on
k
, because
\Pi^{k}_{n}
and
\Pi^{n}_{n}
have the same typed parts.

Step 3 (joining the finite expansions).

We can then define
\Pi
, the expected expansion of
\Pi^{\prime}
, as the join of all the derivations
\Pi_{n}
. This is justified by the following points:

(1)

for any
{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, we have
\Pi^{n}_{0}\leqslant\Pi^{n}_{1}\leqslant\ldots\leqslant\Pi^{n}_{n}
(see Fig.
7
, bottom)

(2)

proof reduction and expansion are monotonic in this
3
3

3

But proof reduction is not monotonic in all cases, simply because, in general, it is non-deterministic in system
\mathscr{R}_{0}
. See Sec.
5.4
case. Thus, since
\Pi_{0}
,
\Pi_{1}
,…,
\Pi_{n}
are respectively obtained after
n
expansion steps from
\Pi^{n}_{0}
,
\Pi^{n}_{1}
,…,
\Pi^{n}_{n}
, we have
\Pi_{0}\leqslant\Pi_{1}\leqslant\ldots\leqslant\Pi_{n}
. This intuitively explains why the
\Pi_{n}
(typing
\mathtt{Y}_{f}
) have an infinitary join
\Pi
, although the construction of this join is delicate for reasons to be presented in the next section.

Summary.

Thus, given an infinite derivation
\Pi^{\prime}
typing
t^{\prime}
and a productive reduction path
t=t_{0}\rightarrow_{\!\beta}t_{1}\rightarrow_{\!\beta}\ldots{\rightarrow_{\!\beta}^{\infty}}t^{\prime}
, the main ingredients to perform infinitary subject expansion are:

1.

Approximating
\Pi^{\prime}
into a family of finite derivations
\,\!{}^{f}\!\Pi^{\prime}
also typing
t^{\prime}
, so that
\Pi^{\prime}
is the (asymptotic) join of the
\,\!{}^{f}\!\Pi^{\prime}
.

2.a.

For each approximation
\,\!{}^{f}\!\Pi^{\prime}
, replacing
t^{\prime}
with
t_{n}
for a sufficiently large
n
(
n
depends on
\,\!{}^{f}\!\Pi^{\prime}
). This gives
\,\!{}^{f}\!\Pi^{n}
, which is also finite, but types the term
t_{n}
instead of
t^{\prime}
.

2.b.

For each
\,\!{}^{f}\!P^{\prime}
, expanding
\,\!{}^{f}\!\Pi^{n}

n
times. This gives
\,\!{}^{f}\!\Pi
, which type
t
.

3.

Obtaining
\Pi
, the expansion of
\Pi^{\prime}
, whose subject is
t
, by taking the join of the
finite

\,\!{}^{f}\!\Pi
while
\,\!{}^{f}\!P^{\prime}
ranges over the approximations of
\Pi^{\prime}
.

3.7.
Problems with infinitary typing and how to solve them

Thus, the ideas of truncation, subject substitution and join guides us about how to perform
\infty
-subject expansion. The particular form of
\Pi_{n}
and
\Pi
does not matter (but they
are given in Appendix
A
for the curious reader). Let us just say here that the
\Pi_{n}
involve a family of finite types
(\rho)_{n\geqslant 1}
inductively defined by
\rho_{1}=[\,]\rightarrow o
and
\rho_{n+1}=[\rho_{k}]_{1\leqslant k\leqslant n}\rightarrow o
and
\Pi
, their infinite join, involves an infinite type
\rho
satisfying
\rho=[\rho]_{\omega}\rightarrow o
: if
t
and
u
are typed with
\rho
, then
t\,u
may be typed with
o
.

Problem 1 (
\Omega
is typable).

Unfortunately, it is not difficult to see that the equality
\rho=[\rho]_{\omega}\rightarrow o
also allows the non-head normalizing term
\Omega=\Delta\Delta
to be typed.

\displaystyle\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 27.82968pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=55.65936pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\mathtt{ax}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x:[\rho]\vdash x:\rho$}}}}\enskip\penalty\hskip 14.22636pt\big(\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 27.82968pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=55.65936pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\mathtt{ax}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x:[\rho]\vdash x:\rho$}}}}\enskip\penalty\big)_{\omega}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=181.22147pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\mathtt{app}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 57.87154pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x:[\rho]_{\omega}\vdash x\,x:o$}}}}\enskip\penalty$}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=206.97133pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\mathtt{abs}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 86.73404pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\vdash\Delta:\rho$}}}}\enskip\penalty\hskip 14.22636pt\big(\vdash\Delta:\rho\big)_{\omega}

\mathtt{app}

\displaystyle\vdash\Omega:o

This last observation shows that the naive extension of the standard non-idempotent type system to infinite terms is unsound as non-head normalizing terms can be typed (actually, every term is typable in system
\mathscr{R}

[
Via18
]
: this means that every
\lambda
-term has a non-empty denotation in the infinitary relational model). Therefore, we need to discriminate between sound derivations (like
\Pi
typing
\mathtt{Y}_{f}
) and unsound ones. For that, we define an infinitary derivation
\Pi
to be
valid
or
approximable
when it is the join of all its
finite
approximations (Figure
4
). For instance,
\Pi^{\prime}
is approximable (it is the join of the
\Pi^{\prime}_{n}
) and
\Pi
, defined as the join of the directed family
(\Pi_{n})_{n\geqslant 1}
is approximable.

Problem 2 (validity is not definable with multisets).
It turns out that approximability cannot be formally defined in system
\mathscr{R}
,
i.e.
, with multisets as intersection types. We managed to define in the example of
\mathtt{Y}_{f}
and
f^{\infty}
only because we explained the concept informally and in those terms, two equal subterms are assigned the same typing. Intuitively, approximability cannot be defined in system
\mathscr{R}
because multisets disable tracking, as explained in Sec.
2.4
. Another (informal) argument will be given later in Sec.
5.4
: since proof reduction is not deterministic in system
\mathscr{R}
, the reduction dynamics of a
\mathscr{R}
-derivation
\Pi
may be very different from that of finite its approximations. We give a formal argument, to long to be presented in the core of this article, in Appendix
E.4
, once we have presented system
\mathtt{S}
and defined approximability in this system: we find two distinct
\mathtt{S}
-derivations
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
, which both collapse on the same
\mathscr{R}
-derivation
\Pi
, but such that
P_{1}
is approximable (in system
\mathtt{S}
) whereas
P_{2}
is not. This proves that approximability cannot be lifted from
\mathscr{R}
to
\mathtt{S}
.

4.
Tracking types in derivations

In this section, we define system
\mathtt{S}
, which is based on the key construct of

sequence
(presented in Sec.
2.4
) which we use to represent intersection in a
rigid
way,
i.e.
, which enables tracking.

•

We first coinductively define arrow types as function types whose domains are sequence of types. A notion of typing judgment naturally follows.

•

We then define derivations trees such that arguments of applications are typed with a sequence of judgments.

General notations

Some of these notations generalize or refine those of Sec.
3
.

•

A
track

k
is any natural number. When
k\geqslant 2
,
k
is called an
argument track
.
As for
\lambda
-terms, 0 is dedicated to the constructor
\lambda x
, 1 is dedicated to the left-hand side of applications. Not only 2 but all the
k\geqslant 2
to the possibly multiple typings of the arguments of applications. For instance, in system
\mathtt{S}
, while typing an application
t\,u
, a subderivation on track
9
(or on track 2, track 3 and so on) will be a subderivation typing the argument
u
.
This also explains why 0 and 1 will have a particular status in the definitions to come, and motivates the definition of
collapse
below.

•

The set of finite words on
\mathbb{N}
is denoted with
\mathbb{N}^{*}
,
\varepsilon
is the empty word,
a\cdot a^{\prime}
the concatenation of
a
and
a^{\prime}
. The prefix order
\leqslant
is defined on
\mathbb{N}^{*}
by
a\leqslant a^{\prime}
if there is
a_{0}
such that
a^{\prime}=a\cdot a_{0}
,
e.g.
,
2\cdot 3\leqslant 2\cdot 3\cdot 0\cdot 1
. When the denotation is clear, we may just write
2031
instead of
2\cdot 0\cdot 3\cdot 1
.

•

The
applicative depth

\mathtt{ad}(a)
generalizes to any
a\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
: it still represents the number of nestings inside arguments,

i.e.
,
\mathtt{ad}(a)
is defined inductively by
\mathtt{ad}(\varepsilon)=0
,
\mathtt{ad}(a\cdot k)=\mathtt{ad}(a)
if
k=0
or
k=1
and
\mathtt{ad}(a\cdot k)=\mathtt{ad}(a)+1
if
k\geqslant 2
.

•

The
collapse
is defined on
\mathbb{N}
by
\overline{k}=\min(k,2)
and on
\mathbb{N}^{*}
inductively by
\overline{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon
,
\overline{a\cdot k}=\overline{a}\cdot\overline{k}
,
e.g.
,
\overline{7}=2
,
\overline{1}=1
and
\overline{2\cdot 3\cdot 0\cdot 1}=2\cdot 2\cdot 0\cdot 1
. Intuitively, the collapse converts a position in system
\mathtt{S}
into a position in a
\lambda
-term (with only 0s, 1s or 2s) .

This extends to words of infinite length,
the collapse of
3^{\omega}
and
(1\cdot 2\cdot 3)^{\omega}
are
2^{\omega}
and
(1\cdot 2^{2})^{\omega}
.

•

A
sequence
of elements of a set
X
is a family
(k\cdot x_{k})_{{k\in K}}
with
K\subseteq\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0,1\}
and
x_{k}\in X
. In this case, if
k_{0}\in K
,
x_{k_{0}}
is the element of
(k\cdot x_{k})_{{k\in K}}

on track

k_{0}
.
We often write
(k\cdot x_{k})_{{k\in K}}
for
(x_{k})_{{k\in K}}
, which, for instance, allows us to denote by
(2\cdot a,4\cdot b,5\cdot a)
or
(4\cdot b,2\cdot a,5\cdot a)
the sequence
(x_{k})_{{k\in K}}
with
K=\{2,4,5\}
,
x_{2}=x_{5}=a
and
x_{4}=b
. In this sequence, the element on track 4 is
b
. If
S=(k\cdot x_{k})_{k\in K}
is a sequence,
we write
K=\mathtt{Rt}(S)
(
\mathtt{Rt}
stands for “
roots
”, which are the
top-level
tracks of
S
).
Sequences come along with an (infinitary)
disjoint union
operator, denoted
\uplus
: let
S_{j}:=(k\cdot x_{k,j})_{{k\in K}_{j}}
be sequences for all
{j\in J}
(where
J
is a possibly infinite set):

–

If the
(K_{j})_{{j\in J}}
are pairwise disjoint, then
\uplus_{j\in J}S_{j}
is the sequence
(k\cdot x_{k})_{{k\in K}}
where
K=\vee_{j\in J}K_{j}
and, for all
{k\in K}
,
x_{k}=x_{k,j}
where
j
is the unique index such that
k\in K_{j}
. In that case, we say that the
S_{j}
are
disjoint
.

–

If the
(K_{j})_{{j\in J}}
are not pairwise disjoint, then
\uplus_{j\in J}S_{j}
is not defined.

When
J
is finite, we use
\uplus
as an
infix
operator:
S_{1}\uplus S_{2}
etc.

The operator
\uplus
is partial, and infinitarily associative and commutative. For instance,
(2\cdot a,\,3\cdot b,\,8\cdot a)\uplus(4\cdot a,\,9\cdot c)=(4\cdot a,\,9\cdot c)\uplus(2\cdot a,\,3\cdot b,\,8\cdot a)=(2\cdot a,\,3\cdot b,\,4\cdot a,\,8\cdot a,\,9\cdot c)
, but
(2\cdot a,\,3\cdot b,\,8\cdot a)\uplus(3\cdot b,\,9\cdot c)
is not defined, because track 3 is in the roots of both sequences (
track conflict
).

•

A
tree

A
of
\mathbb{N}^{*}
is a non-empty subset of
\mathbb{N}^{*}
that is downward-closed for the prefix order (
a\leqslant a^{\prime}\in A
implies
a\in A
).
A
forest
is a set of the form
A\setminus\{\varepsilon\}
for some tree
A
such that
0,\,1\notin A
. Formally, a
labelled tree

T
(resp.
labelled forest

F
) is a function to a set
\Sigma
, whose domain, called its
support

\mathtt{supp}(T)
(resp.
\mathtt{supp}(F)
), is a tree (resp. a forest). If
U=T
or
U=F
, then
U|_{a}
is the function defined on
\{a_{0}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}\,|\,a\cdot a_{0}\in\mathtt{supp}(U)\}
and
U|_{a}(a_{0})=U(a\cdot a_{0})
. If
U
is a labelled tree (resp. forest and
a\neq\varepsilon
), then
U|_{a}
is a tree.

4.1.
Rigid Types

We start now to implement the ideas of Sec.
2.4
to overcome the problems concluding Sec.
3.7
: to enable tracking (so that we may define approximability), every argument derivation or type in the domain of an arrow must now receive a track for label.

Let
\mathscr{O}
be a countable set of types variables (metavariable
o
).
The sets of (rigid) types
\mathtt{Typ}^{111}
(metavariables
T
,
S_{i}
, …)
is
coinductively
defined in Fig.
8
.

\begin{array}[]{lll}T,\,S_{k}&::=&o\hskip 14.22636pt\|\hskip 14.22636pt(k\cdot S_{k})_{k\in K}\rightarrow T\\[2.84544pt]
\end{array}

Figure 8.
\mathtt{S}
-Types

A
sequence type
F=(k\cdot S_{k})_{k\in K}
is a sequence of types in the above meaning and is seen as an intersection of the types
S_{k}
.
We write
(\,)
for the
empty sequence
,
i.e.
, the sequence type whose support is empty. A sequence type of the form
(k\cdot T)
is called a
singleton sequence type
. Note that, in
(k\cdot S_{k})_{{k\in K}}
,
K
may be an infinite subset of
\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0,1\}
and that the definition allows infinitely many nestings of sequences in types.

Remark 4
.

The equality between two types (resp. sequence types) may be defined by mutual coinduction:

F\rightarrow T=F^{\prime}\rightarrow T^{\prime}
if
F=F^{\prime}
and
T=T^{\prime}
and
(T_{k})_{k\in K}=(T^{\prime}_{k})_{k\in K^{\prime}}
if
K=K^{\prime}
and for all
k\in K,~T_{k}=T_{k}^{\prime}
.

The
support of a type
(resp.
a sequence type
), which is a tree of
\mathbb{N}^{*}
(resp. a forest), is defined by mutual coinduction:
\mathtt{supp}(o)=\{\varepsilon\},~\mathtt{supp}(F\rightarrow T)=\{\varepsilon\}\cup\mathtt{supp}(F)\cup 1\cdot\mathtt{supp}(T)
and
\mathtt{supp}((T_{k})_{k\in K})=\cup_{k\in K}k\cdot\mathtt{supp}(T_{k})
.

Thus,
\mathtt{S}
-types may be seen as labelled trees.
For instance,

(7\mathord{\cdot}o_{1},3\mathord{\cdot}o_{2},2\mathord{\cdot}o_{1})\rightarrow o
is represented in Fig.
9

Thus, for types, track 1 is dedicated to the codomains of arrows.

\rightarrow
o
1

o_{1}
2

o_{2}
3

o_{1}
7

Figure 9.
The
\mathtt{S}
-type
(7\mathord{\cdot}o_{1},3\mathord{\cdot}o_{2},2\mathord{\cdot}o_{1})\rightarrow o
as a tree

A type of
\mathtt{Typ}^{111}
is in the set
\mathtt{Typ}^{001}
if its support does not hold an infinite branch ending by
1^{\omega}
. For instance, this excludes the
T
defined by
T=(\,)\rightarrow T
(
i.e.
,
T=(\,)\rightarrow(\,)\rightarrow\ldots
). This restriction means that we may only have finite series of arrows in a type. Indeed, 001-normal forms, even though they may be infinite, contain only finite series of abstraction nodes (
e.g.
,
\lambda x.\lambda x\ldots
is not legal).

We just write
\mathtt{Typ}
for
\mathtt{Typ}^{001}
and consider only sequence types which hold only types from
\mathtt{Typ}
.

When a family of sequence types
(F^{i})_{{i\in I}}
is disjoint, then there is no overlapping of typing information between the
F^{i}
, and
\uplus_{i\in I}F^{i}
is defined.

4.2.
Rigid Derivations

A
\mathtt{S}
-context

C
is a
total
function from
\mathscr{V}
to the set of (001-)sequence types, for instance,
x:(S_{k})_{{k\in K}}
is the context which assigns
(S_{k})_{{k\in K}}
to
x
and
(\,)
to every other variable.
The
domain
of
C
is
\mathtt{dom}(C)=\{x\in\mathscr{V}\,|\,C(x)\neq(\,)\}
. We define the join of contexts pointwise. If
\mathtt{dom}(C)\cap\mathtt{dom}(D)=\emptyset
, we may write
C;D
instead of
C\uplus D
.
A
judgment
is a triple of the form
C\vdash t:T
, where
C
is a context,
t
a 001-term and
T
is a (001-)type. A
sequence judgment
is a sequence of judgments
(C_{k}\vdash t:T_{k})_{k\in K}
(notice that these judgments have the same subject
t
). For instance, if
5\in K
, then the judgment on track 5 is
C_{5}\vdash t:S_{5}
.

The set of
\mathtt{S}
-derivations
(metavariable
P
) is defined

coinductively
by the rules of Fig.
10
.

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 35.98589pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\phantom{.}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=74.74956pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\mathtt{ax}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x:(k\cdot T)\vdash x:T$}}}}\hskip 14.22636pt\hskip 14.22636pt\hskip 14.22636pt{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 4.02779pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty C;x:(S_{k})_{{k\in K}}\vdash t:T$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=99.40186pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\mathtt{abs}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle C\vdash\lambda x.t:(S_{k})_{{k\in K}}\rightarrow T$}}}}\\[19.91684pt]
{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty C\vdash t:(S_{k})_{{k\in K}}\rightarrow T\hskip 14.22636pt(D_{k}\vdash u:S_{k})_{{k\in K}}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=170.98402pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\mathtt{app}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 33.33437pt\hbox{$\displaystyle C\uplus(\uplus_{{k\in K}}D_{k})\vdash t\,u:T$}}}}\end{array}

Figure 10.
System
\mathtt{S}

In the axiom rule,
k
is called an
axiom track
.
In the
\mathtt{app}
-rule, the contexts must be disjoint, so that no track conflict occurs. Otherwise,
\mathtt{app}
-rule cannot be applied.

Derivations from system
\mathtt{S}
may be seen as labelled trees: we define the
support
of
P\rhd C\vdash t:T
coinductively:
\mathtt{supp}(P)=\{\varepsilon\}
if
P
is an axiom rule,
\mathtt{supp}(P)=\{\varepsilon\}\cup 0\mathord{\cdot}\mathtt{supp}(P_{0})
if
t=\lambda x.t_{0}
and
P_{0}
is the subderivation typing
t_{0}
,
\mathtt{supp}(P)=\{\varepsilon\}\cup 1\mathord{\cdot}\mathtt{supp}(P_{1})\cup_{{k\in K}}k\mathord{\cdot}\mathtt{supp}(P_{k})
if
t=t_{1}\,t_{2},~P_{1}
is the left subderivation typing
t_{1}
and
P_{k}
the subderivation typing
t_{2}
on track
k
. The
P_{k}
(
k\in K
) are called
argument derivations
. Graphically, an
\mathtt{app}
-rule may be represented as in the top of Fig.
11
:

C\vdash t:(2\mathord{\cdot}S_{2},3\mathord{\cdot}S_{3},5\mathord{\cdot}S_{5})\rightarrow T
D_{2}\vdash u:S_{2}
D_{3}\vdash u:S_{3}
D_{5}\vdash u:S_{5}
C\uplus D_{2}\uplus D_{3}\uplus D_{5}\vdash t\,u:T
1
2
3
5
Application node as a labelled tree

\displaystyle\penalty C\vdash t:(2\mathord{\cdot}S_{2},3\mathord{\cdot}S_{3},5\mathord{\cdot}S_{5})\rightarrow T\hskip 14.22636pt\hskip 5.69046ptD_{2}\vdash u:S_{2}~\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[2]}\hskip 14.22636ptD_{3}\vdash u:S_{3}~\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[3]}\hskip 14.22636ptD_{5}\vdash u:S_{5}~\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[5]}

\displaystyle C\uplus D_{2}\uplus D_{3}\uplus D_{5}\vdash t\,u:T

Compact representation

Figure 11.
Representing
\mathtt{app}
-nodes in system
\mathtt{S}

In this figure, argument judgments are on tracks 2, 4 and 5 (indicated in red) and the left-hand side judgment is on track 1 (compare with Fig.
5
). The same
\mathtt{app}
-rule has an alternative representation at the bottom of Fig.
11
, where
argument
tracks are indicated between red square brackets inside the argument judgments, whereas track 1 is omitted.

Example 3
.

In order to gain space, we do not write right-hand sides of axioms. We set
S_{\mathtt{ex}}=(8\mathord{\cdot}o,3\mathord{\cdot}o^{\prime},2\mathord{\cdot}o)\rightarrow o^{\prime}
. We indicate the track of argument derivations between brackets
e.g.
,
x:\!(2\mathord{\cdot}o^{\prime})\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[3]}
means that judgment
x:\!(2\mathord{\cdot}o^{\prime})\vdash x\!:\!o^{\prime}
is on track 3.

P_{\mathtt{ex}}={\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 17.63678pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\phantom{.}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule x:\!\!(4\mathord{\cdot}S_{\mathtt{ex}})$}}}}\enskip\penalty\hskip 17.07182pt\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 13.6146pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\phantom{.}$}}\hskip 15.01738pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\phantom{.}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=32.81255pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\mathtt{ax}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x:\!(2\mathord{\cdot}o^{\prime})$}}}}\enskip\penalty\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[3]}\hskip 14.22636pt\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 13.6146pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\phantom{.}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule o^{\prime}$}}}}

In the
\mathtt{ax}
-rule concluding with
x:(5\mathord{\cdot}o)\vdash x:o
, the axiom track is 5.

In Example
3
above,
\mathtt{supp}(P_{\mathtt{ex}})=\{\varepsilon,0,0\mathord{\cdot}1,0\mathord{\cdot}2,0\mathord{\cdot}3,0\mathord{\cdot}8\}
and we have
P_{\mathtt{ex}}(0\mathord{\cdot}8)=x:(5\mathord{\cdot}o)\vdash x:o
. In particular, this judgment is on the
argument track 8
of the
\mathtt{app}
-rule at position 0.

Collapse.
When we forget about tracks, a sequence naturally collapses on a multiset
e.g.
,
(3\mathord{\cdot}a,5\mathord{\cdot}b,8\mathord{\cdot}a)
collapses on
[a,b,a]
. If this collapse is performed (coinductively)for all the sequences nested in derivations, then the derivations of
\mathtt{S}
will collapse on derivations of
\mathscr{R}
. For instance, if we set
\sigma_{\mathtt{ex}}=[o,o^{\prime},o]\rightarrow o^{\prime}
, the derivation
P_{\mathtt{ex}}
of Example
3
collapses on:

\Pi_{\mathtt{ex}}={\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 1.25pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 50.87747pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 12.41737pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\phantom{.}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=27.61253pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x:\sigma_{\mathtt{ex}}$}}}}\enskip\penalty\\
\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 10.83675pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\phantom{.}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=24.45128pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x:[o]$}}}}\enskip\penalty\\
\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 12.23953pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\phantom{.}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=27.25685pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x:[o^{\prime}]$}}}}\enskip\penalty\\
\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 10.83675pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\phantom{.}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=24.45128pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x:[o]$}}}}\enskip\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=139.36748pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x:[o^{\prime},[o,o^{\prime},o]\rightarrow o^{\prime},o,o]\vdash xx:o^{\prime}$}}}}\enskip\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=151.86748pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\vdash\lambda x.xx:[o^{\prime},[o,o^{\prime},o]\rightarrow o^{\prime},o,o]\rightarrow o^{\prime}$}}}}

Remark 5
.

The collapse turns out to be surjective: every
\mathtt{S}
-derivation is the collapse of a
\mathscr{R}
-derivation (it is non-trivial). Tracks can be interpreted as a very low-level specification for derivations, but as we said, they are necessary to express approximability and to prove the main interesting properties of system
\mathtt{S}
. However, while working with derivations and understanding some of the main intuitions of this article, it is still easier to just use multiset intersection and put tracks under the carpet.

Useful notations.

For the proofs of Sec.
7
, the following notations will be needed. The reader who does not want to read the proofs can skip them.

•

For applications:

assume that
P
types
t
,
a\in\mathtt{supp}(P)
and
a=a_{*}\mathord{\cdot}1^{n}
. Then
t|_{a_{*}}
is of the form
t|_{a}\,t_{1}\ldots t_{n}

–

The set
\mathtt{ArgTr}^{i}_{P}(a)
(for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
) contains the tracks of argument derivations typing the
i
-th argument
t_{i}
below
a
.

–

The set
\mathtt{ArgPos}^{i}_{P}(a)
contains the positions of those subderivations.

Formally, we set

1\leqslant i\leqslant n
,
\mathtt{ArgTr}^{i}_{P}(a)=\{k\geqslant 2\,|\,a_{*}\mathord{\cdot}1^{n-i}\mathord{\cdot}k\in\mathtt{supp}(P)\}
and
\mathtt{ArgPos}^{i}_{P}(a)=a_{*}\mathord{\cdot}1^{n-i}\mathord{\cdot}\mathtt{ArgTr}^{i}(a)=\{a_{*}\mathord{\cdot}1^{n-i}\mathord{\cdot}k\in\mathtt{supp}(P)\,|\,k\geqslant 2\}
. When
i
is omitted,
i=1
is assumed,
i.e.
,
\mathtt{ArgTr}_{P}(a)=\mathtt{ArgTr}^{1}_{P}(a)
.

•

For variables and binding:
If
a\in A:=\mathtt{supp}(P)
and
x\in\mathscr{V}
, we set
\mathtt{Ax}^{P}_{a}(x)=\{a_{0}\in A\,|\,a\leqslant a_{0},\,t(a)=x,\nexists a_{0}^{\prime},\,a\leqslant a_{0}^{\prime}\leqslant a_{0},\,t(a^{\prime}_{0})=\lambda x\}
. Thus,
\mathtt{Ax}^{P}_{a}(x)
is the set of positions of
\mathtt{ax}
-rules in
P
above
a
typing occurrences of
x
that are not bound at
a
.
If
a_{0}\in A
is an axiom, we write
\mathtt{tr}^{P}(a_{0})
for its associated axiom track.

•

We write
\mathtt{Ax}^{P}
for the set of all axiom positions in
\mathtt{supp}(P)
.

Usually,
P
is implicit and
we write only
\mathtt{ArgTr}^{i}(a)
,
\mathtt{ArgPos}^{i}(a)
,
\mathtt{Ax}_{a}(x)
and
\mathtt{tr}(a)
.

For instance, in
P_{\mathtt{ex}}
(Example
3
),
\mathtt{ArgTr}^{1}(0\mathord{\cdot}1)=\{2,3,8\}
and
\mathtt{ArgPos}^{1}(0\mathord{\cdot}1)=\{0\mathord{\cdot}2,0\mathord{\cdot}3,0\mathord{\cdot}8\}
(with
a_{0}=0
). Moreover,
\mathtt{Ax}_{0}(x)=\{01,02,03,08\}
,
\mathtt{Ax}_{\varepsilon}(x)=\emptyset
and
\mathtt{tr}(01)=4
,
\mathtt{tr}^{P}(08)=5
, whereas
\mathtt{tr}^{P}(0)
is not defined.

5.
Statics and Dynamics

In this section, we present
bipositions
, which allow pointing inside
\mathtt{S}
-derivation (Sec.
5.1
). We use this notion to present and prove the subject reduction and the subject expansion properties (Sec.
5.3
). Proof expansion is actually defined
uniformly
. We notice that proof reduction is deterministic in system
\mathtt{S}
. This leads us to define a notion of
residuation
and to formulate a first argument showing that approximability is indeed
not
definable while working with multiset intersection (Sec.
5.4
).

5.1.
Bipositions and Bisupport

In a rigid setting as system
\mathtt{S}
, we can identify and point to every part of a derivation, thus allowing to formulate many useful notions.

If
a\in\mathtt{supp}(P)
, then
a
points to a judgment inside
P
typing
t|_{\overline{a}}
. We write this judgment
\mathtt{C}(a)\vdash t|_{\overline{a}}:\mathtt{T}(a)
: we say
a
is an
outer position
of
P
(
\overline{a}
is defined on p.
•
‣
4
). The context
\mathtt{C}(a)
and the type
\mathtt{T}(a)
should be written
\mathtt{C}^{P}(a)
and
\mathtt{T}^{P}(a)
but we often omit
P
. From now on, we shall also write
t|_{a}
and
t(a)
instead of
t|_{\overline{a}}
and
t(\overline{a})
.

In Example
3
,
P_{\mathtt{ex}}(01)=x:(4\cdot S)\vdash x:S
, so
\mathtt{C}(01)=x:(4\cdot S)

i.e.
,
\mathtt{C}(01)(x)=(4\cdot S)
. Since
S=(8\cdot o,3\cdot o^{\prime},2\cdot o)\rightarrow o^{\prime}
, we have
\mathtt{C}(01)(x)(4)=\rightarrow
,
\mathtt{C}(01)(x)(43)=o^{\prime},\ \mathtt{T}(01)(\varepsilon)=\rightarrow
,
\mathtt{T}(01)(1)=o^{\prime}
. Likewise,
P_{\mathtt{ex}}(03)=x:(2\cdot o^{\prime})\vdash o^{\prime}
, so that
\mathtt{C}(03)=x:(2\cdot o^{\prime})
and
\mathtt{T}(03)=o^{\prime}
. Thus,
\mathtt{C}(03)(x)(2)=o^{\prime}
and
\mathtt{T}(03)(\varepsilon)=o^{\prime}
. We also have
\mathtt{C}(0)(x)=(2\cdot o^{\prime},4\cdot(8\cdot o,3\cdot o^{\prime},2\cdot o)\rightarrow o^{\prime},5\cdot o,9\cdot o)
, so that
\mathtt{C}(0)(x)(2)=o^{\prime}
and
\mathtt{C}(0)(x)(42)=o
.

This motivates the notion of
bipositions
: a biposition (metavariable
\mathtt{p}
) is a pointer into a type nested in a judgment of a derivation.

Definition 15

(Bisupport)
.

•

A pair
(a,c)
is a
right biposition
of
P
if
a\in\mathtt{supp}(P)
and
c\in\mathtt{supp}(\mathtt{T}^{P}(a))
.

•

A triple
(a,x,k\cdot c)
is a
left biposition
if
a\in\mathtt{supp}(P),\,x\in\mathscr{V}
and
k\cdot c\in\mathtt{supp}(\mathtt{C}^{P}(a)(x))
.

•

The
bisupport
of a derivation
P
, written
{\mathtt{bisupp}(P)}
, is the set of its (right or left) bipositions.

We consider a derivation as a
function
from its bisupport to the set
\mathscr{O}\cup\{\rightarrow\}
and write now
P(a,c)
for
\mathtt{T}^{P}(a)(c)
and
P(a,x,k\cdot c)
for
\mathtt{C}^{P}(a)(x)(k\cdot c)
. For instance,
(01,\varepsilon)
,
(01,1)
,
(03,\varepsilon)
are right bipositions in
P_{\mathtt{ex}}
and
P_{\mathtt{ex}}(01,\varepsilon)=\rightarrow
,
P_{\mathtt{ex}}(01,1)=o^{\prime}
,
P_{\mathtt{ex}}(03,\varepsilon)=o^{\prime}
. Moreover,
(01,x,4)
,
(01,x,43)
and
(0,x,42)
are left bipositions of
P_{\mathtt{ex}}
and
P_{\mathtt{ex}}(01,x,4)=\rightarrow
,
P_{\mathtt{ex}}(01,x,43)=o^{\prime}
and
P_{\mathtt{ex}}(0,x,42)=o
.

5.2.
Quantitativity and Coinduction

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}

\heartsuit
~{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle a\mathord{\cdot}10\mathord{\cdot}\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle\heartsuit}\rangle}

~{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle a\mathord{\cdot}10\mathord{\cdot}a_{2}\rangle}
\overline{x:(7\mathord{\cdot}S_{7})}
~{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle a\mathord{\cdot}10\mathord{\cdot}a_{7}\rangle}
\overline{x:(3\mathord{\cdot}S_{3})}
~{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle a\mathord{\cdot}10\mathord{\cdot}a_{3}\rangle}
P_{2}

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}
P_{3}

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}

Subderivation typing the redex
P_{r}
\heartsuit
~{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle a\mathord{\cdot}\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle\heartsuit}\rangle}
