\clubsuit
~{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle a\mathord{\cdot}a_{7}\mathord{\cdot}\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle\clubsuit}\rangle}
~{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle a\mathord{\cdot}a_{7}\rangle}
P_{3}
D_{3}\!\vdash s\!:\!S_{3}
~{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle a\mathord{\cdot}a_{3}\rangle}

Subderivation typing

the reduct

Figure 12.
Subject Reduction and Residuals

The presence of an infinite branch inside a derivation makes it possible that a type in a context is
not
“created” in an axiom rule. For instance, we set, for all
k\geqslant 2,~j_{k}=f:(i\cdot(2\cdot o)\rightarrow o)_{i\geqslant k},\,x:(8\cdot o^{\prime})\vdash f^{\infty}:o
and we coinductively define a family
(P_{k})_{k\geqslant 2}
of
\mathtt{S}
-derivations by

P_{k}={\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 65.37115pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\phantom{.}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=133.5201pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\mathtt{ax}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle f:k\cdot(2\cdot o)\rightarrow o\vdash f:(2\cdot o)\rightarrow o$}}}}\enskip\penalty\hskip 14.22636ptP_{k+1}\rhd j_{k+1}~\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[2]}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=234.16626pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\mathtt{app}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 32.25354pt\hbox{$\displaystyle f:(i\cdot(2\cdot o)\rightarrow o)_{i\geqslant k},\,x:(8\cdot o^{\prime})\vdash f^{\infty}:o$}}}}

We observe that the
P_{k}
are indeed correct derivations of
\mathtt{S}
.
Every type assignent to
f
can be traced back to an
\mathtt{ax}
-rule.
However, notice that
x
has been assigned a type (on track 8) although
x
does not appear in the typed term
f^{\infty}
and the part of the context assigned to
x
cannot be traced back to any axiom rule typing
x
with
o^{\prime}
(using axiom track
8
): we say that there is a
phantom axiom track
in the
P_{k}
. Thus, Remark
2
is not valid for the derivations
P_{k}
.
This motivates the notion of
quantitative
derivation, in which this does not happen:

Definition 16
.

A derivation
P
is
quantitative
when, for all
a\in\mathtt{supp}(P)
and
x\in\mathscr{V}
,
\mathtt{C}^{P}(a)(x)=\uplus_{a^{\prime}\in\mathtt{Ax}^{P}_{a}(x)}(\mathtt{tr}^{P}(a^{\prime})\cdot\mathtt{T}^{P}(a^{\prime}))
.

This definition specifies that
P
is quantitative when every context in
P
can be computed from the
\mathtt{ax}
-rules. Notice that
P
is always quantitative whenever the derivation is finite as observed in Remark
2
, or its subject is finite.

Now, assume
P
is quantitative. Then
\mathtt{Rt}(\mathtt{C}(a)(x))=\{\mathtt{tr}(a_{0})\,|\,a_{0}\in\mathtt{Ax}_{a}(x)\}
and for all
a\in A,~x\in\mathscr{V}
and
k\in\mathtt{Rt}(\mathtt{C}(a)(x))
, we write
\mathtt{pos}(a,\,x,\,k)
for the unique position
a^{\prime}\in\mathtt{Ax}_{a}(x)
such that
\mathtt{tr}(a^{\prime})=k
. The reader who does not want to read the proofs can forget about this notation.
Actually,

\mathtt{pos}(a,\,x,\,k)
can be defined by a downward induction on
a
as follows:

•

If
a\in\mathtt{Ax}^{P}
, then actually
a\in\mathtt{Ax}(x)
and
\mathtt{tr}(a)=k
and we set

\mathtt{pos}(a,\,x,\,k)=a
.

•

If
a\cdot 1\in A
, we set
\mathtt{pos}(a,\,x,\,k)=\mathtt{pos}(a\cdot\ell,\,x,\,k)
, where
\ell
is the unique positive integer such that
k\in\mathtt{Rt}(\mathtt{C}(a\cdot\ell)(x))
.

•

If
a\cdot 0\in A
, we set
\mathtt{pos}(a,\,x,\,k)=\mathtt{pos}(a\cdot 0,\,x,\,k)

5.3.
One Step Subject Reduction and Expansion

System
\mathtt{S}
enjoys both subject reduction and expansion: if
t\rightarrow^{*}t^{\prime}
, then
\rhd C\vdash t:T
iff
\rhd C\vdash t^{\prime}:T
.

Proposition 17

(One Step Subject Reduction)
.

Assume
t\rightarrow_{\beta}t^{\prime}
and
P\rhd C\vdash t:T
. Then there exists a derivation
P^{\prime}
such that
P^{\prime}\rhd C\vdash t^{\prime}:T
.

Proposition 18

(One Step Subject Expansion)
.

Assume
t\rightarrow_{\beta}t^{\prime}
and
P^{\prime}\rhd C\vdash t^{\prime}:T
.
Then there exists a derivation
P
such that
P\rhd C\vdash t:T
.

These proposition are valid in general, whether the derivations are quantitative or not. However, in the following, we shall consider only the quantitative case (this allows us not handling phantom axiom tracks). They may also be proven really simply by using coinduction. However, in the forthcoming sections, we shall need to deal with a validity criterion, whose soundness w.r.t. reduction and expansion (Lemma
6.3
) can only be dealt with by having a suitable notion of residuation. More precisely, we shall need to have a good understanding of the action of reduction on bipositions.
We thus give an alternative proof of the subject reduction property using
residuals

and defining directly the derivation reduct
P^{\prime}
in Sec.
5.5
. Details can be found in Appendix
C
. We also explain here why subject reduction may be seen as a
deterministic
process in system
\mathtt{S}
and why it is vital to ensure soundness (Sec.
5.4
). Subject expansion is
not
deterministic, but it may be processed
uniformly
.

All this is illustrated by Fig.
12
: we assume that
t|_{b}=(\lambda x.r)s
and
t\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\rightarrow}}t^{\prime}
,
P
is a quantitative derivation concluding with
C\!\vdash\!t\!:\!T
. We also assume that
a\in P
is such that
\overline{a}=b
(thus,
a
is the position of a judgment typing the redex to be fired) and that there are exactly 3
\mathtt{ax}
-rules typing
x
above
a
, using axiom tracks 2, 3 and 7. Notice that
\mathtt{ax}
-rule typing
x
on track 7 must be above
a\cdot 10
, so that its position is of the form
a\cdot 10\cdot a_{7}
. Likewise for the two other axioms.

Now, let us have a look at how reduction is performed inside
P
. We omit
\mathtt{ax}
-rules right-hand sides. We also indicate the position of a judgment between angle brackets,
e.g.
,
~{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle a\cdot 10\cdot a_{3}\rangle}
means that judgment
x:(3\cdot S_{3})\vdash x:S_{3}
is at position
a\cdot 10\cdot a_{3}
.

Notice how this transformation is
deterministic
: for instance, assume
7\in K
. There must be an axiom rule typing
x
using axiom track 7,
e.g.
,
x:\,(7\cdot S_{7})\vdash x:\,S_{7}
at position
a\cdot 10\cdot a_{7}
and also a subderivation at argument track 7, namely,
P_{7}
concluded by
s:S_{7}

at position
a\cdot 7
. Then, when we fire the redex at position
b
, the subderivation
P_{7}

must
replace the axiom rule on track 7, even if there may be other
k\neq 7
such that
S_{k}=S_{7}
, in contrast to system
\mathscr{R}
(see Sec.
5.4
).

Thus, Proposition
17
canonically gives only one derivation
P^{\prime}
typing
t^{\prime}
, so that we may also write
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\rightarrow}}P^{\prime}
.

Now, we observe that subject expansion cannot be deterministic in the same sense. When we pass from a derivation typing
r[s/x]
to a derivation typing
(\lambda x.r)s
, we create new axiom rules which type
x
.
Those axiom rules must be assigned axiom tracks.
But if for instance, 3 axiom rules are created above position
a
, there is no more reason to choose tracks 2, 3 and 7 than the tracks 8, 4, 38: the axiom tracks may be chosen arbitrarily, as long as they do not raise track conflicts. This explains the non-determinism of subject expansion.

However, as it will turn out in Sec.
7.4
, we will need to expand simultaneously
families
of derivations — and this, infinitely many times.
For that, we should find a way to perform subject expansion uniformly. Let then
\lfloor\cdot\rfloor
be any
injection
from
\mathbb{N}^{*}
to
\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0,1\}
.
We write
\mathtt{Exp}_{b}(P^{\prime},\lfloor\cdot\rfloor,t)
for the
unique
expansion of
P^{\prime}
such that
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\rightarrow}}P^{\prime}
and, for all
a_{0}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, if there is an
\mathtt{ax}
-rule typing
x
created at position
a_{0}
, then the axiom track that has been assigned is
\lfloor a_{0}\rfloor
.
Since
\lfloor\cdot\rfloor
is injective, no track conflict may occur. We give all the details in Appendix
C.3
. The term
t
must be indicated in the expression, because, for one
t^{\prime}
and one
b
, there may be several
t
such that
t\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\rightarrow}}t^{\prime}
.

Remark 6
.

If
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}P^{\prime}
then
P
is quantitative iff
P^{\prime}
is quantitative.

Remark
6
can be understood by looking at Fig.
12
: indeed, if
P
is not quantitative, there must be a phantom axiom track (Sec.
5.2
) on an infinite branch. But whether this phantom axiom track visits
r
,
s
or is outside
(\lambda x.r)s
, the contracted redex, it will be found in
P^{\prime}
.
Conversely, if
P^{\prime}
is not quantitative, there is a phantom axiom rule on an infinite branch, which visits an occurrence of
s
, or visits
r
but no substituted occurrence of
s
, or is outside
r[s/x]
. In all cases, this phantom axiom track will be found in
P
.

A First Look at Residuals.

Deterministic subject reduction naturally allows defining the residuals of positions and
right
bipositions after reduction, extending the notion of residuals for positions in
\lambda
-terms. We define them more precisely in Sec.
5.5
, to sketch the formal details of the proof of the subject reduction property.

In Fig.
12
,
\heartsuit
represents a judgment nested in
P_{r}
. Thus, its position must be of the form
a\cdot 10\cdot\alpha_{{\scriptscriptstyle\heartsuit}}
. After reduction, the
\mathtt{app}
-rule and
\mathtt{abs}
-rule at positions
a
and
a\cdot 0
have been destroyed and the position of this judgment
\heartsuit
will be
a\cdot\alpha_{{\scriptscriptstyle\heartsuit}}
. We set then
\mathtt{Res}_{b}(a\cdot 10\cdot\alpha_{{\scriptscriptstyle\heartsuit}})=a\cdot\alpha_{{\scriptscriptstyle\heartsuit}}
.

Likewise,
\clubsuit
represents a judgment nested in the argument derivation
P_{7}
on track 7 above
a

. Thus, its position must be of the form
a\cdot 7\cdot\alpha_{{\scriptscriptstyle\clubsuit}}
where
a\cdot 7
is the root of
P_{7}
. After reduction,
P_{7}
will replace the
\mathtt{ax}
-rule typing
x
on track 7, so its root will be at
a\cdot a_{7}
(by definition of
a_{7})
. Thus, after reduction, the position of judgment
\clubsuit
will be
a\cdot a_{7}\cdot\alpha_{{\scriptscriptstyle\clubsuit}}
. We set then
\mathtt{Res}_{b}(a\cdot 7\cdot\alpha_{{\scriptscriptstyle\clubsuit}})=a\cdot a_{7}\cdot\alpha_{{\scriptscriptstyle\clubsuit}}
.

We can thus define the
residuals
of most positions
\alpha\in\mathtt{supp}(P)
, but not all,
e.g.
,
a\cdot 1
, that corresponds to the abstraction of the redex, is destroyed during reduction and does not have a residual. For
right
bipositions, when
(a,c)\in{\mathtt{bisupp}(P)}
and
a^{\prime}=\mathtt{Res}_{b}(a)
is defined, we set
\mathtt{Res}_{b}(a,c)=(a^{\prime},c)
.

Note that defining residuals in system
\mathscr{R}
would be impossible: system
\mathscr{R}
lacks pointers and is not deterministic (Sec.
5.4
and Fig.
13
).

5.4.
Safe Truncations of Typing Derivations and Determinism of Reduction

Now that we have described the deterministic reduction dynamics of system
\mathtt{S}
, we can explain why the non-deterministic system
\mathscr{R}
, based on multiset intersection, is unfit to express the notion of
approximability
, informally introduced at the end of Sec.
3.6
.

\Pi_{r}
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\tau}
\mathtt{ax}
x:{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\sigma}
\mathtt{ax}
x:{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\sigma}
\lambda x

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[\sigma,\sigma]\rightarrow\tau}
\char 64\relax
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\tau}
\Pi^{1}_{s}
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\sigma}
\Pi^{2}_{s}
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\sigma}
reduces into
\Pi^{1,2}_{r[s/x]}
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\tau}
\Pi^{1}_{s}
s:{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\sigma}
s:{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\sigma}
\Pi^{2}_{s}
or
\Pi^{2,1}_{r[s/x]}
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\tau}
\Pi^{2}_{s}
s:{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\sigma}
s:{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\sigma}
\Pi^{1}_{s}

Figure 13.
Non-Determinism of Subject Reduction (Multiset Intersection)

Let us consider a redex
t=(\lambda x.r)s
and its reduct
t^{\prime}=r[s/x]
. If a
\mathscr{R}
-derivation
\Pi
types
t
, then
\Pi
has one subderivation
\Pi_{r}
of the form
\Pi_{r}\rhd\Gamma,\,x:[\sigma_{i}]_{{i\in I}}\vdash r:\tau
.

Notice that proof reduction is non-deterministic in system
\mathscr{R}_{0}
: let us consider a redex
t=(\lambda x.r)s
and its reduct
r[s/x]
. Assume that
t
is typed so that
s
is typed exactly
twice
with the same type. For instance, say that
\Pi
is a derivation typing
t
which has a subderivation
\Pi_{r}\rhd\Gamma,x:[\sigma,\sigma]\vdash r:\tau
(thus
\lambda x.r:[\sigma,\sigma]\rightarrow\tau
) and two subderivations
\Pi^{1}_{s}
and
\Pi^{2}_{s}
concluding with
\Delta_{i}\vdash s:\sigma
(one may assume
\Pi^{1}_{s}\neq\Pi^{2}_{s}
). This situation is represented in Fig.
13
and in particular, there are two different axiom rules typing
x
with
\sigma
in
r
. Then, to obtain a
derivation reduct
typing
t^{\prime}=r[s/x]
with
\tau
, there are two possibilities depending on which argument derivation
\Pi^{i}_{s}
replaces which axiom rule typing
x
.

This makes a sharp difference with system
\mathtt{S}
: assume that
S_{2}=S_{3}=S_{7}=S
so that argument derivation
P_{2},\,P_{3},\,P_{7}
type
s
with the same type
S
. Then each
P_{k}
(
k\in K
) will replace axiom rule at position
a\cdot 10\cdot a_{k}
(see Fig.
12
) without other choice. In system
\mathtt{S}
, there is a unique (canonical) derivation reduct.

In contrast, let us dig into the following independent situations in system
\mathscr{R}
:

•

Assume
\Pi^{1}_{s}
and
\Pi^{2}_{s}
(typing
s
), both concluding with the
same type
\sigma=\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{2}
, as in Fig.
13
. Thus, we also have two axiom leaves #1 and #2
concluded by
x:[\sigma]\vdash x:\sigma
. In that case, during reduction, the axiom rules #1 may be replaced with
\Pi^{1}_{s}
(in that case, axiom #2 is replace with
\Pi^{2}_{s}
) or with
\Pi^{2}_{s}
(in that case, axiom #2 is replaced with
\Pi^{1}_{s}
), because the types of axioms #1 and #1 match with those of the argument derivations
\Pi^{1}_{s}
and
\Pi^{2}_{s}
.
When we truncate
\Pi
into a
finite
\,\!{}^{f}\!\Pi
, the subderivations
\Pi^{1}_{s}
and
\Pi^{2}_{s}
are also
cut into two derivations
\,\!{}^{f}\!\Pi^{1}_{s}
and
\,\!{}^{f}\!\Pi^{2}_{s}
. In each

\,\!{}^{f}\!\Pi^{i}_{s}
,
\sigma
can be cut into a type
\,\!{}^{f}\!\sigma_{i}
. When

\Pi^{1}_{s}
and
\Pi^{2}_{s}
are different, it is possible that
\,\!{}^{f}\!\sigma_{2}\neq\,\!^{f}\!\sigma_{1}
for every finite truncation
of
\Pi
. Thus, it is possible that, for every truncation

\,\!{}^{f}\!\Pi
, the axiom leaf #1 does not match
\,\!{}^{f}\!\Pi_{2}
: indeed, a match between axiom rule and argument derivation that is possible in
\Pi
could be impossible
for any of its truncations.

•

Assume this time
\sigma_{1}\neq\sigma_{2}
. When we truncate

\Pi
into a finite
\,\!{}^{f}\!\Pi
, both
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
can
be truncated into the same finite type
\,\!{}^{f}\!\sigma
. In that case, we can associate
\,\!{}^{f}\!\Pi_{1}
with axiom #2 and
\,\!{}^{f}\!\Pi_{2}
with axiom #1
inside
\,\!{}^{f}\!\Pi
in
\,\!{}^{f}\!\Pi
(which is impossible in
\Pi
), thus producing a reduct derivation
\,\!{}^{f}\!\Pi^{\prime}
typing

t^{\prime}
, which has no meaning w.r.t.
\Pi
(
\,\!{}^{f}\!\Pi^{\prime}
would not be a truncation of any derivation reduct of
\Pi
).

That is why we need the
deterministic
association between the argument derivations and the axiom rules typing each in system
\mathtt{S}
(thanks to tracks),
so that the associations between them are preserved even when we truncate derivations. Ses not allow to formulate a well-fit notion of
approximability for derivations that would be stable under
(anti)reduction and hereditary for subterms. Whereas determinism ensures that proof reduction is monotonic in system
\mathtt{S}
, it is not monotonic in system
\mathscr{R}
.

Another, more formal argument proving that approximability cannot be defined in system
\mathscr{R}
is presented in Appendix
E.4
:
there exists a
\mathscr{R}
-derivation
\Pi
and two
\mathtt{S}
-derivations
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
that both collapse on
\Pi
such that
P_{1}
is approximable (Definition
22
to come) and
P_{2}
is not.

5.5.
Proving Subject Reduction with Residuals

In the beginning of Sec.
5.3
, we emphasized the necessity of describing the effect of reduction on bipositions.
In this section, we sketch a proof of the subject reduction property in the quantitative case using residuation. A detailed proof can be found in Appendix
C
(in the non-quantitative case), although Fig.
12
synthetizes the core arguments. For that, we define and use the notion of residual of a biposition, which will be instrumental to prove that approximability is stable under reduction and expansion. The reader can skip this section if they want to and keep in mind the following summary:

•

Residuation is defined for almost all
right
bipositions (but not for
left
ones) following Fig.
12
.

•

It is actually not defined when the (right) biposition points inside the application, the abstraction or an occurrence of the bound variable of the contracted redex.

•

Residuation is a partial injection, but it is surjective: every biposition of the derivation typing the reduct is the residual of a biposition in the initial derivation.

Hypotheses and Notations.

We assume again that
P
is a (quantitative or not)
\mathtt{S}
-derivation typing
t
and
t|_{b}=(\lambda x.r)s
. We set
t^{\prime}|_{b}=r[s/x]
, so that
t\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}t^{\prime}
.
We then use the following notations and conventions:

•

Let
\beta\in\mathtt{supp}(t)
.
We set
\mathtt{Rep}_{P}(\beta)=\{\alpha\in\mathtt{supp}(P)\,|\,\overline{\alpha}=\beta\}
. If
\alpha\in\mathtt{Rep}_{P}(\beta)
, we say that
\alpha
is a representative of
\beta
(inside
P
).

•

The metavariable
a
ranges over
\mathtt{Rep}_{P}(b)
,
i.e.
, over the representatives of the position of the contracted redex. The metavariable
\alpha
will be used for any position in
\mathbb{N}^{*}
.

•

If
a\in\mathtt{Rep}_{P}(b)
, we set
\mathtt{Ax}^{\lambda}_{P}(a):=\mathtt{Ax}_{a\cdot 10}(x)
and
\mathtt{Tr}^{\lambda}_{P}(a)=\mathtt{Rt}(\mathtt{C}^{P}(a\cdot 10)(x))
. Since
P
is assumed to be quantitative,
\mathtt{Tr}^{\lambda}_{P}(a):=\{\mathtt{tr}(\alpha_{0})\,|\,\alpha_{0}\in\mathtt{Ax}^{\lambda}(a)\}
.
Thus,
\mathtt{Ax}^{\lambda}_{P}(a)
is the set of positions of the redex variable (to be substituted) above
a
and
\mathtt{Tr}^{\lambda}_{P}(a)
is the set of the axiom tracks that have been used for them. We often omit
P
in the notations.

For instance, in Fig
12
,
\mathtt{Ax}^{\lambda}(a)=\{a\cdot 10\cdot a_{2},\;a\cdot 10\cdot a_{3},a\cdot 10\cdot a_{7}\}
and
\mathtt{Tr}^{\lambda}(a)=\{2,3,7\}
.
Since
P
is quantitative,
\mathtt{C}(a\cdot 10)(x)
must be of the form
(k\cdot S_{k})_{{k\in K}}
where
K=\mathtt{Tr}^{\lambda}(a)
, for any
a\in\mathtt{Rep}_{P}(b)
.

For
k\in\mathtt{Tr}^{\lambda}(a)
, we write
a_{k}
for the unique
a_{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
such that
\mathtt{pos}(a\cdot 10,\,x,\,k)=a\cdot 10\cdot a_{k}
: thus,
a\cdot 10\cdot a_{k}
is the position of the axiom rule typing
x
above
a
using axiom track
k
.

Residuation for positions.
Assume
\alpha\in A,~\overline{\alpha}\neq a,\,a\cdot 1,\,a\cdot 10\cdot a_{k}
for no
a\in\mathtt{Rep}_{P}(b)
and
k\in\mathtt{Tr}^{\lambda}_{P}(a)
. We follow Fig.
12
and the
residual position
of
\alpha
, written
\mathtt{Res}_{b}(\alpha)
, is defined as follows:

•

If
\alpha\geqslant a\cdot k\cdot\alpha_{0}
for some
a\in\mathtt{Rep}_{P}(b)
and
k\geqslant 2
(paradigm
\clubsuit
), then
\mathtt{Res}_{b}(\alpha)=a\cdot a_{k}\cdot\alpha_{0}

•

If
\alpha=a\cdot 10\cdot\alpha_{0}
for some
a\in\mathtt{Rep}_{P}(b)
with
\alpha_{0}\notin a_{k}
(paradigm
\heartsuit
), then
\mathtt{Res}_{b}(\alpha)=a\cdot\alpha_{0}

•

If
\overline{a}\ngeqslant b
,
\mathtt{Res}_{b}(\alpha)=a
.

By case analysis, we notice that
t^{\prime}(\mathtt{Res}_{b}(a))=t(a)
for all
a\in\mathtt{dom}(\mathtt{Res}_{b})
. We set
A^{\prime}=\mathtt{codom}(\mathtt{Res}_{b})
(we call
A^{\prime}

residual support
of
P
).

Residuation for
right
bipositions.
Now, whenever

\alpha^{\prime}:=\mathtt{Res}_{b}(\alpha)
is defined, the
residual
biposition
of
\mathtt{p}:=(\alpha,\gamma)\in{\mathtt{bisupp}(P)}
is
\mathtt{Res}_{b}(\mathtt{p})=(\alpha^{\prime},\,\gamma)
.

We notice that
\mathtt{Res}_{b}
is a
partial injective

function (both for positions and right bipositions). In particular,

\mathtt{Res}_{b}
is a bijection from
\mathtt{dom}(\mathtt{Res}_{b})
to
A^{\prime}
and we write

\mathtt{Res}_{b}^{-1}
for its inverse. We set, for all
\alpha^{\prime}\in A^{\prime}
,
\mathtt{T}^{\prime}(\alpha)=\mathtt{T}(\mathtt{Res}^{-1}_{b}(\alpha^{\prime}))
.

Handling contexts.
For any
\alpha^{\prime}\in A^{\prime}
, let
\mathtt{C}^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime})
be the context defined by
\mathtt{C}^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime}):=(\mathtt{C}(\alpha)\setminus x)\uplus(\uplus_{k\in\mathtt{K}(\alpha)}\mathtt{C}(\alpha\cdot k))
, where
\alpha:=\mathtt{Res}_{b}^{-1}(\alpha^{\prime})
,
\mathtt{K}(\alpha):=\mathtt{Rt}(\mathtt{C}(\alpha)(x))

and
\mathtt{C}^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime})\setminus x
is the context
C^{\prime}
such that
C^{\prime}(x)=(\,)
and
C^{\prime}(y)=\mathtt{C}^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime})(y)
for all
y\neq x
. Intuitively,
\mathtt{C}^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime})
erases the types assigned to
x
and replace them by the contexts assigned to the matching occurrences of
s
, as expected in the reduct, where the argument
s
has replaced
x
.
Notice that
\mathtt{C}^{\prime}(\alpha)=\mathtt{C}(\alpha)
for any
\alpha\in A
such that
\overline{\alpha}\ngtr b
,
e.g.
,
\mathtt{C}^{\prime}(\varepsilon)=\mathtt{C}(\varepsilon)=C
. Moreover, since
\mathtt{Res}_{b}(\varepsilon)=\varepsilon
,
\mathtt{T}^{\prime}(\varepsilon)=\mathtt{T}(\varepsilon)=T
.

Remark 7
.

•

Residuation is defined for
right
bipositions only (and not for left ones). Indeed, there is not good way to define residiuals for left bipositions. This may be understood by having a look à Fig.
12
and remembering that left bipositions pertain to left-hand sides of judgments,
i.e.
, in the typing contexts. When reduction is performed, a judgment nested in the argument
s
may be moved at
higher depth
in
r
. But typing context have “descendants” below them till the root of the derivation. For instance, if
y
is free in
t
and
y:(k\cdot S_{k})_{{k\in K}}
appears somewhere in
s
, then we find
y:(k\cdot S_{k})_{{k\in K}}
all the way down to the root of
t
. When
s
is substituted when
t\rightarrow_{\!\beta}t^{\prime}
,
y:(k\cdot S_{k})_{{k\in K}}
may have more descendants in
t^{\prime}
than in
t
. In these conditions, it is not possible to have quantitative notion of residuation for contexts, at least naively (it is actually possible, but only if we consider
classes
of left bipositions, as in
[
Via18
]
)

•

However, altough residuation cannot be defined for left bipositions, morally, the definition
\mathtt{C}^{\prime}
specifies the effect of reduction on the
left
-hand sides of judgments of derivation
P
.

We define now
P^{\prime}
as the labelled tree such that
\mathtt{supp}(P^{\prime})=A^{\prime}
and
P^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime})
is
\mathtt{C}^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime})\vdash t^{\prime}|_{\alpha^{\prime}}:\,\mathtt{T}^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime})
. We claim that
P^{\prime}
is a correct derivation concluded by
C\vdash t^{\prime}:T
: indeed,
\overline{A^{\prime}}\subset\mathtt{supp}(t^{\prime})
stems from
\overline{A}\subset\mathtt{supp}(t)
. Then, for any
\alpha^{\prime}\in A^{\prime}
and
\alpha=\mathtt{Res}_{b}^{-1}(\alpha)
,

t^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime})=t(\alpha)
and the rule at position
\alpha^{\prime}

is correct in
P^{\prime}
because the rule at position
\alpha
in
P
is
correct after we notice that:

•

In the abstraction case,
t^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime})=\lambda y
implies
\mathtt{C}^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime}\cdot 0)(y)=\mathtt{C}(\alpha\cdot 0)(y)
).

•

If
t^{\prime}|_{\alpha^{\prime}}
is a substituted occurrence of
s
, then,
t(\alpha)=x
and
P(\alpha)=x:(k\cdot\mathtt{T}(\alpha))\vdash x:\mathtt{T}(\alpha)
with
\alpha=a\cdot 10\cdot a_{k}
for some
a\in\mathtt{Rep}_{P}(b)
and the typing rules ensure that
\mathtt{T}(\alpha)=\mathtt{T}(a\cdot k)=\mathtt{T}^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime})
.

Thus,
P^{\prime}
is a derivation concluding with
C\vdash t^{\prime}:T
. This proves that the subject reduction property holds in system
\mathtt{S}
.

Observation 19
.

As noted above,
\mathtt{Res}_{b}
is a surjective and partial injective function from
\mathtt{supp}(P)
to
\mathtt{supp}(P^{\prime})
(thus,
\mathtt{Res}^{-1}(\mathtt{p}^{\prime})
is always a singleton set when
\mathtt{p}^{\prime}\in\mathtt{bisupp}_{\mathtt{R}}(P^{\prime})
). Actually there are 3 cases whan
\mathtt{Res}_{b}(\alpha)
is not defined:

•

\alpha
points to an
\mathtt{ax}
-rule typing the variable of the redex:
t(\alpha)=x
. Equivalently,
\alpha=a\cdot 10\cdot a_{k}
for some
a\in\mathtt{Rep}_{P}(b)
and
k\in\mathtt{Tr}^{\lambda}(a)
.

•

\alpha
points to the contracted redex:
t|_{\alpha}=(\lambda x.r)s
. Equivalently,
\alpha=a\in\mathtt{Rep}_{P}(b)
.

•

\alpha
points to the abstraction of the constracted redex:
t|_{\alpha}=\lambda x.r
. Equivalently,
\alpha=a\cdot 1
for some
a\in\mathtt{Rep}_{P}(b)
.

Actually, the constructions presented here are mostly valid when
P
is not assumed to be quantitative anymore. In that case,
\mathtt{Tr}^{\lambda}_{P}(a):=\mathtt{Rt}(\mathtt{C}^{P}(a\cdot 10)(x))
may be different from
\mathtt{X}(a):=\{\mathtt{tr}(\alpha_{0})\,|\,\alpha_{0}\in\mathtt{Ax}^{\lambda}(a)\}
(
\mathtt{X}(a)\subseteq\mathtt{Tr}^{\lambda}_{P}(a)
holds and
\mathtt{Tr}^{\lambda}_{P}(a)\setminus\mathtt{X}(a)
corresponds to the phantom axiom tracks of
x
above
a\cdot 10
). Then,
\mathtt{C}^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime})
is still defined as
(\mathtt{C}(\alpha)\setminus x)\uplus(\uplus_{k\in\mathtt{Tr}^{\lambda}(a)}\mathtt{C}(\alpha\cdot k))
with
\mathtt{K}(\alpha):=\mathtt{Rt}(\mathtt{C}(\alpha)(x))
(thus,
\mathtt{K}(\alpha)
may contain some phantom axiom tracks) but
\mathtt{Res}_{b}(a\cdot k\cdot\alpha)
is not defined anymore when
k\in\mathtt{Tr}^{\lambda}(a)\setminus\mathtt{K}(a)

6.
Approximable Derivations and Unforgetfulness

We formally define
approximations
and
approximability
(Sec.
6.1
). We prove that one-step proof reduction is monotonic. Sec.
6.2
shows that very left-biposition is intrisically related to a right one, which is pivotal to prove soundness later (if a term is approximablity typable, then it is normalizing).
In Sec.
6.3
, we define the unforgetfulness criterion in system
\mathtt{S}
.
We prove infinitary subject reduction and that the infinite proof reduction actually preserves approximability (Sec.
6.4
). As a by-product, we prove that if a term is suitably typed, then it is hereditary head normalizing, which is one of the two implication of the fundamental equivalence between typability and infinitary normalization.

6.1.
Approximability and the Lattice of Approximations

In Sec.
5.4
, we saw
\mathscr{R}
is unfit to recover soundness through approximability. Let us now work with system
\mathtt{S}
only and formalize this notion.

As seen in Sec.
3.6
, we must be able to truncate derivations (notion of approximation) and define the join of some families of derivations. This can be properly done in system
\mathtt{S}
:

Definition 20

(Approximations)
.

•

Let
P
and
P_{*}
be two derivations typing a same term
t
. We say

P_{*}
is an
approximation
of
P
, and we write
P_{*}\leqslant_{\infty}P
, if
{\mathtt{bisupp}(P_{*})}\subseteq{\mathtt{bisupp}(P)}
and for all
\mathtt{p}\in{\mathtt{bisupp}(P_{*})},~P_{*}(\mathtt{p})=P(\mathtt{p})
.

•

We write
\mathtt{Approx}_{\infty}(P)
for the set of approximations of a derivation
P
and
\mathtt{Approx}(P)
for the set of
finite
approximations of
P
.

Thus,
P_{*}\leqslant_{\infty}P
if
P_{*}
is a
correct
restriction of
P
to a subset of
{\mathtt{bisupp}(P)}
(
i.e.
, a restriction that gives an
\mathtt{S}
-derivation). We usually write
\,\!{}^{f}\!P
for a
finite
approximation of
P
(
i.e.
,
{\mathtt{bisupp}(\,\!^{f}\!P)}
is finite) and in that case only, we write
\,\!{}^{f}\!P\leqslant P
instead of
\,\!{}^{f}\!P\leqslant_{\infty}P
. Actually,
\leqslant_{\infty}
and
\leqslant
are associated to lattice structures induced by the set-theoretic inclusion, union and intersection on bisupports :

Theorem 3
.

Let
t
be a 001-term.
The set of derivations typing
t
is a directed complete semi-lattice w.r.t.
\leqslant_{\infty}
:

•

If
D
is a directed set of derivations typing
t
:

–

The
join

\mathop{\mathtt{sup}}D
of
D
is the function
P
defined by
\mathtt{dom}(P)=\cup_{P_{*}\in D}{\mathtt{bisupp}(P_{*})}
and
P(\mathtt{p})=P_{*}(\mathtt{p})
(for any
P_{*}\in D
such that
\mathtt{p}\in{\mathtt{bisupp}(P_{*})}
), which also is a derivation.

–

The
meet

\mathop{\mathtt{inf}}D
of
D
is the function
P
defined by
\mathtt{dom}(P)=\cap_{P_{*}\in D}{\mathtt{bisupp}(P_{*})}
and
P(\mathtt{p})=P_{*}(\mathtt{p})
(for all
P_{*}\in D
), which also is a derivation.

•

If
P
is a derivation typing
t
,
\mathtt{Approx}_{\infty}(P)
is a complete lattice and
\mathtt{Approx}(P)
is a lattice.

Proof.

See Appendix
B
for the details.
∎

Remark 8
.

Some
\mathtt{S}
-derivations do not have any finite approximations,
e.g.
, any
\mathtt{S}
-derivation typing
\Omega
, because
\Omega
is not head normalizing.

Approximation is compatible with reduction:

Lemma 21

(Monotonicity of approximation)
.

(1)

Reduction is monotonic: if
P_{*}\leqslant_{\infty}P,~P_{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\rightarrow}}P^{\prime}_{*}
and
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\rightarrow}}P^{\prime}
, then
P^{\prime}_{*}\leqslant_{\infty}P^{\prime}
.

(2)

If
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\rightarrow}}P^{\prime}
, then, for any
P^{\prime}_{*}\leqslant_{\infty}P^{\prime}
,
there is a unique
P_{*}\leqslant_{\infty}P
such that
P^{\prime}_{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\rightarrow}}P^{\prime}_{*}
.

(3)

Uniform expansion is monotonic: let
\lfloor\cdot\rfloor:\mathbb{N}^{*}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0,1\}
an injection.
If
P^{\prime}
types
t^{\prime}
,
t\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\rightarrow}}t^{\prime}
and
P^{\prime}_{*}\leqslant_{\infty}P^{\prime}
, then
P_{*}\leqslant_{\infty}P
with
P_{*}=\mathtt{Exp}_{b}(P^{\prime}_{*},\lfloor\cdot\rfloor,t),~P=\mathtt{Exp}_{b}(P^{\prime},\lfloor\cdot\rfloor,t)
.

As we saw in Sec.
5.4
, determinism of reduction in system
\mathtt{S}
is crucial to ensure Point 1 of Lemma
21
, which in turn is pivotal to ensure that the notion of approximation is well-behaved w.r.t. conversion.

We define here our validity condition,
i.e.
, approximability: a derivation
P
is approximable when it is the join of all its finite approximations.
Intuitively, this means that
P
is approximable if all its bipositions are
meaningful,
i.e.
, can be part of a
finite
derivation
\,\!{}^{f}\!P
approximating
P
.

Definition 22
.

A
\mathtt{S}
-derivation
P
is said to be
approximable
if
P
is the join of its
finite
approximations,
i.e.
,
P=\mathop{\mathtt{sup}}_{\,\!{}^{f}\!P\leqslant P}\,\!^{f}\!P
.

By definition of
\leqslant
, we immediately obtain the following characterization of approximability:

Lemma 23
.

A
\mathtt{S}
-derivation
P
is approximable iff, for all finite
\,\!{}^{0}\!B\subseteq{\mathtt{bisupp}(P)}
, there is a
\,\!{}^{f}\!P\leqslant P
such that
\,\!{}^{0}\!B\subseteq{\mathtt{bisupp}(\,\!^{f}\!P)}
.

6.2.
Equinecessity: forgetting about left bipositions

Approximability is stable under reduction and expansion (forthcoming Lemma
6.3
) and as expected, it is a crucial property of system
\mathtt{S}
. However, there are some difficulties to overcome:

•

The approximability condition (Lemma
23
) pertains both to right and
left
bipositions.

•

Thus, this stability property shall involve residuals of bipositions.

•

But residuals are defined for
right
bipositions only (Remark
7
).

There seems to be a catch. However, approximability may be reduced to
right
bipositions only, as we sketch in this section (full details are in Appendix
C
).

Assume for instance that
P
is a derivation, that
x:(k\cdot S_{k})\vdash x:S_{k}
is an axiom node in
P
occuring at position
a
and
P_{*}\leqslant_{\infty}P
is a finite or not approximation of
P
.
Let us write
\mathtt{T}
and
\mathtt{T}_{*}
for
\mathtt{T}^{P}
and
\mathtt{T}^{P_{*}}
, and so on. We have thus
S_{k}=\mathtt{T}(a)
and
x:(k\cdot S_{k})=\mathtt{C}(a)
.

Notice that
P_{*}
may not contain that axiom rule (
i.e.
, both
a\in\mathtt{supp}(P_{*})
and
a\notin\mathtt{supp}(P_{*})
are possible
a priori
): it occurs whenever this axiom has been cut off during truncation. Assume that
a\in\mathtt{supp}(P_{*})
:
in particular,
(a,\varepsilon)\in\mathtt{supp}(P_{*})
, since it points to the root of
\mathtt{T}(a)
. Actually, since
P_{*}(a)=x:(k\cdot\mathtt{T}_{*}(a))\vdash x:\mathtt{T}_{*}(a)
,
\mathtt{supp}(\mathtt{C}_{*}(a)(x))=k\cdot\mathtt{supp}(\mathtt{T}_{*}(A))
. This means that, for all
c\in\mathbb{N}
,
(a,x,k\cdot c)\in{\mathtt{bisupp}(P_{*})}
iff
(a,c)\in{\mathtt{bisupp}(P_{*})}
. We say then that bipositions
(a,x,k\cdot c)
and
(a,c)
are
equinecessary w.r.t. derivation
P
(the notion of equinecessity depends on
P
, the considered derivation).

A crucial observation is that the cases of equinecessity we have observed above relate left
bipositions
with right ones (
(a,x,k\cdot)
is on the left and
(a\,c)
on the right). Actually, we showed that, in an axiom node, any left biposition is equinecessary with a right-one (since
\mathtt{supp}(\mathtt{C}(a)(y))=\emptyset
if
y\neq x
and
\mathtt{supp}(\mathtt{C}(a))(x)=k\cdot\mathtt{supp}(\mathtt{T}(a)))
. This expresses the fact that, in an
\mathtt{ax}
-rule, the left-hand side and the right-hand side are the same (up to the axiom track in the context).

More generally, in a quantitative derivation (Definition
16
), any left biposition is equinecessary with a right one. Indeed, by definition, in a quantitative derivation, contexts can be computed by looking at the axiom rules and any left biposition must come from the left-hand side of an axiom rule (since there are no phantom axiom tracks). We may thus observe:

Observation 24
.

Let
P
be a quantitative derivation. Every left biposition of
P
is equinecessary with at least one right biposition.

Observation
24
is proved in details in Appendix
C
.
But our troubles are not over yet: when
P
types
t^{\prime}
and
t\rightarrow_{\!\beta}t^{\prime}
, some right bipositions
\mathtt{p}\in{\mathtt{bisupp}(P)}
do not have a residual. If we want to transport approximability through reduction and expansion, we need to handle this. The idea is that any right biposition is equinecessary with one which has a residual.

By Remark
19
,
\mathtt{Res}_{b}(\mathtt{p})
(with
\mathtt{p}=(\alpha,c)
) is not defined when
t(\alpha)=x
,
t|_{\alpha}=(\lambda x.r)s
or
t|_{\alpha}=\lambda x.r
. We explain now why in each case,
\mathtt{p}
and some biposition which has a residual are equinecessary.

(1)

If
t(\alpha)=x
, say
\alpha=a\cdot 10\cdot a_{k}
,
P(\alpha)=x:(k\cdot S_{k})\vdash x:S_{k}
with
S_{k}:=\mathtt{T}(a)
. Then the argument
s
of the redex must also have type
S_{k}
. Namely,
P(a\cdot k)=D\vdash s:S_{k}
for some context
D
, and it is clear that biposition
\mathtt{p}=(a\cdot 10\cdot a_{k},c)
and biposition
(a\cdot k,c)
, which has a residual, are equinecessary.

(2)

If
t|_{\alpha}=(\lambda x.r)s
, then
\alpha=a\in\mathtt{Rep}_{P}(b)
. Since
r
and
(\lambda x.r)s
must have the same type, it is clear
that
\mathtt{p}=(a,c)
and
\mathtt{p}_{0}:=(a\cdot 10,c)
are equinecessary.
Then, either
t(a\cdot 10)\neq x
and
\mathtt{p}_{0}
has a residual (we are done) or
t(a\cdot 10)=x
and we use case 1 to conclude that
\mathtt{p}
is equinecessary with a biposition which has a residual.

(3)

If
t|_{\alpha}=\lambda x.r
, then
\alpha=a\cdot 1
with
\overline{a}=b
and
P(\alpha)=C\vdash\lambda x.t:(S_{k})_{{k\in K}}\rightarrow T
where
T:=\mathtt{T}(a)
(which is the type of
(\lambda x.r)s
) and the
(S_{k})_{{k\in K}}
have been assigned to
x
. Then either
\mathtt{p}=(\alpha,c)
points to a symbol inside
T
(we may then use case 2) or
\mathtt{p}
points to a symbol in some
S_{k}
(we may then use case 1) or
c=\varepsilon
,
i.e.
,
\mathtt{p}
points to the top-level
\rightarrow
in
(S_{k})_{{k\in K}}\rightarrow T
. In the latter case,
\mathtt{p}=(a\cdot 1,\varepsilon)
is equinecessary to
\mathtt{p}_{0}:=(a\cdot 1,1)
(if there is an arrow a position
\varepsilon
, there must me something at position 1). Since
\mathtt{p}_{0}
points to a symbol in
T
and that this case has already been handled, this shows also that
\mathtt{p}
is equinecessary to a biposition which has a residual.

We may thus state:

Observation 25
.

Let
P
be a quantitative derivation. Every left biposition of
P
is equinecessary with at least one right biposition which has a residual.

Observation
25
is proved in detail in Appendix
C.4
.

Quantitativity (Definition
16
) is necessary to ensure approximability.

Lemma 26

(Approximability and Quantitativity)
.

If
P
is not quantitative, then
P
is not approximable.

Proof.

If
P
is not quantitative, then
P
contains some left biposition
\mathtt{p}:=(a,x,k\cdot c)
that does not come from an
\mathtt{ax}
-rule,
i.e.
,
k
is a phantom axiom track. This phantom axiom track must have infinitely many equinecessary occurrences (along an infinite branch).
An approximation
P_{*}\leqslant_{\infty}P
that contains
\mathtt{p}
has to contain all these occurrences and thus, cannot be finite. So
P
cannot be approximable.
∎

Lemma 27

(Approximability is stable under reduction and expansion)
.

If
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\rightarrow}}P^{\prime}
, then
P
is approximable iff
P^{\prime}
is approximable.

Proof.

We use Lemma
23
.

•

Assume
P
approximable. Let us show that
P^{\prime}
is also approximable.
First, since
P
is approximable, then
P
is quantitative by Lemma
26
, so
P^{\prime}
is also quantitative (Remark
6
).
Let
\,\!{}^{0}\!B^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathtt{bisupp}(P^{\prime})}
be a finite set of bipositions. We must prove that there exists
\,\!{}^{f}\!P^{\prime}\leqslant P^{\prime}
such that
\,\!{}^{0}\!B^{\prime}\lx@nobreakspace\subseteq{\mathtt{bisupp}(\,\!^{f}\!P^{\prime})}
.
By Observation
24
, there is a finite set
\,\!{}^{0}\!B^{\prime}_{0}
of right bipositions such that every
\mathtt{p}^{\prime}\in\,\!^{0}\!B^{\prime}
is equinecessary with some
\mathtt{p}^{\prime}_{0}\in\,\!^{0}\!B^{\prime}_{0}
.
Let
\,\!{}^{0}\!B:=\mathtt{Res}^{-1}_{b}(\,\!^{0}\!B^{\prime}_{0})
. Since
\mathtt{Res}_{b}
is surjective on right biposition (Observation
19
),
\,\!{}^{0}\!B^{\prime}_{0}\subseteq\mathtt{Res}_{b}(\,\!^{0}\!B)
.
Since
P
is approximable, there is
\,\!{}^{f}\!P\leqslant P
such that
\,\!{}^{0}\!B\in{\mathtt{bisupp}(\,\!^{f}\!P)}
. Let
\,\!{}^{f}\!P^{\prime}
defined by
\,\!{}^{f}\!P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}\,\!^{f}\!P^{\prime}
, so that
\,\!{}^{0}\!B^{\prime}_{0}\subseteq{\mathtt{bisupp}(\,\!^{f}\!P^{\prime})}
. By Lemma
21
,
\,\!{}^{f}\!P^{\prime}\leqslant P^{\prime}
. By equinecessity,
\,\!{}^{0}\!B^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathtt{bisupp}(\,\!^{f}\!P^{\prime})}
. Thus,
P^{\prime}
is approximable.

•

Assume that
P^{\prime}
is approximable.
Let us show that
P
is also approximable.
First, since
P^{\prime}
is approximable, then
P^{\prime}
is quantitative by Lemma
26
, so
P
is also quantitative (Remark
6
).
Let
\,\!{}^{0}\!B\subseteq{\mathtt{bisupp}(P)}
be a finite set of bipositions. We must prove that there exists
\,\!{}^{f}\!P\leqslant P
such that
\,\!{}^{0}\!B\lx@nobreakspace\subseteq{\mathtt{bisupp}(\,\!^{f}\!P)}
.
By Observation
25
, there is a finite set
\,\!{}^{0}\!B_{0}
of right bipositions which have a residual, such that every
\mathtt{p}\in\,\!^{0}\!B
is equinecessary with some
\mathtt{p}_{0}\in\,\!^{0}\!B_{0}
.
Let
\,\!{}^{0}\!B^{\prime}:=\mathtt{Res}_{b}(\,\!^{0}\!B^{\prime}_{0})
. Since
\mathtt{Res}_{b}
is totally defined on
\,\!{}^{0}\!B_{0}
,
\,\!{}^{0}\!B_{0}\subseteq\mathtt{Res}^{-1}_{b}(\,\!^{0}\!B^{\prime})
.
Since
P^{\prime}
is approximable, there is
\,\!{}^{f}\!P^{\prime}\leqslant P^{\prime}
such that
\,\!{}^{0}\!B^{\prime}\in{\mathtt{bisupp}(\,\!^{f}\!P^{\prime})}
. By Lemma
21
(2nd point), there is
\,\!{}^{f}\!P\leqslant_{\infty}P
such that
\,\!{}^{f}\!P\rightarrow_{\!\beta}\,\!^{f}\!P^{\prime}
, so that
\,\!{}^{0}\!B_{0}\subseteq{\mathtt{bisupp}(\,\!^{f}\!P)}
. Since
\,\!{}^{f}\!P^{\prime}
is finite,
\,\!{}^{f}\!P
is also finite. By equinecessity,
\,\!{}^{0}\!B\subseteq{\mathtt{bisupp}(\,\!^{f}\!P)}
. Thus,
P
is approximable.

∎

Remark 9

(Root approximability)
.

Approximability (Definition
22
) is a condition pertaining to a whole derivation
P
in the sense that
P
is approximable iff each biposition
\mathtt{p}
in
P
is inside some finite approximation of
P
(Lemma
23
). One may wonder whether it is possible to reformulate approximability such that it pertains only to the
conclusion
of
P
, as some other criteria (
e.g.
, unforgetfulness, Sec.
3
and forthcoming Sec.
6.3
). We prove in Appendix
C.6

that there is no way to do so, at least naively.

6.3.
Unforgetfulness

We remember from Sec.
3.4
that weak normalization for the finite calculus is characterized in system
\mathscr{R}_{0}
by means of
unforgetful
derivations. In order to characterize weak normalization in
\Lambda^{001}
(Sec.
3.3
), we shall adapt Theorem
2
to system
\mathtt{S}
. This will yield Theorem
4
, one of the main results of this paper, stated as follows:

Theorem
.

A term
t
is weakly-normalizing in
\Lambda^{001}
if and only if
t
is typable by means of an approximable unforgetful derivation, and if and only if
t
is hereditary head normalizing.

To state and prove this theorem, we must first export the definition of unforgetfulness to system
\mathtt{S}
. We recall that the targets of arrows are regarded as positive and their sources as negative. The following definitions are straightforward adaptations from system
\mathscr{R}_{0}
.

Definition 28
.

Inductively:

•

For all types
T
,
(\,)
occurs negatively in
(\,)\rightarrow T
.

•

(\,)
occurs positively (resp. negatively) in
(S_{k})_{{k\in K}}
if there exists
{k\in K}
such that
(\,)
occurs positively (resp. negatively) in
S_{k}
.

•

(\,)
occurs positively (resp. negatively) in
(S_{k})_{{k\in K}}\rightarrow T
if
(\,)
occurs positively (resp. negatively) in
T
or negatively (resp. positively) in
(S_{k})_{{k\in K}}
.

Definition 29

(Unforgetfulness)
.

•

A judgment
C\vdash t:T
is
unforgetful
when
(\,)
does not occur positively in
T
and
(\,)
does not occur negatively in
C(x)
for any
x\in\mathscr{V}
.

•

A derivation is
unforgetful
when it concludes with an unforgetful judgment.

We easily check by induction on
b\in\mathtt{supp}(t)
that, if
t
is a 001-NF and
P\rhd C\vdash t:T
is unforgetful, then every subterm
t|_{b}
of
t
is typed in
P
(
i.e.
,
\forall b\in\mathtt{supp}(t),\ \exists a\in\mathtt{supp}(P),\ \overline{a}=b
).

Lemma 30
.

If
P\rhd C\vdash t:\,T
is an unforgetful derivation typing a head normal form

t=\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{p}.x\,t_{1}\ldots t_{q}
, then, there are
unforgetful subderivations of
P
typing
t_{1}
,
t_{2}
,…,
t_{q}
.
Moreover, if
P
is approximable, so are they.

Proof.

Whether
x=x_{i}
for some
i
or not, the unforgetfulness condition ensures that every argument of the head variable
x
is typed, since
(\,)
cannot occur negatively in its unique given type.
∎

Lemma 31
.

If
P\rhd C\vdash t:T
is a
finite
derivation, then

t
is head normalizing.

Proof.

•

By the typing rules, the head redex—if it exists
i.e.
, if
t
is head reducible, and not a head normal form—must be typed.

•

When we reduce a typed redex, the number of rules of the derivation must
strictly decrease (at least one
\mathtt{app}
-rule and one
\mathtt{abs}
-rule
disappear). See Figure
12
.

•

Since there is no infinite decreasing sequence of natural number, the head reduction strategy must halt at some point, meaning that a HNF is reached.

∎

Proposition 32
.

If a term
t
is typable by a unforgetful approximable derivation,
then
t
is hereditarily head normalizing.

Proof.

Assume that
P\rhd C\vdash t:T
is an approximable and unforgetful derivation.
By Lemma
31
, there is a head normal form
t^{\prime}:=\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{p}.x\,t_{1}\ldots t_{q}
such that
t\rightarrow_{\mathtt{h}}^{*}t^{\prime}
. By Proposition
17
and , there is an approximable derivation
P^{\prime}\rhd C\vdash t^{\prime}:T
. Since
P^{\prime}
and
P
conclude with the same type and context,
P^{\prime}
also is unforgetful. By Lemma
30
, there are approximable unforgetful derivations
P_{1}
,
P_{2}
,…,
P_{q}
respectively typing
t_{1}
,
t_{2}
,…,
t_{q}
. Notice that the
t_{i}
occur at applicative depth 1.

This observation allows us to build by induction on
d\in\mathbb{N}
a path
t=:t_{0}\rightarrow_{\!\beta}^{*}t_{1}\rightarrow_{\!\beta}^{*}t_{2}\rightarrow_{\!\beta}^{*}t_{d}\rightarrow_{\!\beta}^{*}\ldots
, corresponding to hereditary head reduction (Sec.
3.3
), such that the reduction steps from
t_{d}
to
t_{d+1}
all occur at applicative depth
d
. This path stops at
t_{d}
(
i.e.
, is of finite length), if
t_{d}
is a normal form. It is of infinite length if no
t_{d}
is a normal form. In both cases, it is by construction a productive reduction path.

•

If the path is finite, some
t_{d}
is a normal form. Thus,
t
is weakly normalizing.

•

If the path is of infinite length, let
t^{\prime}
be its limit. By definition of the limit, the specification of
t_{d}
entails that, for all
d\in\mathbb{N}
,
t^{\prime}
does not have any redex under applicative depth
d
. Thus,
t^{\prime}
has no redex:
t^{\prime}
is a normal form,
i.e.
,
t
is hereditarily head normalizing.

∎

6.4.
The Infinitary Subject Reduction Property

In this section, we prove subject reduction for productive reduction paths. The initial derivation may be approximable or not.

Thus, when
t{\rightarrow_{\!\beta}^{\infty}}t^{\prime}
, we have to construct a derivation
P^{\prime}
typing
t^{\prime}

from a derivation
P
typing a term
t
. The main intuition is as in Sec.
3.5
: when a reduction is performed at applicative depth
n
, the contexts and types are not affected below depth
n
. Thus, a productive reduction path stabilizes contexts and types at any fixed applicative depth. It allows to define a derivation typing the limit
t^{\prime}
.

The following (and straightforward)
subject substitution lemma
, similar to Lemma
13
, is very useful while
working with productive paths. It states that we can freely change the untyped parts of a term in a typing derivation.

Lemma 33
.

Assume
P\rhd C\vdash t:T
and for all
a\in\mathtt{supp}(P),~t(a)=t^{\prime}(a)
(
P
is not necessarily assumed to be approximable).

Let
P[t^{\prime}/t]
be the
labelled tree
obtained from
P
by replacing
t
by
t^{\prime}
(more precisely,
P[t^{\prime}/t]
is the labelled tree
P^{\prime}
such that
\mathtt{supp}(P^{\prime})=\mathtt{supp}(P)
and, for all
a\in\mathtt{supp}(P),~P^{\prime}(a)=\mathtt{C}^{P}(a)\vdash t^{\prime}|_{a}:\mathtt{T}^{P}(a)
).

Then
P[t^{\prime}/t]
is a correct derivation.

Now, let us formally prove the infinitary subject reduction property. For that, we assume:

•

t\rightarrow^{\infty}t^{\prime}
. For instance, say that
t=t_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b_{0}}}{{\rightarrow}}t_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b_{1}}}{{\rightarrow}}\dots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b_{n-1}}}{{\rightarrow}}t_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b_{n}}}{{\rightarrow}}t_{n+1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b_{n+1}}}{{\rightarrow}}\ldots
with
b_{n}\in\{0,~1,~2\}^{*}
and
\mathtt{ad}(b_{n})\longrightarrow\infty
and
t^{\prime}
is the limit of this path.

•

There is a derivation
P\rhd C\vdash t:T
and
A=\mathtt{supp}(P)
.

By following step by step the reduction path,

b_{0},~b_{1},\ldots
, we get a sequence of derivations
P_{n}\rhd C\vdash t_{n}:T
of support
A_{n}
. We then write
\mathtt{C}_{n},\;\mathtt{T}_{n}
for
\mathtt{C}^{P_{n}}
and
\mathtt{T}^{P_{n}}
. When performing
t_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b_{n}}}{{\rightarrow}}t_{n+1}
, notice that
\mathtt{C}_{n}(a)
and
\mathtt{T}_{n}(a)
are not modified for any
a
such that
b_{n}\nleqslant\overline{a}

i.e.
,
\mathtt{C}_{n}(a)=\mathtt{C}_{n+1}(a)
and
\mathtt{T}_{n}(a)=\mathtt{T}_{n+1}(a)
if
b_{n}\nleqslant\overline{a}
.

Let
a\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
be such that, for all
n\geqslant N,~\mathtt{ad}(b_{n})>\mathtt{ad}(a)
. There are two cases:

•

a\in A_{n}
for all
n\geqslant N
. Moreover,

\mathtt{C}_{n}(a)=\mathtt{C}_{N}(a),~\mathtt{T}_{n}(a)=\mathtt{T}_{N}(a)
for all
n\geqslant N
, and
t^{\prime}(a)=t_{n}(a)=t_{N}(a)
.

•

a\notin A_{n}
for all
n\geqslant N
.

We set
A^{\prime}=\{a\in\mathbb{N}^{*}\,|\,\exists N,\,\forall n\geqslant N,~a\in A_{n}\}
. We define a labelled tree
P^{\prime}
whose support is
A^{\prime}
by

P^{\prime}(a)=\mathtt{C}_{n}(a)\vdash t^{\prime}|_{a}:\mathtt{T}_{n}(a)
and we set
\mathtt{C}^{\prime}(a)=\mathtt{C}_{n}(a),\mathtt{T}^{\prime}(a)=\mathtt{T}_{n}(a)

for any
n\geqslant N(\mathtt{ad}(a))
(where
N(\ell)
is the smallest rank
N
such that
\forall n\geqslant N,~\mathtt{ad}(a_{n})>\ell
) .

Lemma 34
.

The labelled tree
P^{\prime}
is a derivation.

Proof.

Let
a\in A^{\prime}
and
n\geqslant N(|a|+1)
. Thus,
t^{\prime}(a)=t_{n}(a)
and the
types and contexts involved at node
a
and its premises are the same in

P^{\prime}
and
P_{n}
. So the node
a
of
P^{\prime}
is correct, because it is
correct in
P_{n}
.
∎

Lemma 35
.

If
P
is approximable, so is
P^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Let
\,\!{}^{0}\!B\subseteq{\mathtt{bisupp}(P^{\prime})}
. We set
\ell=\max\{\mathtt{ad}(\mathtt{p})\,|\,\mathtt{p}\in\,\!^{0}\!B\}
(
\mathtt{ad}(\mathtt{p})
is the applicative depth of the underlying
a\in\mathtt{supp}(P)
).

By productivity, there is
N
such that,
\forall n\geqslant N,~\mathtt{ad}(b_{n})\geqslant\ell+1
, and thus,
t_{N}(a)=t_{n}(a)=t^{\prime}(a),~\mathtt{C}_{N}(a)=\mathtt{C}_{n}(a)=\mathtt{C}^{\prime}(a)
and
\mathtt{T}_{N}(a)=\mathtt{T}_{n}(a)=\mathtt{T}^{\prime}(a)
for all
a
such that
\mathtt{ad}(a)\leqslant\ell
and
n\geqslant N
. In particular,
\,\!{}^{0}\!B\subseteq{\mathtt{bisupp}(P_{N})}
.

Since
P
is approximable, by Lemma
6.3
,
P_{N}
is approximable. So, there is
\,\!{}^{f}\!P_{N}\leqslant P
such that
\,\!{}^{0}\!B\subseteq{\mathtt{bisupp}(\,\!^{f}\!P_{N})}
.

Let
d=\#{\mathtt{bisupp}(\,\!^{f}\!P_{N})}
and
N^{\prime}\geqslant N
such that, for all
n\geqslant N^{\prime}
,
\mathtt{ad}(b_{n})>d
. Let
\,\!{}^{f}\!P_{N^{\prime}}
the derivation defined by
\,\!{}^{f}\!P_{N}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b_{N}}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}\ldots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b_{N^{\prime}-1}}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}\,\!^{f}\!P_{N^{\prime}}
. Since
b\in\mathtt{supp}(\,\!^{f}\!P_{N^{\prime}})
implies
\mathtt{ad}(b)\leqslant N^{\prime}<d<\mathtt{ad}(b_{n})
for any
n\geqslant N^{\prime}
,
t_{N^{\prime}}
and
t^{\prime}
do not differ on
\mathtt{supp}(\,\!^{f}\!P_{N^{\prime}})
and we can apply Lemma
33
. Thus, let
\,\!{}^{f}\!P^{\prime}
be the derivation obtained by replacing
t_{N^{\prime}}
with
t^{\prime}
in
\,\!{}^{f}\!P
. We have
\,\!{}^{0}\!B\subseteq\,\!^{f}\!P^{\prime}\leqslant P^{\prime}
. Thus,
P^{\prime}
is approximable.
∎

Proposition 36

(Infinitary Subject Reduction)
.

Assume
t\rightarrow^{\infty}t^{\prime}
and
P\rhd C\vdash t:T
. Then there exists a derivation
P^{\prime}
such that
P^{\prime}\rhd C\vdash t^{\prime}:T
.

Moreover, if
P
is approximable,
P^{\prime}
may be chosen to be approximable.

Proof.

Consequence of Lemmas
34
and
35
.
∎

7.
Typing Normal Forms and Subject Expansion

In this section, we prove that (possibly infinite) normal forms are
fully
typable (
i.e.
, without leaving untyped subterms) in system
\mathtt{S}
and that all the quantitative derivations typing a normal form are approximable (
i.e.
, the converse of Lemma
26
is valid for normal forms).

Indeed, as hinted at in Sec.
2.2
and Sec.
3.4
, a proof of
Hereditarily Head Normalizing

\Rightarrow

Typable
proceed by giving first an unforgetful typing of normal forms, and then, using a subject expansion property.

Actually, we will describe all the quantitative derivations typing any 001-normal form and prove them to be approximable. The latter part is an extra-difficulty imposed by working in the infinitary setting. Then, we will prove an infinitary subject expansion property, which is enough to show the above implication.

Describing all the possible typings of 001-normal forms is based on the distinction between what we call
constrained
and
unconstrained
positions in a normal form. We use this notions from Sec.
7.1
to Sec.
7.3
.

Constrained and Unconstrained Positions.

Infinitary normal forms (Böhm trees without
\bot
) are coinductive assemblages of head normal forms (
cf.
Sec.
3.3
). It is then important to understand how a head normal form
t=\lambda x_{1}\ldots\lambda x_{p}.x\,t_{1}\ldots t_{q}
may be typed. Let us have a look at figure
14
and ignore for the moment the
\mathtt{clev}
and positions (between chevrons) annotations: the head variable
x
has been assigned an arrow type
(S^{1}_{k})_{{k\in K}(1)}\rightarrow\ldots\rightarrow(S^{q}_{k})_{{k\in K}(q)}\rightarrow T
whereas the first argument
t_{1}
is typed with types
S^{1}_{k}
(
k
ranging over
K(1)
),…, the
q
-th argument
t_{q}
is typed with types
S^{q}_{k}
(
k
ranging over
K(q)
).

In the term
t
, the subterms
x,\,x\,t_{1},\,x\,t_{1}t_{2},\ldots,\,x\,t_{1}\ldots t_{q\!-\!1}
, which are “partial” applications of the head variable
x
,
are typed with arrow types, as well as the subterms
\lambda x_{p}.x\,t_{1}\ldots t_{q},\,\lambda x_{p\!-\!1}x_{p}.x\,t_{1}\ldots t_{q},\ldots
and
\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{p}.x\,t_{1}\ldots t_{q}
, which start with abstractions. By contrast, notice that subterm
x\,t_{1}\ldots t_{n}
, where
x
is “fully” applied, has type
T
and that this type
T
may be
any
type. So, we say that the type of subterm
x\,t_{1}\ldots t_{q}
is
unconstrained
(in
t
), whereas for instance:

•

x\,t_{1}\ldots t_{q\!-\!1}
has type
(S^{q}_{k})_{{k\in K}(q)}\rightarrow T
: this type depends on the types
S^{q}_{k}
given to the subterm
t_{q}
. We say informally that it
calls
for the types of the subterm
t_{q}
.

•

\lambda x_{p}.x\,t_{1}\ldots t_{q}
has type
\mathtt{C}(0^{p})(x_{p})\rightarrow T
: this type depends on the types assigned to
x_{p}
deeper in the term, which are stored in
\mathtt{C}(0^{p})(x_{p})
. We say informally that it
calls for the types
of variable the
x_{p}
.

\mathtt{clev}
q
q\!-\!\!1
1
0
1
p
x
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
t_{1}
\char 64\relax
t_{\!q\!-\!1}
\char 64\relax
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}
t_{q}
\char 64\relax
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}0}
\lambda\!\!\;x_{p}
\lambda\!\!\;x_{1}
Subtree of a normal form
t
Corresponding types
(S^{1}_{k})_{{k\in K}(1)}\rightarrow\!\ldots\!\rightarrow(S^{q}_{k})_{{k\in K}(q)}\rightarrow T~{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle\mathring{a}_{q}\rangle}
(S^{1}_{k})_{{k\in K}(1)}
(S^{2}_{k})_{{k\in K}(2)}\rightarrow\!\ldots\!\rightarrow(S^{q}_{k})_{{k\in K}(q)}\rightarrow T~{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle\mathring{a}_{q\!-\!1}\rangle}
(S^{q\!-\!1}_{k})_{{k\in K}(q\!-\!1)}
(S^{q}_{k})_{{k\in K}(q)}\rightarrow T~{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle\mathring{a}_{1}\rangle}
(S^{q}_{k})_{{k\in K}(q)}
T~{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle\mathring{a}\rangle}
\mathtt{C}(\mathring{a})(x_{p})\rightarrow T~{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle\mathring{a}_{-\!1}\rangle}
\mathtt{C}(\mathring{a}_{1\!-\!p})(x_{1})\rightarrow\!\ldots\!\rightarrow\mathtt{C}(\mathring{a})(x_{p})\rightarrow T~{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\langle\mathring{a}_{-\!p}\rangle}

Figure 14.
Typing Normal Forms

7.1.
Support Candidates

Before using the notion of constrained position above, we must devote our attention to the possible forms of the support
A:=\mathtt{supp}(P)
of a derivation
P
typing a 001-normal form
t
. This is done by considering the
preorder

\prec
defined below, extending the prefix order.

If
\overline{a},\overline{a}^{\prime}\in\mathtt{supp}(t)
, we write
a\prec a^{\prime}
when there exists
a_{0}
such that
a_{0}\leqslant a,~a_{0}\leqslant a^{\prime}
and
\mathtt{ad}(a)=\mathtt{ad}(a_{0})
. Then we observe that if
P
is a derivation typing
t
, then
\mathtt{supp}(P)
is downward closed for
\prec
over
\mathtt{supp}(t)
, meaning by that, for all
a_{1}
such that
\overline{a_{1}}\in\mathtt{supp}(t)
and
a_{2}\in\mathtt{supp}(P)
, then
a_{1}\prec a_{2}
implies
a_{1}\in\mathtt{supp}(P)
. Intuitively,
a\prec a^{\prime}
holds when
a
and
a^{\prime}
are in the same sub-head normal form of
t
or if
a^{\prime}
is nested in an argument derivation of the sub-head normal form containing
a
.

For instance,
a:=021031\prec 021037=:a^{\prime}
since
a_{0}:=02103\leqslant a,a^{\prime}
and
\mathtt{ad}(a_{0})=\mathtt{ad}(a)=2
. If
021037\in\mathtt{supp}(P)
, since
7
is an argument track,
t(02103)=\char 64\relax
(
i.e.
,
a
points to an
\mathtt{app}
-rule in
P
). Last,
02031
, which is this
\mathtt{app}
-rule left-hand side, should also be in
\mathtt{supp}(P)
, as well as every prefix of
021037
. And we have thus
021031\in\mathtt{supp}(P)
as expected.

Conversely, a non-empty set
A
downward closed for
\prec
such that
\overline{A}\subseteq\mathtt{supp}(t)

will be called a
support candidate
for a derivation typing
t
and we prove that, for all support candidates
A
associated with a normal form
t
, there is indeed a derivation
P
typing
t
such that
A=\mathtt{supp}(P)
. This shall be Lemma
37
.

For now, assume that there is a derivation
P
typing the 001-normal form
t
. Each
a\in A
is either an unconstrained position or
related
to an unconstrained position, which we denote
\mathring{a}
, in the sense that
a
is intuitively in the same head normal form than
\mathring{a}
(inside
P
). The
constrain level

\mathtt{clev}(a)
of
a
is defined as the graph distance between
a
and
\mathring{a}
.
First, notice that, given
a\in A:=\mathtt{supp}(P)
,
we have
t|_{a}=\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{n}.u
, where
u
is not an abstraction. The integer
n
is sometimes called the
order
(as in
[
BCFS16
]
) of
t|_{a}
. We say then that
a
is an order
n
position.

•

If
n\geqslant 1
, we say that
a
is a
non-zero position
and we set
\mathring{a}=a\cdot 0^{n}
(so that
t|_{\mathring{a}}=u
) and
\mathtt{clev}(a)=n
.

•

If
n=0
, we distinguish two subcases, by first defining
\mathring{a}
as the shortest prefix
a_{0}\leqslant a
such that
a=a_{0}\cdot 1^{\ell}
(for some
\ell
) and setting
\mathtt{clev}(a)=\ell
:

- If
\ell=0
, then we set
\mathring{a}=a
and we say that
a
is a
unconstrained position
.

- If
\ell\geqslant 1
, we say that
a
is a
partial (zero) position
.

As it has been observed above,
a
is unconstrained when, intuitively, the type of the underlying subterm does not depend on deeper parts of the derivation. If
i\geqslant 0
, we write
\mathring{a}_{i}
for
\mathring{a}\cdot 1^{i}
, and
\mathring{a}_{-i}
for the rank
i
prefix of
\mathring{a}
(
e.g.
,
\mathring{a}=\mathring{a}_{\!-\!2}\cdot 0^{2}
if
t|_{\mathring{a}}
is of order
\geqslant 2
). Thus, if
a\in\mathtt{supp}(P)
and
d:=\mathtt{clev}(a)
, then
a=\mathring{a}_{d}
if
a
is a partial zero position and
a=\mathring{a}_{-d}
if
a
is a non-zero position.
More generally, following the discussion beginning Sec.
7
, we observe that
if
\mathtt{clev}(a)=d
, then
\mathtt{T}(a)
is an arrow type
F_{1}\rightarrow\ldots\rightarrow F_{d}\rightarrow\mathtt{T}(\mathring{a})
, where
\mathtt{T}(\mathring{a})
is an unconstrained type. More precisely, using Fig.
14
to guide us:

•

When
a
is a non-zero position,
i.e.
,
\mathring{a}=a\cdot 0^{d}
and
t|_{a}
is of the form
\lambda x_{q\!-\!d\!+\!1}\ldots\lambda x_{p}.x\,t_{1}\ldots t_{q}
with
t|_{\mathring{a}}=x\,t_{1}\ldots t_{q}
, then
F_{1}=\mathtt{C}(a\cdot 0)(x_{q\!-\!d\!+\!1}),\,F_{2}=\mathtt{C}(a\cdot 0^{2})(x_{q\!-\!d\!+\!2}),\ldots,\,F_{d}=\mathtt{C}(a\cdot 0^{d})(x_{p})=\mathtt{C}(\mathring{a})(x_{p})
.

•

When
a
is partial
i.e.
,
a=\mathring{a}\cdot 1^{d}
and
t|_{\mathring{a}}
is of the form
x\,t_{1}\ldots t_{q}
with
t|_{a}=x\,t_{1}\ldots t_{p\!-\!d}
, then
t|_{\mathring{a}}=t|_{a}\,t_{p\!-\!d\!+\!1}\ldots t_{q}
and

F_{1}:=(k\cdot\mathtt{T}(\mathring{a}\cdot 1^{d-1}\cdot k))_{k\in\mathtt{ArgTr}^{1}(a)},\ldots,\,F_{d}:=(k\cdot\mathtt{T}(\mathring{a}\cdot k))_{k\in\mathtt{ArgTr}^{k}(a)}
(see end of Sec.
4.2
for notation
\mathtt{ArgTr}^{i}
): thus,
F_{i}
is the sequence of types given to the
i
-th argument
t_{p\!-\!d\!+\!i}
of
t|_{a}
w.r.t. position
a
.

7.2.
Natural Extensions

Let
A
be a support candidate for the 001-normal form
t
. We want to build a derivation
P
such that
\mathtt{supp}(P)=A
. For that, we need to find types for every subterm
t|_{a}
when
a
ranges over
A
. To obtain this, let us just assign first types at the unconstrained positions in
A
. Thus, let
\mathring{\mathtt{T}}
be a function from
\mathring{A}
to the set of types. We need to extend
\mathring{\mathtt{T}}
on
A
so that we get a correct derivation
P
typing
t
. We will call the resulting construction a
natural extension
.

First, note that
\mathtt{clev}(a)
may be defined in
A
as it was for a derivation
P
in the previous section (except that now, we only have
A
, a support candidate, instead of a whole derivation
P
): we set
a\in\mathring{A}
iff (
a\cdot 0\notin A
and there is no
a^{\prime}
such that
a=a^{\prime}\cdot 1
). Moreover, if
a\in A\setminus\mathring{A}
, then either there are unique
a^{\prime}\in\mathring{A}
and
n\geqslant 1
such that
a^{\prime}=a\cdot 0^{n}
, or there are unique
a^{\prime}\in\mathring{A}
and
n\geqslant 1
such that
a=a^{\prime}\cdot 1^{n}
. In both cases, we set
\mathring{a}=a^{\prime}
and
\mathtt{clev}(a)=n
. The notation
\mathring{a}_{\pm n}
can easily be redefined.

As we have seen in Sec.
7.1
, we must capture the way
calls
are made in a derivation by a type to other types which are deeper in the derivation.
For that, to each
a\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, we attribute an
indeterminate

X_{a}
, which shall be a placeholder for
\mathtt{T}(a)
(when
\mathtt{T}(a)
is built). Intuitively,
X_{a}
calls for
\mathtt{T}(a)
, the type given to the subterm at position
a
.
For all
a\in A,\ x\in\mathscr{V}
, we set
A_{a}(x)=\{a_{0}\in A\,|\,a\leqslant a_{0},\,t(a)=x,\nexists a_{0}^{\prime},\,a\leqslant a_{0}^{\prime}\leqslant a_{0},\,t(a^{\prime}_{0})=\lambda x\}
so that we intend to have
\mathtt{Ax}^{P}_{a}(x)=A_{a}(x)
(as well as
\mathtt{tr}^{P}(a_{0})=\lfloor a_{0}\rfloor
) when
P
is built.

Combining all the above intuitions, we set, for all
a\in A,\,x\in\mathscr{V},~\mathtt{E}(a)(x)=(\lfloor a_{0}\rfloor\cdot X_{a_{0}})_{a_{0}\in A_{a}(x)}
(thus,
\mathtt{E}(a)(x)
calls for the types given to
x
in
\mathtt{ax}
-rules above
a
). If
a\in A
is partial or unconstrained,
d=\mathtt{clev}(a)
(
i.e.
,
a=\mathring{a}\cdot 1^{d}
) and
1\leqslant i\leqslant d
, we define the sequence
\mathtt{R}_{i}(a)
by
\mathtt{R}_{i}(a)=(k\cdot X_{\mathring{a}_{d-i}\cdot k})_{k\in\mathtt{ArgTr}_{A}^{i}(a)}
with
\mathtt{ArgTr}^{i}_{A}
defined as for derivations by
\mathtt{ArgTr}^{i}_{A}=\{k\geqslant 2\,|\,a_{*}\cdot 1^{d-i}\cdot k\in A\}
. The intuition is that
\mathtt{R}_{i}(a)
calls for the types given to the argument of the
i
-th application below
a
.

•

If
a\in A
is a non zero position,
e.g.
,
t|_{a}
is of the form
\lambda x_{1}\ldots x_{d}.t|_{\mathring{a}}
. We then set:

\mathtt{Call}(a)=\mathtt{E}(a\cdot 0)(x_{1})\rightarrow\ldots\rightarrow\mathtt{E}(\mathring{a})(x_{d})\rightarrow\mathring{\mathtt{T}}(\mathring{a})

•

If
a\in A
is partial, we set:

\mathtt{Call}(a)=\mathtt{R}_{1}(a)\rightarrow\ldots\rightarrow\mathtt{R}_{n}(a)\rightarrow\mathring{\mathtt{T}}(\mathring{a})

•

If
a\in A
is unconstrained, we set
\mathtt{Call}(a)=\mathring{\mathtt{T}}(a)
.

We then extend
\mathring{\mathtt{T}}
(defined on unconstrained positions only, for the time being) to
A
by the following coinductive definition: for all
a\in A
,

\mathtt{T}(a)=\mathtt{Call}(a)[\mathtt{T}(a^{\prime})/X_{a^{\prime}}]_{a^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}

For all
a\in A
, we define the contexts
\mathtt{C}(a)
by:

\mathtt{C}(a)(x)=\mathtt{E}(a)(x)[\mathtt{T}(a^{\prime})/X_{a^{\prime}}]_{a^{\prime}\in A_{a}(x)}

Those definitions are well-founded, because whether
a
is non zero position or a partial one, every occurrence of an
X_{\alpha}
in
\mathtt{E}(a)
or
\mathtt{Call}(a)
is at depth
\geqslant 1
and the coinduction is
productive
. Finally, let
P
be the labelled tree whose support is
A
and such that, for
a\in A
,
P(a)
is
\mathtt{C}(a)\vdash t|_{a}:\mathtt{T}(a)
.

Lemma 37
.

The labelled tree
P
defined above is a derivation.

Proof.

Let
a\in A
. Whether
t(a)
is
x
,
\lambda{x}
or
\char 64\relax
, we check the associated rule has been correctly applied. Roughly, this comes from the fact that the variable
X_{a^{\prime}}
is “on the good track” (
i.e.
,
\lfloor a^{\prime}\rfloor
) in
\mathtt{E}(a)(x)
, as well as in
\mathtt{R}_{i}(a)
, thus allowing to retrieve correct typing rules.
∎

We then call the derivation
P
built above the
natural extension
of the pair
(A,\,\mathring{\mathtt{T}})
. Natural extension give all the possible quantitative derivations typing a normal form. For our purpose, they also give:

Lemma 38
.

A normal form
t
is unforgetfully typable.

Proof.

We set
A=\mathtt{supp}(t)
and
\mathring{\mathtt{T}}(a)=o
for each unconstrained position (where

o
is a type variable). Then, the extension
P
of
(A,\,\mathring{\mathtt{T}})
is an unforgetful derivation typing
t
.
∎

7.3.
Approximability of Normal Derivations

In this section, we prove that (1) for derivations typing 001-normal forms, it is enough to be quantitative to be valid and that (2) as a corollary, all 001-normal forms are approximably and unforgetfully typable:
when we truncate a derivation, we must keep all equinecessary positions. Thus, when we approximate
P
into
\,\!{}^{f}\!P
, if we keep
\mathtt{p}\in{\mathtt{bisupp}(P)}
, we must keep all the bipositions
\mathtt{p}^{\prime}
which are equinecessary with
\mathtt{p}
and we need to prove that this can be finitarily done with any
finite
subset
\,\!{}^{0}\!B
of the bisupport of a derivation typing a normal form.
To prove this, we define the rank of a position as follows:

Definition 39
.

Let
a\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
. The
rank
of
a
, denoted
\mathtt{rk}(a)
is defined by

\mathtt{rk}(a)=\max(\mathtt{ad}(a),\max(a))
.

Thus,
\mathtt{rk}(a)
bounds the width and the applicative depth of
a
.
For instance,
\mathtt{rk}(0^{6}\cdot 2^{5})=5
and
\mathtt{rk}(2\cdot 8\cdot 3)=8
.

Lemma 40
.

If
P
is a quantitative derivation typing a 001-normal form
t
, then
P
is approximable.

Proof sketch.

Let
P\rhd C\vdash t:T
be a quantitative derivation typing a 001-normal form
t
.
Let us prove that
P
is approximable. For that, we will build,
for any finite subset
\,\!{}^{0}\!B
of
{\mathtt{bisupp}(P)}
, a finite derivation
\,\!{}^{f}\!P\leqslant P
containing
\,\!{}^{0}\!B
. We will proceed as follows:

•

We choose a finite support candidate
\,\!{}^{f}\!A\subseteq A
of
t

i.e.
, we will discard all positions in
A
but finitely many.

•

Then, we choose, for each
\mathring{a}\in\,\!^{f}\!A
, a finite approximation
\,\!{}^{f}\!\mathtt{T}(\mathring{a})
of
\mathtt{T}(\mathring{a})
.

The natural extension of
(\,\!^{f}\!A,\,\!^{f}\!T)
will be a finite derivation
\,\!{}^{f}\!P\leqslant P
typing
t
such that
\,\!{}^{0}\!B\subseteq\mathtt{supp}(\,\!^{f}\!P)
.

Actually, we define
P_{n}
, the
rank
n
truncation
of
P
as follows:

•

We define
A_{n}
by discarding every position
a\in A
such that
\mathtt{ad}(a)>n
or
a
contains a track
>n
,
i.e.
, we set
A_{n}=\{a\in A\,|\,\mathtt{rk}(a)\leqslant n\}
. Since
t\in\Lambda^{001}
,
A
does not have infinite branch of finite applicative depth and thus,
A_{n}
is a
finite
set of positions.

•

For each
\mathring{a}\in\mathring{A}_{n}
, we define
\mathring{\mathtt{T}}_{n}(\mathring{a})
by discarding every
c\in\mathtt{supp}(\mathtt{T}(\mathring{a}))
such that
\mathtt{ad}(c)>n
or
c
has a track
>n
,
i.e.
, we restrict define
\mathring{\mathtt{T}}_{n}
so that

\mathtt{supp}(\mathring{\mathtt{T}}_{n}(\mathring{a}))=\{c\in\mathtt{supp}(\mathtt{T}_{n}(\mathring{a}))\,|\,\mathtt{rk}(c)\leqslant n\}
). Since
\mathtt{T}_{n}(\mathring{a})\in\mathtt{Typ}
(and not in
\mathtt{Typ}^{111}-\mathtt{Typ}^{001}
),
\mathring{\mathtt{T}}_{n}(\mathring{a})
is a
finite
type.

We define now
P_{n}
as the natural extension of
(A_{n},\,\mathring{\mathtt{T}}_{n})
. Using the quantitativity of
P
, we may prove then that, for all
\,\!{}^{0}\!B\subseteq{\mathtt{bisupp}(P)}
, there exists a large enough
n
such that
\,\!{}^{0}\!B\subseteq{\mathtt{bisupp}(P_{n})}
. The idea is the following: as we have seen, each biposition
\mathtt{p}
may “call” a chain of deeper bipositions. However, the set of bipositions called by
\mathtt{p}
is finite and we may define the
called rank

\mathtt{cr}(\mathtt{p})
of
\mathtt{p}
as the maximal applicative depth of a biposition called by
\mathtt{p}
. The called rank of
\mathtt{p}
can be understood as the maximal rank of a biposition which is equinecessary with
\mathtt{p}
. A crucial point is that (1) since
P
is quantitative and types a normal form, for all
\mathtt{p}\in{\mathtt{bisupp}(P)}
, the called rank of
\mathtt{p}
is finite (2)
P_{n}
contains all the bipositions of
P
whose called rank is
\leqslant n
(see Appendix
D
for a detailed proof of these two claims). Then, since
\,\!{}^{0}\!B
is finite, we define
n
as
\max(\{\mathtt{cr}(\mathtt{p})\,|\,\mathtt{p}\in\,\!^{0}\!B\})
and we have
\,\!{}^{0}\!B\subseteq{\mathtt{bisupp}(P_{n})}
. This proves that
P
is approximable.
∎

Lemmas
38
and
40
give immediately the following proposition:

Proposition 41
.

Every 001-normal form is approximably and unforgetfully typable in system
\mathtt{S}
.

7.4.
The Infinitary Subject Expansion Property

In Sec.
6.4
, we defined the derivation
P^{\prime}
resulting
from a productive reduction path from any (approximable or not) derivation

P
. Things do not work so smoothly for subject expansion when we try
to define a good derivation
P
which expands a derivation
P^{\prime}

typing the limit of a productive reduction path. Indeed, approximability play a central role w.r.t. expansion. Assume that:

•

t{\rightarrow_{\!\beta}^{\infty}}t^{\prime}
. Say by means of the productive reduction path
t=t_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b_{0}}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}t_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b_{1}}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}\ldots t_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b_{n}}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}t_{n+1}\rightarrow_{\!\beta}\ldots
with

b_{n}\in\{0,~1,~2\}^{*}
and
\mathtt{ad}(b_{n})\longrightarrow\infty
.

•

P^{\prime}
is an approximable derivation of
C^{\prime}\vdash t^{\prime}:\,T^{\prime}
.

•

We fix an arbitrary injection
a\mapsto\lfloor a\rfloor
from
\mathbb{N}^{*}
to
\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0,1\}
.

We want to show that there exists a derivation
P
concluding with
C^{\prime}\vdash t:T^{\prime}
. We will follow closely the ideas presented in Sec.
3.6
, but now we can implement them formally. A complete proof is given in Appendix
C.5
.

Let
\,\!{}^{f}\!P^{\prime}\leqslant P^{\prime}
be a finite approximant of
P^{\prime}
.
Since
\,\!{}^{f}\!P^{\prime}
is finite, for a large enough
n
,
t^{\prime}
can be replaced by
t_{n}
inside
\,\!{}^{f}\!P^{\prime}
, according to Lemma
33
: we set
\,\!{}^{f}\!P_{n}=\,\!^{f}\!P^{\prime}[t_{n}/t^{\prime}]
, which is a finite derivation typing
t_{n}
. But when
t_{n}
is typed instead of
t^{\prime}
, we can perform
n
steps of
\lfloor\cdot\rfloor
-expansion (starting from
\,\!{}^{f}\!P_{n}
) to obtain a finite derivation
\,\!{}^{f}\!P
typing
t
, that we denote
\mathtt{Exp}(\,\!^{f}\!P^{\prime})
(
\mathtt{Exp}(\,\!^{f}\!P^{\prime})
implicitly depends on
\lfloor\cdot\rfloor
and the productive path, but we omit them in the notation).

Notice then that the definition of
\mathtt{Exp}(\,\!^{f}\!P^{\prime})
is sound because
\mathtt{Exp}(\,\!^{f}\!P^{\prime})
does not depend on the value of
n
, as long as
n
is great enough (meaning that no reduction takes place in
\mathtt{supp}(\,\!^{f}\!P^{\prime})
after rank
n
, which ensures in particular
that
t_{k}
and
t^{\prime}
coincide on
\mathtt{supp}(\,\!^{f}\!P^{\prime})
), because, when we expand a redex in an untyped part of a derivation, it is as if we perform subject substitution.

Now, let us prove that the set
\mathscr{D}:=\{\mathtt{Exp}(\,\!^{f}\!P^{\prime})\;|\;\,\!^{f}\!P^{\prime}\in\mathtt{Approx}(P^{\prime})\}
(the set of the expansions of the finite approximations of
P^{\prime}
) is a directed set.
Assume
\,\!{}^{f}\!P^{\prime}_{1}\leqslant\,\!^{f}\!P^{\prime}_{2}\leqslant P^{\prime}
. Let
n
big enougth so that no reduction takes place in
\mathtt{supp}(\,\!^{f}\!P^{\prime}_{2})
after rank
n
.
We set
\,\!{}^{f}\!P_{i,n}:=\,\!^{f}\!P^{\prime}_{1}[t_{n}/t^{\prime}]
for
i=1,2
, so that
\mathtt{Exp}(\,\!^{f}\!P_{i})
is obtained from
\,\!{}^{f}\!P_{i,n}
after
n
steps of
\lfloor\cdot\rfloor
-expansion.
By monotonicity of uniform expansion (Lemma
21
), we have
\mathtt{Exp}(\,\!^{f}\!P_{1})\leqslant\mathtt{Exp}(\,\!^{f}\!P_{2})
. Thus,
\mathtt{Exp}
is monotonous.
Since
\mathtt{Approx}(P^{\prime})
is directed and
\mathtt{Exp}
is monotonous,
\mathscr{D}
is directed.

Then, by Theorem
3
, we define
P
as the join of the
\,\!{}^{f}\!P
when
\,\!{}^{f}\!P^{\prime}
ranges over
\mathtt{Approx}(P^{\prime})
. It is immediate to see that the context and the type concluding
P
are the same as those of
P^{\prime}
, because
\,\!{}^{f}\!P^{\prime}
and
\mathtt{Exp}(\,\!^{f}\!P^{\prime})
have the same contexts and types for all
\,\!{}^{f}\!P^{\prime}\leqslant P^{\prime}
.
This yields a derivation satisfying the desired properties.

Proposition 42
.

Assume
t{\rightarrow_{\!\beta}^{\infty}}t^{\prime}
and
P^{\prime}\rhd C^{\prime}\vdash t^{\prime}:T^{\prime}
.

If
P^{\prime}
is approximable, then there exists an approximable derivation
P
such that
P\rhd C^{\prime}\vdash t:T^{\prime}
.

Since infinitary subject reduction and expansion (for productive reduction paths) preserve unforgetful derivations, it yields our main characterization theorem :

Theorem 4

(Infinitary Normalization Theorem)
.

Let
t
be a 001-term. Then
t
has a (possibly infinite) normal form in
\Lambda^{001}
if and only if
t
is typable by means of an approximable unforgetful derivation, and if and only iff
t
is hereditarily head normalizing.

Proof.

We follow the proof scheme of Fig.
2
.

•

If
t
is typable by means of an approximable and unforgetful derivation, then
t
is HHN by Proposition
32
.

•

If
t
is HHN, then
t
is obviously 001-WN.

•

If
t
is 001-WN, let
t^{\prime}
be the 001-normal form of
t
. By Lemma
38
, there is an approximable and unforgetful derivation
P^{\prime}
concluding with
P^{\prime}\rhd C\vdash t^{\prime}:T
for some
C
and
T
. By Proposition
42
, there is an approximable derivation
P\rhd C\vdash t:T
. Since
P^{\prime}
is unforgetful,
P
also is.

∎

Remark 10
.

Theorem
4
proves that hereditary head reduction is asymptotically complete for infinitary weak normalization: it computes the 001-normal form of a 001-term, whenever there is a productive reduction path from this term to a 001-normal form.

8.
Conclusion

We have provided a type-theoretic characterization of the set of hereditary head normalizing terms (the terms whose Böhm tree does not contain
\bot
). This characterization is stated in Theorem
4
. Hereditary head normalization was proved
non
-recursive enumerable by Tatsuta
[
Tat08a
]
, and thus, impossible to characterize with an inductive type system.

To obtain this characterization, we had then to build a
coinductive
type system, system
\mathtt{S}
, based on intersection types, which ensure not only soundness (the implication: if a term is typable, then it has some expected semantic property) as in most type systems, but also completeness (the converse implication: if a term has some expected semantic property, then it is typable). More precisely, our characterization uses
non-idempotent
intersection type theory, introduced in
[
CDC80b
]
and developped on by Gardner and de Carvalho
[
Gar94
,
dC18
]
.

System
\mathtt{S}
enjoys the main properties of non-idempotent intersection type system: it quantitative (in the finite-case), syntax-directed, relevant and has a simple reduction combinatorics. Its main novel features are:

•

It is infinitary, meaning that types and derivations are
coinductively
generated.

•

It is endowed a validity condition called
approximability
. An infinitary derivation is approximable when it is the join of its finite
approximations
. Approximability is used to discard
unsound coinductive
derivations.

•

It is based on
sequence types
, which are annotated multisets allowing
tracking
and retrieving
determinism
(thus, in this respect, system
\mathtt{S}
is rigid). Sequence types are necessary to define approximability.

This contribution shows that non-idempotent intersection type theory extends to infinitary settings. The characterization presented here has been extented to obtain one of the set of
hereditary permutators

[
Via19
]
, another set of
\lambda
-terms that Tatsuta
[
Tat08b
]
proved
not
to be characterizable in an inductive way, because it also captures an asymptotic behavior.

These results the way for other semantic characterizations in other infinitary settings, in particular while considering Lévy-Longo trees and Berarducci trees, which provide semantics refining Böhm trees.

It also seems that approximability, which is defined here in terms of directed sets, should admit a categorical generalization and could help define infinitary models in a more generic way.

Contents of the appendices.

•

In Appendix
A
, we give the expansions
\Pi
and
\Pi_{n}
(typing
\mathtt{Y}_{f}
) of the derivations
\Pi^{\prime}
and its finite truncations
\Pi^{\prime}_{n}
typing
f^{\infty}
, which we studied in Sec.
3.6
.

•

In Appendix
B
, we detail the proof of Theorem
3
which states that the set of
\mathtt{S}
-derivations typing a given
t
is a directed complete semi-lattice.

•

Appendix
C
: we detail more the mechanisms of residuation and equinecessity in Sec.
C.1
, which we introduced in Sec.
C.2
. This help us prove subject reduction and expansion (without hypothesis of quantitativity, contrary to Sec.
5.5
).
In Sec.
C.3
, we prove uniform subject expansion in system
\mathtt{S}
(Proposition
18
).
In Sec.
C.6
, using equinecessity, we prove that approximabilty cannot be expressed as a predicate that pertains only to the root judgment of a derivation, as claimed in Remark
9
.

•

In Appendix
D
, we explain why every
quantitative
derivation
P
typing a
normal form
is indeed approximable. For that, we prove the two crucial claims of the proof of Lemma
40
: (1) all bipositions of such a derivation
P
have finite called rank and (2) the bipositions of rank
\leqslant n
define an approximation of
P
.

•

In Appendix
E
, we build
\mathscr{R}
, the infinitary version of system
\mathscr{R}_{0}
(Sec.
3.4
) based on intersection types as infinite multisets. This help us formally prove in Sec.
E.4
that approximability cannot be defined when intersection types are multisets (whereas Sec.
5.4
gives only suggestion why it is impossible).

Lemma
.

For all

, the derivation
\Pi_{n}
is obtained from

by
