steps of subject expansion.

The last part of this appendix is more informal. We explain how the derivation
\Pi^{\prime}
typing
f^{\infty}
can be expanded into a derivation
\Pi
typing
\mathtt{Y}_{f}
by taking the infinite reduction sequence
\mathtt{Y}_{f}\rightarrow^{\infty}f^{\infty}
backwards. The derivations
\Pi_{n}
play a key role and
\Pi
will be obtained by taking their join them. First, we observe that, for all
k<n
:

•

The type
\rho_{k}
is a truncation of
\rho_{n}
since

[\rho_{i}]_{1\leqslant i<k}
is obviously a truncation of
[\rho_{i}]_{1\leqslant i<n}
(so that
\rho_{k}=[\rho_{i}]_{1\leqslant i<k}\rightarrow o
is a truncation
\rho_{n}=[\rho_{i}]_{1\leqslant i<n}\rightarrow o
). Thus, the family
(\rho_{n})
is “directed”.

•

The derivation
\Psi_{k}
is a truncation of
\Psi_{n}
thanks to the first observation.

•

The derivation
\Pi_{k}
is a truncation of
\Pi_{n}
.

Those observations are corroborated by drawing
\rho_{n},\ \Psi_{n},\ \Pi_{n}
for small values of
n
.
We then observe that the type
\rho
coinductively defined by
\rho=[\rho]_{\omega}\rightarrow o
is the “join” of the types
\rho_{n}
(the family
(\rho_{n})
is directed and every finite truncation of
\rho
is “included” in a
\rho_{n}
for some great enough
n
).
We define

\Psi={\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 10.9477pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 47.3331pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\phantom{.}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=97.44398pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle f:[[o]\rightarrow o]\vdash f:[o]\rightarrow o$}}}}\enskip\penalty\\
\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 26.4408pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\phantom{.}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=55.65938pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x:[\rho]\vdash x:\rho$}}}}\enskip\penalty\\

Then
\Psi
is the “infinitary join” of the
\Psi_{n}
, notably because the
\rho
is the infinitary join of the
\rho_{n}
.

\Pi={\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Psi_{n}\rhd f:[[o]\rightarrow o]\vdash\Delta_{f}:\rho\hskip 14.22636pt(\Psi_{k}\rhd f:[[o]\rightarrow o]\vdash\Delta_{f}:\rho)_{\omega}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=232.16888pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 98.09665pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\mathtt{Y}_{f}:o$}}}}

We notice likewise that, intuitively,
\Pi
is the “infinitary join” of the
\Pi_{n}
, and
\Pi
conclude, as expected, with
\Gamma\vdash\mathtt{Y}_{f}:o
.

Appendix B
Lattices of (finite or not) approximations

In this Appendix, we prove Theorem
3
,
i.e.
, we show that the set of
\mathtt{S}
-derivations typing a given term
t
is a d.c.p.o.

Notation
.

Let
P
be a
\mathtt{S}
-derivation. The set of approximations of
P
is denoted
\mathtt{Approx}_{\infty}(P)
and the set of finite approximations of
P
is denoted
\mathtt{Approx}(P)
.

Definition 43
.

If
U=F\rightarrow T
, we set
\mathtt{Tl}(U)=F
and
\mathtt{Hd}(U)=T
(
tail
and
head
).

We define now approximations for types, contexts and context sequences.

Definition 44

(Approximations of context sequences)
.

•

Let
T
and
T^{\prime}
two
\mathtt{S}
-types. We write
T\leqslant T^{\prime}
if
\mathtt{supp}(T)\subseteq\mathtt{supp}(T^{\prime})
and for all
a\in\mathtt{supp}(T)
,
T(a)=T^{\prime}(a)
.

•

Let
(S_{k})_{{k\in K}}
and
(S^{\prime}_{k})_{{k\in K}^{\prime}}
. We write
(S_{k})_{{k\in K}}\leqslant(S^{\prime}_{k})_{{k\in K}^{\prime}}
if
K\subseteq K^{\prime}
and for all
k\in K
,
S_{k}\leqslant S_{k}^{\prime}
.

•

Let
C
and
C^{\prime}
two
\mathtt{S}
-contexts. We write
C\leqslant C^{\prime}
if, for all
x\in\mathscr{V}
,
C(x)\leqslant_{\infty}C^{\prime}(x)
.

•

Let
(C_{j})_{{j\in J}}
and
(C^{\prime}_{j})_{{j\in J}^{\prime}}
two context families. We write

(C_{j})_{{j\in J}}\leqslant_{\infty}(C^{\prime}_{j})_{{j\in J}^{\prime}}
if
J\subseteq J^{\prime}
and, for all
{j\in J}
,
C_{j}\leqslant_{\infty}C^{\prime}_{j}
.

We say a family
(C_{j})_{{j\in J}}
of contexts is a
compatible context family
when
\uplus_{{j\in J}}C_{j}
is defined (no track conflict).
We easily prove:

Lemma 45
.

In system
\mathtt{S}
, the sets of types, sequence types, contexts and compatible context families are c.p.o.

Moreover, to prove that the set of
\mathtt{S}
-derivations typing a same term
is also a c.p.o., we need:

Lemma 46
.

•

If
(k_{i}\cdot T_{i})_{i\in I}
is a direct family of singleton sequence types, then, for all
{i\in I}
,
k_{i}=k
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0,1\}
,
(T_{i})_{i\in I}
is directed and
\mathop{\mathtt{sup}}_{i\in I}(k_{i}\cdot T_{i})=(k\cdot\mathop{\mathtt{sup}}_{i\in I}T_{i})
.

•

Let
(T_{i})_{i\in I}
,
(T^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in I}
and
(F_{i})_{i\in I}
a directed families of
\mathtt{S}
-types, of
\mathtt{S}
-arrow types and of
\mathtt{S}
-sequence types respectively.

–

If
\forall{i\in I}
,
\mathtt{Hd}(T^{\prime}_{i})=T_{i}
, then
\mathtt{Hd}(\mathop{\mathtt{sup}}_{i\in I}T^{\prime}(i))=\mathop{\mathtt{sup}}_{i\in I}T(i)

–

If
\forall{i\in I}
,
\mathtt{Tl}(T^{\prime}_{i})=F_{i}
, then
\mathtt{Tl}(\mathop{\mathtt{sup}}_{i\in I}T^{\prime}(i))=\mathop{\mathtt{sup}}_{i\in I}F_{i}
.

•

If
(S_{i})_{i\in I}
and
(T_{i})_{i\in I}
are directed families such that
(S_{i},T_{i})\in\mathtt{PPP}_{d_{i}}
(for some
d_{i}
) for all
{i\in I}
,
S=\mathop{\mathtt{sup}}_{i\in I}S_{i}
, and
T=\mathop{\mathtt{sup}}_{i\in I}T_{i}
, then
(S,T)\in\mathtt{PPP}_{d}
for
d=\mathop{\mathtt{sup}}_{i\in I}d_{i}\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
.

•

Let
(C_{i})_{i\in I}
be a directed sequence of contexts and
x\in\mathscr{V}
. Then have
(\mathop{\mathtt{sup}}_{i\in I}C_{i})(x)=\mathop{\mathtt{sup}}_{i\in I}(C_{i}(x))
.

B.1.
Meets and Joins of Directed Derivations Families

Lemma 47
.

Let
(P_{i})_{i\in I}
be a directed (and non-empty) family of
\mathtt{S}
-derivations typing the same term
t
.

We define
P
by
{\mathtt{bisupp}(P)}=\cup_{{i\in I}}{\mathtt{bisupp}(P_{i})}
and
P(\mathtt{p})=P_{i}(\mathtt{p})
for any
{i\in I}
such that
\mathtt{p}\in{\mathtt{bisupp}(P_{i})}
(
P_{i}(\mathtt{p})
does not depend on
i
).

Then
P
is a
\mathtt{S}
-derivation and we write
P=\mathop{\mathtt{sup}}_{{i\in I}}P_{i}
.

If
I
is finite and all the
P_{i}
are, then
P
is also finite.

Proof.

We set
A_{i}=\mathtt{supp}(P_{i})
,
B_{i}={\mathtt{bisupp}(P_{i})}
for all
{i\in I}
,
A=\cup_{i\in I}A_{i}
,
B={\mathtt{bisupp}(P_{i})}
, so that
B=\mathtt{dom}(P)
.
For all
a\in A
, let
I_{a}
denote the set
\{i\in I\,|\,a\in\mathtt{supp}(P_{i})\}
. We write
\mathtt{T}^{i}
and
\mathtt{C}^{i}
instead of
\mathtt{T}^{P_{i}}
and
\mathtt{C}^{P_{i}}
and so on.

Let us check now that
P
is a correct
\mathtt{S}
-derivation (such that
A=\mathtt{supp}(P)
): this is actually enough to prove the whole statement since, if
P
is correct, any derivation
P^{\prime}\geqslant P_{i}
for all
{i\in I}
will clearly verify
P^{\prime}\geqslant P
by definition of
\leqslant
.

For all
a\in A
, let
\mathtt{C}(a)
denote the restriction of
P(a)
on
\mathscr{V}\times\mathbb{N}^{*}
and let
\mathtt{T}(a)
denote the restriction of
P
on
\mathbb{N}^{*}
.
Thus, we have
\mathtt{T}(a)=\mathop{\mathtt{sup}}_{{i\in I_{a}}}\mathtt{T}^{i}(a)
and
\mathtt{C}(a)=\mathop{\mathtt{sup}}_{{i\in I_{a}}}\mathtt{C}^{i}(a)
.
By Lemma
45
, for all
a\in A
,
\mathtt{T}(a)
and
\mathtt{C}(a)
are a
\mathtt{S}
-type and context respectively.

For any
a\in A
such that
t(a)=\char 64\relax
, we set
\mathtt{AT}(i)=\{k\geqslant 2\,|a\cdot k\in A\}
,
i.e.
,
\mathtt{AT}(i)=\cup_{{i\in I}}\mathtt{ArgTr}^{1}_{P_{i}}(a)
(
\mathtt{AT}(i)
is simply
\mathtt{ArgTr}^{1}_{P}(a)
, but this latter notation is not proved to be licit yet, as long as
P
is not proved to be a correct
\mathtt{S}
-derivation!).

We prove the correctness of
P
according to which constructor is
t(a)
, for any
a\in A
.

•

Case
t(a)=x
. Let
i,i^{\prime}\in I
such that
a\in\mathtt{supp}(P_{i})
and
P_{i}\leqslant P_{i^{\prime}}
. Thus,
P_{i}(a)
and
P_{i^{\prime}}(a)
have the form:

\begin{array}[]{c}P_{i_{0}}(a)=x:k_{i_{0}}\cdot\mathtt{T}^{i_{0}}(a)\vdash x:\mathtt{T}^{i_{0}}(a)\hskip 14.22636pt(i_{0}\in\{i,i^{\prime}\})\\
\end{array}

with
k_{i_{0}}=\mathtt{tr}^{i_{0}}(a)
and
\mathtt{C}^{i_{0}}(a)=x:k_{i}\cdot\mathtt{T}^{i_{0}}(a)
. Since
P_{i}\leqslant P_{i^{\prime}}
,
k_{i}=k_{i}^{\prime}
.

By Lemma
46
,
\mathtt{C}^{i}(a)=\mathop{\mathtt{sup}}_{{i\in I}}(\mathtt{tr}^{i}(a)\mathord{\cdot}\mathtt{T}^{i}(a))=k\cdot\tt
, where
k
is defined by
\mathtt{tr}^{i}(a)
for any
i\in I
such that
a\in\mathtt{supp}(P)
. Thus,
P(a)=x:k\mathord{\cdot}\mathtt{T}(a)\vdash x:\mathtt{T}(a)
,
i.e.
,
a
is a correct axiom node in
P
.

•

Case
t(a)=\lambda x
. Then
t|_{a}=\lambda x.u
for some
u
. Let
i,i^{\prime}\in I
such that
a\in\mathtt{supp}(P_{i})
and
P_{i}\leqslant P_{i^{\prime}}
. Thus,

\mathtt{T}^{i}(a)
is an arrow type (this implies that

\mathtt{T}^{i}(a)(0)=\rightarrow
and that
P_{i}
is an
(\mathtt{abs})
-node).

Since
P_{i}\leqslant P_{i^{\prime}}
, we also have
\mathtt{T}^{i^{\prime}}(a)(0)=\rightarrow
.
Thus,
P_{i}(a)
and
P_{i^{\prime}}(a)
have the form:

P_{i_{0}}(a)={\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 31.4048pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\mathtt{C}^{i_{0}}(a\cdot 0)\vdash u:\mathtt{T}^{i_{0}}(a\cdot 0)$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=161.292pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\mathtt{abs}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathtt{C}^{i_{0}}(a)\vdash\lambda x.u:\mathtt{C}^{{i_{0}}}(a\cdot 0)(x)\rightarrow\mathtt{T}^{{i_{0}}}(a\cdot 0)$}}}}\hskip 14.22636pt(i_{0}\in\{i,i^{\prime}\})

with
\mathtt{C}^{i_{0}}(a\cdot 0)=\mathtt{C}^{i_{0}},x:\mathtt{C}^{{i_{0}}}(a\cdot 0)
,
\mathtt{T}^{i_{0}}(a)=\mathtt{C}^{{i_{0}}}(a\cdot 0)\rightarrow\mathtt{T}^{i_{0}}(a\cdot 0)
. Since
P_{i}\leqslant P_{i^{\prime}}
, we have

\mathtt{C}^{i}(a)\leqslant\mathtt{C}^{i^{\prime}}(a)
,

\mathtt{T}^{i}(a\cdot 0)\leqslant\mathtt{T}^{i^{\prime}}(a\cdot 0)
and

\mathtt{C}^{i}(a\cdot 0))\leqslant\mathtt{C}^{i^{\prime}}(a\cdot 0)(x)
.

By Lemma
46
, the above equalities implies that
\mathtt{C}(a\cdot 0)=\mathtt{C}(a),x:\mathtt{C}(a\cdot 0)(x)
and
\mathtt{T}(a)=\mathtt{C}(a\cdot 0)\rightarrow\mathtt{T}(a\cdot 0)
. Thus,
a
is a correct
\mathtt{abs}
-node in
P
.

•

Case
t(a)=\char 64\relax
: then
t|_{a}=u\,v
for some
u
and
v
. Moreover,
P_{i}(a)
and
P_{i^{\prime}}(a)
have the form:

P_{i_{0}}(a)={\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\mathtt{C}^{i_{0}}(a\cdot 1)\vdash u:\mathtt{T}^{i_{0}}(a\cdot 1)\hskip 14.22636pt(\mathtt{C}^{i_{0}}(a\cdot k)\vdash v:\mathtt{T}^{i_{0}}(a\cdot k)\hskip 5.69046pt\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[k]})_{k\in\mathtt{ArgTr}^{1}_{P_{i_{0}}}(a)}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=290.01262pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\mathtt{app}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 104.55092pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathtt{C}^{i_{0}}(a)\vdash u\,v:\mathtt{T}^{i_{0}}(a)$}}}}

where
\mathtt{Hd}(\mathtt{T}^{i_{0}}(a\cdot 1))=\mathtt{T}^{i_{0}}(a)
,
\mathtt{Tl}(\mathtt{T}^{i_{0}}(a\cdot 1))=(k\cdot\mathtt{T}^{i_{0}}(a\cdot k))_{k\in\mathtt{ArgTr}^{1}_{P_{i_{0}}}(a)}
and
\mathtt{C}^{i_{0}}(a)=\uplus_{k\in\{1\}\cup\mathtt{ArgTr}^{1}_{P_{i_{0}}}}\mathtt{C}^{i_{0}}(a\cdot k)
. Since
P_{i}\leqslant P_{i^{\prime}}
,
\mathtt{T}^{i}(a\cdot 1)\leqslant\mathtt{T}^{i^{\prime}}(a\cdot 1)
and
(\mathtt{C}^{i}(a\cdot k))_{\{1\}\cup\mathtt{ArgTr}^{1}_{P_{i}}(a)}\leqslant(\mathtt{C}^{i^{\prime}}(a\cdot k))_{\{1\}\cup\mathtt{ArgTr}^{1}_{P_{i^{\prime}}}(a)}
.

By Lemma
46
, the above equalities implies that
\mathtt{Hd}(\mathtt{T}(a\cdot 1))=\mathtt{T}(a)
,
\mathtt{Tl}(\mathtt{T}(a\cdot 1))=\uplus_{k\in\mathtt{AT}(a)}(k\cdot\mathtt{T}(a\cdot k))
and
\mathtt{C}(a)=\uplus_{k\in\{1\}\cup\mathtt{AT}(a)}\mathtt{C}(a\cdot k)
.
Thus,
a
is a correct
\mathtt{app}
-node in
P
.

∎

Lemma 48
.

Let
(P_{i})_{i\in I}
be a non-empty family of
\mathtt{S}
-derivations typing the same term
t
, such that
\forall i,\,j\in I,~\exists P\in\mathtt{Deriv},~P_{i},\,P_{j}\leqslant_{\infty}P
.

We define
P
by
{\mathtt{bisupp}(P)}=\cap_{{i\in I}}{\mathtt{bisupp}(P_{i})}
and
P(\mathtt{p})=P_{i}(\mathtt{p})
for any
{i\in I}
(
P_{i}(\mathtt{p})
does not depend on
i
).

Then,
P
defines a correct derivation (which is finite if one of the
P_{i}
is finite). We write
P=\mathop{\mathtt{inf}}_{{i\in I}}P_{i}
.

Proof.

We prove this in the same fashion as Lemma
47
.
∎

The previous lemmas define the join and the meet of derivations (under the same derivation) as their set-theoretic union and intersection. More precisely, the statement below entails Theorem
3
:

Theorem
.

The set of
\mathtt{S}
-derivations typing a same term
t
endowed with
\leqslant_{\infty}
is a directed complete semi-lattice.

•

If
D
is a directed set of derivations typing
t
:

–

The
join

\mathop{\mathtt{sup}}D
of
D
is the function
P
defined by
\mathtt{dom}(P)=\cup_{P_{*}\in D}{\mathtt{bisupp}(P_{*})}
and
P(\mathtt{p})=\mathop{\mathtt{sup}}_{P_{*}\in D}P_{*}(\mathtt{p})
, which also is a derivation.

–

The
meet

\mathop{\mathtt{inf}}D
of
D
is the function
P
defined by
\mathtt{dom}(P)=\cap_{P_{*}\in D}{\mathtt{bisupp}(P_{*})}
and
P(\mathtt{p})=\mathop{\mathtt{inf}}_{P_{*}\in D}(\mathtt{p})
, which also is a derivation.

•

If
P
is a
\mathtt{S}_{\mathtt{hp}}
-derivation typing
t
,
\mathtt{Approx}_{\infty}(P)
is a complete lattice and
\mathtt{Approx}(P)
is a lattice.

Appendix C
Reduction and Approximability

In this Appendix, (1) we prove
Observation
24
(every biposition is equincessary with a residuable right-position), which is crucial to ensure
that approximability in system
\mathtt{S}
is stable under reduction and expansion (Lemma
6.3
) (2) we release the hypothesis of quantitativity and we prove that subject reduction and expansion are monotonic (3) we prove the infinitary subject expansion property and at last (4) we prove that approximability cannot be defined only in terms of the concluding judgment of a derivation, as announced in Remark
9
.

In this appendix, right bipositions play a particular role, so we set:

Notation
.

Let
P
be a
\mathtt{S}
-derivation. The set of
right bipositions
in
P
is denoted
\mathtt{bisupp}_{\mathtt{R}}(P)
(
i.e.
,
\mathtt{bisupp}_{\mathtt{R}}(P)={\mathtt{bisupp}(P)}\cap\mathbb{N}^{*}\times\mathbb{N}^{*}
).

C.1.
Quasi-Residuation in System
\mathtt{S}

In this section, we define the notion of quasi-residuation in system
\mathtt{S}
. This extends Sec.
5.3
.

Hypotheses.

For the remainder of this section, we assume that
P\rhd C\vdash t:T
is a
\mathtt{S}
-derivation and
t|_{b}=(\lambda x.r)s
,
t\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}t^{\prime}
, so that
t^{\prime}|_{b}=r[s/x]
. We follow Fig.
12
and we reuse some notations of Sec.
5.3
,
5.4
and Sec.
5.5
.
We set, for all
a\in\mathtt{supp}(P)
,
\mathtt{X}(a)=\{\mathtt{tr}^{P}(\alpha)~|~\alpha\in\mathtt{Ax}^{P}_{a\cdot 10}(x)\}
. We have
\mathtt{X}(a)\subseteq\mathtt{Tr}^{\lambda}_{P}(a)
, and if
P
is quantitative,
\mathtt{X}(a)=\mathtt{Tr}^{\lambda}_{P}(a)
holds.

For all
k\in\mathtt{X}(a)
, we also denote by
a_{k}
the unique
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
such that
a\cdot 10\cdot\alpha\in\mathtt{Ax}^{\lambda}(a)
and
\mathtt{tr}^{P}(a\cdot 10\cdot\alpha)=k
,
i.e.
,
a\cdot 10\cdot a_{k}
is the unique axiom assigning track
k
to
x
above
a
(note that
a_{k}
implicitly depends on
P
).

Residuation

Let
\alpha\in\mathtt{supp}(P)
, we define
\mathtt{Res}_{b}(\alpha)
case-wise.

•

If
\overline{\alpha}\not\geqslant b
,
\mathtt{Res}_{b}(\alpha)=\{\alpha\}
.

•

If
\alpha=a\cdot 1\cdot 0\cdot\alpha_{0}
for some
a\in\mathtt{Rep}_{P}(b)
and
t(a)\neq x
,
\mathtt{Res}_{b}(a)=a\cdot\alpha_{0}

•

If
\alpha=a\cdot k\cdot\alpha_{0}
for some
a\in\mathtt{Rep}_{P}(b)
and
k\in\mathtt{X}(a)
, then
\mathtt{Res}_{b}(\alpha)=a\cdot a_{k}\cdot\alpha_{0}
.

•

In any other case,
\mathtt{Res}_{b}(a)
is not defined.

Right bipositions.
Let
(\alpha,\gamma)\in\mathtt{supp}(P)
. If
\alpha^{\prime}=\mathtt{Res}_{b}(\alpha)
is defined, then we set
\mathtt{Res}_{b}(\alpha,\gamma)=(\alpha^{\prime},\gamma)
. In any other case,
\mathtt{Res}_{b}(\alpha,\gamma)
is not defined.

Quasi-Residuation

Let
\alpha\in\mathtt{supp}(P)
. We define
\mathtt{QRes}_{b}(\alpha)
, the
quasi-residual
of
\alpha
.

•

Extension of
\mathtt{Res}_{b}
:
if
\mathtt{Res}_{b}(\alpha)
is defined, then
\mathtt{QRes}_{b}(\alpha)=\mathtt{Res}_{b}(\alpha)
.

•

Variable of the redex:
if
\alpha=a\cdot 10\cdot a_{k}
for some
a\in\mathtt{Rep}_{P}(b)
and
k\in\mathtt{X}(a)
, then
\mathtt{QRes}_{b}(\alpha)=a\cdot a_{k}
.

•

Root of the redex:
if
\alpha=a\in\mathtt{Rep}_{P}(b)
, then
\mathtt{QRes}_{b}(\alpha)=\alpha
.

Thus, the only case in which
\mathtt{QRes}_{b}(\alpha)
is not defined is when
\overline{\alpha}=b\cdot 1
.

Right bipositions.
Let
(\alpha,\gamma)\in\mathtt{supp}(P)
, we define
\mathtt{QRes}_{b}(\alpha,\gamma)
case-wise:

•

If
\alpha^{\prime}=\mathtt{QRes}_{b}(\alpha)
is defined, then
\mathtt{QRes}_{b}(\alpha,\gamma)=(\alpha^{\prime},\gamma)
.

•

Abstraction of the redex:
If
\alpha=a\cdot 1
for some
a\in\mathtt{Rep}_{P}(b)
(
i.e.
,
t(\alpha)=\lambda x
):

–

if
\gamma=1\cdot\gamma_{0}
for some
\gamma_{0}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, then
\mathtt{QRes}_{b}(a\cdot 1,\gamma)=(a,\gamma_{0})
.

–

If
\gamma=k\cdot\gamma_{0}
for some
k\geqslant 2
and
\gamma_{0}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, then
k\in\mathtt{K}(a)
and
\mathtt{QRes}_{b}(a\cdot 1,\gamma)=(a\cdot a_{k},\gamma)
.

–

If
\gamma=\varepsilon
, then
\mathtt{QRes}_{b}(a\cdot 1,\varepsilon)=(a,\varepsilon)
.

Note that
\mathtt{Res}_{b}
and
\mathtt{QRes}_{b}
implicitly depend on
P
in the argument of the redex, since the mapping of (bi)positions in the argument derivations typing
s
depend on axiom tracks inside
P
, but we omit
P
in the notation since there is

First properties

We observe by case-analysis that
\mathtt{Res}_{b}
is injective. For all
\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathtt{Res}_{b}(\mathtt{supp}(P))
(where
\alpha=\mathtt{Res}^{-1}_{b}(\alpha)
), we set
\mathtt{T}^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime})=\mathtt{T}(\alpha)
and

\mathtt{C}^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime})=\mathtt{C}(\alpha)
if
\overline{\alpha}\ngtr b\cdot 10
and

\mathtt{C}^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime})=\mathtt{C}(\mathtt{Res}_{b}(\alpha^{\prime}))\setminus x)\uplus(\uplus_{k\in\mathtt{K}(\alpha)}\mathtt{C}(a\cdot k))
if
\overline{\alpha}\geqslant b\cdot 1
,
\mathtt{K}(\alpha)=\mathtt{Rt}(\mathtt{C}(\alpha)(x))
and
a
is the unique prefix of
\alpha
such that
\overline{a}=b
. This generalizes the construction of Sec.
5.5
.
We define
P^{\prime}:=\mathtt{Red}_{b}(P)
(and we write
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}P^{\prime}
) as the tree labelled with
\mathtt{S}
-judgments by
\mathtt{supp}(P^{\prime})=\mathtt{Res}_{b}(\mathtt{supp}(P))
and for all
\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathtt{supp}(P^{\prime})
,
P^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime})=\mathtt{C}^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime})\vdash t^{\prime}|_{\alpha^{\prime}}:\mathtt{T}^{\prime}(\alpha)
.

Quasi-residuation preserves types. Residuation preserves constructors:

Lemma 49
.

Assume
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}P^{\prime}
. Let
\alpha\in\mathtt{supp}(P)

•

If
\alpha^{\prime}=\mathtt{Res}_{b}(\alpha)
is defined, then
t^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime})=t(\alpha)
.

•

If
\alpha^{\prime}=\mathtt{QRes}_{b}(\alpha)
is defined, then
\mathtt{T}^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime})=\mathtt{T}(\alpha)
.

Lemma
49
is pivotal to prove:

Lemma 50

(Subject Reduction)
.

Assume
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}P^{\prime}
. Then the labelled tree
P^{\prime}
is a correct
\mathtt{S}
-derivation.

Proof.

We reason by case analysis to prove that each node of
P^{\prime}
is a correct typing rule of system
\mathtt{S}
, using Lemma
49
and the definition of
\mathtt{C}^{\prime}
. For
\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathtt{supp}(P^{\prime})
, a special care must be given when
\alpha^{\prime}
or one of its children corresponds to
b
or an occurrence of
s
.
∎

Lemma 51
.

Assume that
P
is a
quantitative

\mathtt{S}
-derivation and
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}P^{\prime}
.

•

\mathtt{Res}_{b}
is a bijection from a subset of
\mathtt{bisupp}_{\mathtt{R}}(P)
to
\mathtt{bisupp}_{\mathtt{R}}(P^{\prime})
.

•

\mathtt{QRes}_{b}
is a total surjective function from
\mathtt{bisupp}_{\mathtt{R}}(P)
to
\mathtt{bisupp}_{\mathtt{R}}(P^{\prime})
. Moreover, for all
\mathtt{p}^{\prime}\in{\mathtt{bisupp}(P^{\prime})}
,
p
has at most six antecedents by
\mathtt{QRes}_{b}

In short, the last point of Lemma
51
holds, because
\mathtt{QRes}_{b}(\alpha,\gamma)
is defined in 6 cases, each one being injective. A derivation typing a redex of the form
(\lambda x.x)s
shows that this number may be reached.

Remark 11
.

Note that, for all
\mathtt{p}\in\mathtt{bisupp}_{\mathtt{R}}(P)
and
\mathtt{p}^{\prime}:=\mathtt{QRes}_{b}(\mathtt{p})
, we have
\mathtt{P}(\mathtt{p})=\mathtt{P}^{\prime}(\mathtt{p}^{\prime})
except maybe when
\mathtt{p}=(a\cdot 1,\varepsilon)
for some
a\in\mathtt{Rep}_{P^{\prime}}(b)
.

Monotonicity.
Observe that
P_{1}\leqslant_{\infty}P_{2}
iff
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
type the same term,
\mathtt{supp}(P_{1})\leqslant_{\infty}\mathtt{supp}(P_{2})
and for all
a\in\mathtt{supp}(P_{1})
,
\mathtt{C}_{1}(a)\leqslant_{\infty}\mathtt{C}_{2}(a)
(Definition
44
) and
\mathtt{T}_{1}(a)\leqslant_{\infty}\mathtt{T}_{2}(a)
.
In particular, if
P_{1}\leqslant_{\infty}P_{2}
type
t
,
t\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}t^{\prime}
, then
\mathtt{Res}_{b}(\mathtt{supp}(P_{1}))\subseteq\mathtt{Res}_{b}(\mathtt{supp}(P_{2}))
and
\mathtt{T}^{\prime}_{1}(\mathtt{Res}_{b}(\alpha))=\mathtt{T}_{1}(\alpha)\leqslant_{\infty}\mathtt{T}_{2}(\alpha)=\mathtt{T}^{\prime}_{2}(\mathtt{Res}_{b}(\alpha^{\prime}))
. Last,
\mathtt{C}^{\prime}_{i}(\alpha^{\prime})(y)=(\mathtt{tr}(^{\prime})_{i}(\alpha^{\prime}_{0})\cdot\mathtt{T}^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime}_{0}))_{\alpha^{\prime}_{0}\in\mathtt{Ax}^{P_{i}}_{(}\alpha^{\prime})(y)}
. Since
\mathtt{Ax}^{P_{1}}_{\alpha^{\prime}}(y)\subseteq\mathtt{Ax}^{P_{2}}_{\alpha^{\prime}}(y)
for all
\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathtt{supp}(P^{\prime}_{1})
(because
\mathtt{supp}(P_{1})\subseteq\mathtt{supp}(P_{2})
), we have
\mathtt{C}^{\prime}_{1}(\alpha^{\prime})(y)\leqslant_{\infty}\mathtt{C}^{\prime}_{2}(\alpha^{\prime})(y)
for all
\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathtt{supp}(P^{\prime}_{1})
and
y\in\mathscr{V}
. Thus,
\mathtt{Res}_{b}
is monotonic on the set of
\mathtt{S}
-derivations typing
t
.

C.2.
Equinecessary bipositions

In this section, we use the notion of
equinecessity
to prove that we can forget about left bipositions while working with approximability, as we suggested in the beginning of Sec.
C
.

Definition 52
.

Let
P
a
quantitative

\mathtt{S}
-derivation and
\mathtt{p}_{1},\,\mathtt{p}_{2}
two bipositions of
P
.

•

We say
\mathtt{p}_{1}

subjugates

\mathtt{p}_{2}
if, for all
P_{*}\leqslant_{\infty}P
,
\mathtt{p}_{1}\in P_{*}
implies
\mathtt{p}_{2}\in P_{*}
.

•

We say
\mathtt{p}_{1}
and
\mathtt{p}_{2}
are
equinecessary
(written
\mathtt{p}_{1}\leftrightarrow\mathtt{p}_{2}
) if, for all
P_{*}\leqslant_{\infty}P
,
\mathtt{p}_{1}\in P_{*}
iff
\mathtt{p}_{2}\in P_{*}
.

•

Let
B_{1},B_{2}\subseteq{\mathtt{bisupp}(P)}
. We also write
B_{1}\leftrightarrow B_{2}
if, for all
P_{*}\leqslant_{\infty}P
,
B_{1}\subseteq{\mathtt{bisupp}(P)}
iff
B_{2}\subseteq{\mathtt{bisupp}(P)}
.

Note that subjugation and equinecessity are implicitly defined w.r.t.
P
.
There are many elementary equinecessity cases that are easy to observe.
We need only a few ones and we define
\mathtt{asc}(\mathtt{p})
and
\mathtt{Asc}(\mathtt{p})
(standing for “ascendance”)
so that
\mathtt{p}\leftrightarrow\mathtt{asc}(\mathtt{p})
and
\mathtt{p}\leftrightarrow\mathtt{Asc}(\mathtt{p})
for all
\mathtt{p}\in{\mathtt{bisupp}(P)}
.

•

\mathtt{asc}(\mathtt{p})
is defined for any
\mathtt{p}\in{\mathtt{bisupp}(P)}
which
is not in an axiom leaf.

–

Left bipositions:

\mathtt{asc}(a,\,x,\,k\cdot c)=(a\cdot\ell,\,x,\,k\cdot c)
, where

\ell\geqslant 0
is the unique integer such that
(a\cdot\ell,\,x,\,k\cdot c)\in{\mathtt{bisupp}(P)}
.

–

Right bipositions (
\mathtt{abs}
):

if
t(\overline{a})=\lambda x
,
\mathtt{asc}(a,\,\varepsilon)=(a\cdot 0,\,\varepsilon),~\mathtt{asc}(a,\,1\cdot c)=(a\cdot 0,\,c)
and
\mathtt{asc}(a,\,k\cdot c)=(a\cdot 0,\,x,\,k\cdot c)
if
k\geqslant 2
.

–

Right bipositions (
\mathtt{app}
)
: if
t(\overline{a})=\char 64\relax
,
\mathtt{asc}(a,\,c)=(a\cdot 1,\,1\cdot c)
.

•

\mathtt{Asc}(\mathtt{p})
is a
right biposition
and is defined as the highest right biposition related to
\mathtt{p}
by
\mathtt{asc}
.

–

Right bipositions:
if
\mathtt{p}=(a,c)
, let
h
be maximal such that
\mathtt{asc}^{h}(\mathtt{p})
is defined. In that case, we set
\mathtt{Asc}(\mathtt{p})=\mathtt{asc}^{h}(\mathtt{p})
.

–

Left bipositions:
if
\mathtt{p}=(a,x,k\cdot c)
, let
h
be maximal (if it exists) such that
\mathtt{asc}^{h}(\mathtt{p})
exists. In that case,
\mathtt{asc}^{h}(\mathtt{p})
is of the form
(a_{0},x,k\cdot c)
with
t(a_{0})=x
. We set then
\mathtt{Asc}(\mathtt{p})=(a_{0},c)
.

Since
t\in\Lambda^{001}
(and not in
\Lambda^{111}\setminus\Lambda^{001}
),
\mathtt{Asc}(\mathtt{p})
is defined for any right biposition
\mathtt{p}
. If
P
is quantitative, then
\mathtt{Asc}(\mathtt{p})
is also defined for any left biposition.

An examination of the
\mathtt{app}
-rule shows that, if
t(a)=\char 64\relax
, for all
k\geqslant 2
and
c\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
:

(a\cdot 1,k\cdot c)\leftrightarrow(a\cdot k,c)\hskip 28.45274pt(E1)

Indeed, if
t|_{a}=u\,v
is typed—say that
u:(k\cdot S_{k})_{{k\in K}}\rightarrow T
and
(v:S_{k}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[k]})_{k\in K}
—the domain of the arrow typing
u
correspond to the types assigned to the argument
u
(if
c\in\mathtt{supp}(S_{k})
, then
(a\cdot 1,k\cdot c)
and
(a\cdot k,c)
are both in
{\mathtt{bisupp}(P)}
).

Now, assume that
P
is still quantitative,
t|_{b}=(\lambda x.r)s
,
a\in{\mathtt{bisupp}(P)}
,
\overline{a}=b
,
k\in\mathtt{Tr}^{\lambda}_{P}(a)
. Thus,
t|_{a\cdot 1}=\lambda x.r:(k\cdot S_{k})_{{k\in K}}\rightarrow T
and
(s:S_{k}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[k]})_{k\in K}
.
Let
c\in\mathtt{supp}(S_{k})
and
\mathtt{p}:=(a\cdot 1,k\cdot c)\in{\mathtt{bisupp}(P)}
. Notice that
\mathtt{asc}{p}=(a\cdot 10,x,k\cdot c)
and
\mathtt{Asc}(\mathtt{p})=(a\cdot 10\cdot a_{k},c)
. Thus:

(a\cdot 1,k\cdot c)\leftrightarrow(a\cdot 10\cdot a_{k},c)\hskip 28.45274pt(E2)

In particular, by
(E1)
and
(E2)
:

(a\cdot 10\cdot a_{k},\,c)\leftrightarrow(a\cdot k,\,c)

This relation specifies that the types of the occurrences of
x
match the types of the argument
s
of the redex,
i.e.
, if
c\in\mathtt{supp}(S_{k})
, both
(a\cdot k,c)
(pointing in
s:S_{k}
) and
(a\cdot 10\cdot a_{k},c)
(pointing in
x:S_{k}
) are in
{\mathtt{bisupp}(P)}
.

Lemma 53
.

Assume that
P
is quantitative,
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}P^{\prime}
,
t|_{a}=(\lambda x.r)s
and

\mathtt{p}\in{\mathtt{bisupp}(P)}
. If
\mathtt{Res}_{b}(\mathtt{p})
is not defined, one of the following cases occur:

•

If
\mathtt{p}
is a left-biposition
(\alpha,y,k\cdot c)
with
\mathtt{Asc}(\mathtt{p})=:(\alpha_{*},c)
:

–

If
y\neq x
, then
\mathtt{p}\leftrightarrow\mathtt{p}_{0}:=\mathtt{Asc}(\mathtt{p})
and
\mathtt{Res}_{b}(\mathtt{p}_{0})
is defined.

–

If
y=x
, then
\alpha_{*}=a\cdot 10\cdot a_{k}
for some
a
and
k
such that
\overline{a}=b
and
k\in\mathtt{Tr}^{\lambda}_{P}(a)
.
Then

\mathtt{p}\leftrightarrow\mathtt{p}_{0}:=(a\cdot 1,k\cdot c)
by
(E3)
and
\mathtt{Res}_{b}(\mathtt{p}_{0})
is defined.

•

If
\mathtt{p}
is a right-biposition
(\alpha,c)
with
\mathtt{Asc}(\mathtt{p})=:(\alpha_{*},c_{*})
, then
\alpha=a\cdot 10\cdot\alpha_{0}
for some
a
such that
\overline{a}=b
:

–

If
t|_{a_{*}}=y\neq x
, then
\mathtt{p}\leftrightarrow\mathtt{p}_{0}:=\mathtt{Asc}(\mathtt{p})
and
\mathtt{Res}_{b}(\mathtt{p}_{0})
is defined.

–

If
t|_{a_{*}}=x
, let
k:=\mathtt{tr}^{P}(a_{*})
and
\mathtt{p}_{0}:=(a\cdot k,c_{*})
. Then
\mathtt{p}\leftrightarrow\mathtt{p}_{0}
and
\mathtt{Res}_{b}(\mathtt{p}_{0})
is defined by
(E3)
.

Proof.

The cases resorting to
(E3)
are justified in the statement. Let us check that we are exhaustive.
Notice first that, when
\mathtt{p}
is a left-biposition,
\mathtt{Asc}(\mathtt{p})
is always defined since
P
is quantitative. Moreover, when
(\alpha_{*},c):=\mathtt{Asc}(\mathtt{p})
, then
t(\alpha_{*})=y
for some
y\in\mathscr{V}
(
\alpha_{*}
points to an
\mathtt{ax}
-rule).

•

If
y\neq x
,
\mathtt{Res}_{b}(\alpha_{*})
and thus
\mathtt{Res}_{b}(\alpha_{*},c)
are both defined.

•

If
y=x
,
\mathtt{Asc}(\mathtt{p})
is of the form
(a\cdot 10\cdot a_{k},c)
for some
a
satisfying
\overline{a}=b
. But, by
(E3)
,
\mathtt{Asc}(\mathtt{p})
is equinecessary to
(a\cdot k,c)
(pointing inside
s
), which always has a residual by definition of
\mathtt{Res}_{b}
.

∎

One then notices that Observation
25
, which is crucial to prove correctness (Lemma
6.3
),
is a corollary of Lemma
53
.

C.3.
Uniform Subject Expansion

We prove now the
uniform
subject expansion property for system
\mathtt{S}
. By
uniform
, we mean that we use a function
\lfloor\cdot\rfloor:\mathbb{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}^{*}
to decide the value of the axiom tracks in the axiom rules created during anti-reduction. Uniformity is crucial to ensure that subject expansion is monotonic.

Assuming
t|_{b}=(\lambda x.r)s
and
t\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}t^{\prime}
, we will built a derivation
P
typing
t
from a derivation
P^{\prime}
typing
t^{\prime}
. The constructions is based on the following observation: there are three kinds of positions in
t^{\prime}
: (1) positions outside the reduct
r[s/x]
(2) positions inside
r
(but outside an occurrence of
s
) (3) positions inside an occurrence of
s
.
Things are a bit less clear in
t
: we have (1) positions outside the redex
(\lambda x.r)s
(2) positions inside
r
(3) positions inside
s
(4) positions corresponding to the application or the abstraction of the redex
(\lambda x.r)s
. Moreover, inside
r
, one should distinguish positions corresponding to
x
(which is destroyed during reduction) and the other positions. All this has been illustrated with Fig.
12
.

Hypotheses.
Let
P^{\prime}\rhd\Gamma\vdash t^{\prime}:\tau
a quantitative
\mathtt{S}
derivation with
t^{\prime}|_{b}=r[s/x]
and
t
the term such that
t|_{b}=(\lambda x.r)s
and
t\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}t^{\prime}
.
Let
\lfloor\cdot\rfloor
be an injective function from
\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0,1\}
to
\mathtt{N}^{*}
.
We set
A^{\prime}=\mathtt{supp}(P^{\prime})
.

Building the support of
P
.
We first build
A:=\mathtt{supp}(P)
and a quasi-residuation function
QR_{b}
from
A
to
A^{\prime}
.

We write
\mathtt{T}^{\prime}
,
\mathtt{C}^{\prime}
and
\mathtt{tr}^{\prime}
for
\mathtt{T}^{P^{\prime}}
,
\mathtt{C}^{P^{\prime}}
and
\mathtt{tr}^{P^{\prime}}
. We set
B=\mathtt{supp}(t)
,
B^{\prime}=\mathtt{supp}(t^{\prime})
and
A^{\prime}=\mathtt{supp}(P)
. Let
B_{x}=\{\beta\in B\,|\,t(\beta)=x\}
(we assume Barendregt convention),
B^{\prime}_{s\varepsilon}=\mathtt{Res}_{b}(B)
,
B^{\prime}_{s}={\beta^{\prime}_{0}\cdot\beta^{\prime}_{1}\in B^{\prime}\,|\,\beta^{\prime}_{0}\in B^{\prime}_{s\varepsilon}}
and
A_{b}=\mathtt{Rep}_{P^{\prime}}(b)
,

A^{\prime}_{s\varepsilon}=\{\alpha^{\prime}\in A^{\prime}\,|\,\overline{\alpha^{\prime}}\in B^{\prime}_{s\varepsilon}\}
and
A^{\prime}_{s}=\{\alpha^{\prime}\in A^{\prime}\,|\,\overline{\alpha^{\prime}}\in B^{\prime}_{s}\}
. Thus,
A^{\prime}_{s\varepsilon}
(resp.
A^{\prime}_{s}
) is the set of positions (in
P^{\prime}
) of the occurrences of
s
(resp. inside an occurrence of
s
) resulting from substitution We set
A^{\prime}_{s\mathtt{i}}=A^{\prime}_{s}\setminus A_{s\varepsilon}
for the set of positions
internal
to an occurrence of
s
.

Outside the redex and inside
r
.

First, we set
A^{0}_{b}=\{\alpha^{\prime}\in A^{\prime}\,|\,\overline{\alpha^{\prime}}\not\geqslant b\}
,
A^{\prime}_{r}=\{al^{\prime}\in A^{\prime}\,|\,\overline{\alpha^{\prime}}\geqslant b,\,\overline{\alpha^{\prime}}\notin A^{\prime}_{s\mathtt{i}}\}
(note that we have
\overline{\alpha^{\prime}_{0}}\in\mathtt{supp}(r)
)
and
A_{r}=\{a\cdot 10\cdot\alpha^{\prime}_{0}\,|\,a\in\mathtt{Rep}_{P^{\prime}}(b),\,\alpha^{\prime}_{0}\in\mathbb{N}^{*},\,a\cdot\alpha^{\prime}_{0}\in A^{\prime}_{r}\}
.
Thus,
A^{0}_{b}
is the set of position outside the reduct in
t^{\prime}
and

A_{r}
corresponds to the set of positions inside the subterm
r
in
t
.
We define
QR_{b}
as the identity on
A^{0}_{b}
and by
QR_{b}(a\cdot 10\cdot\alpha^{\prime}_{0})=a\cdot\alpha^{\prime}_{0}
from
A_{r}
to
A^{\prime}_{r}
.

We split
A_{r}
in two: let
A^{x}_{b}=\{a\cdot 10\cdot\alpha^{\prime}_{0}\,|\,a\in\mathtt{Rep}_{P^{\prime}}(b)\,a\cdot\alpha^{\prime}_{0}\in A^{\prime}_{s\varepsilon}\}
and
A^{1}_{b}=A_{r}\setminus A^{x}_{b}
. Intuitively,
A^{x}_{b}
corresponds to the occurrences of
x
in
t
(which are in
r
) whereas
A^{1}_{b}
corresponds to the other positions inside the subterm
r
in
t
.

Inside
s
.

We set

A_{s}=\{a\cdot\lfloor a\cdot 10\cdot\alpha^{\prime}_{0}\rfloor\cdot\alpha_{1}\,|\,a\in\mathtt{Rep}_{P^{\prime}}(b),\,a\cdot\alpha_{0}\in A^{\prime}_{s\varepsilon},\,a\cdot\alpha^{\prime}_{0}\cdot\alpha_{1}\in A^{\prime}\}
. Intuitively,
A_{s}
will correspond to the position of
s
in
P
.
Indeed,
a\cdot\alpha^{\prime}_{0}
(resp.
a\cdot 10\cdot\alpha^{\prime}_{0}
) corresponds to an occurrence of
s
in
t^{\prime}
(resp. of
x
in
t
) whereas
a
points to an
\mathtt{app}
-rule typing the application of the redex
(\lambda x.r)s
below.
As we explained above, the natural number
k:=\lfloor a\cdot 10\cdot\alpha_{0}\rfloor
gives us the axiom track that the occurrence of
x
at position
a\cdot 10\cdot\alpha_{0}
should be assigned while typing
t
. Then, we should add an argument at
\mathtt{app}
-node
a
on track
k
. Hence,
a\cdot\lfloor a\cdot\alpha_{0}\rfloor
.
We define
QR_{b}
from
A_{s}
to
A^{\prime}_{s}
by mapping
a\cdot\lfloor a\cdot 10\cdot\alpha^{\prime}_{0}\rfloor\cdot\alpha_{1}
on
a\cdot\alpha^{\prime}_{0}\cdot\alpha_{1}
(note that
a\cdot\alpha^{\prime}_{0}\in A^{\prime}_{s}
). Observe that this induces a bijection from
A_{s}
to
A^{\prime}_{s}
.

We may now define
A
, which will be the support of
P
, as a disjoint union:

A:=A^{0}_{b}\cup A^{x}_{b}\cup A^{1}_{b}\cup A_{s}\cup\mathtt{Rep}_{P^{\prime}}(b)\cup\mathtt{Rep}_{P^{\prime}}(b)\cdot 1

We define
QR_{b}
on
\mathtt{Rep}_{P^{\prime}}(b)
as the identity and we leave it undefined on
\mathtt{Rep}_{P^{\prime}}(b)\cdot 1
(which corresponds to the abstraction
\lambda x.r
). Since
A^{\prime}=A^{0}_{b}\cup A^{\prime}_{r}\cup A^{\prime}_{s}
, we have that
QR_{b}
is a function from

A\setminus\mathtt{Rep}_{P^{\prime}}(b)\cdot 1
to
A^{\prime}
.

Building types and contexts.
We set, for all
\alpha\in A\setminus\mathtt{Rep}_{P^{\prime}}(b)\cdot 1
,
\mathtt{T}(\alpha)=\mathtt{T}^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime})
with
\alpha^{\prime}=QR_{b}(\alpha)
.
If
a\in\mathtt{Rep}_{P^{\prime}}(b)
, we set
\mathtt{T}(a\cdot 1)=\mathtt{T}(a\cdot k)_{k\in K}\rightarrow\mathtt{T}(a)
where
K=\{k\geqslant 2\,|\,a\cdot k\in A\}
.

In order to define the contexts in
P
, we must observe that, in
r
(inside
(\lambda x.r)s
),
x
is a placeholder for occurrence of
s
.

Since
P^{\prime}
is quantitative, we have, for all
\alpha^{\prime}\in A^{\prime}
,
y\in\mathscr{V}
,
\mathtt{C}^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime})(y)=(\mathtt{tr}^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime}_{0})\cdot\mathtt{T}^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime}_{0}))_{\alpha^{\prime}_{0}\in A^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime},y)}
where
A^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime},y):=\mathtt{Ax}^{P^{\prime}}_{\alpha^{\prime}}(y)
.

Let
\alpha\in A_{r}
and
\alpha^{\prime}:=QR_{b}(\alpha)
. For all
y\in\mathscr{V}
,
we set
A^{\prime}_{0}(\alpha^{\prime},y):=A^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime},y)\setminus A^{\prime}_{s}
, the set of positions of axioms typing
y
(in
P^{\prime}
) above
\alpha^{\prime}
which are not in any occurrence of
s
.
We set
A(x,\alpha)=\{\alpha_{0}\in A^{x}_{b}\,|\,\alpha_{0}\geqslant\alpha\}
(the positions of
x
in
A
above
\alpha
). If
\alpha_{0}\in A(x,\alpha)
, then the axiom rule typing
x
at position
\alpha_{0}
is a placeholder for a typed occurrence of
s
at position
QR_{b}(\alpha_{0})
in
P^{\prime}
.
We set
A^{\prime}_{x}(\alpha^{\prime},y):=\{\alpha^{\prime}_{0}\in A^{\prime}_{s\varepsilon}\,|\,\alpha^{\prime}_{0}\geqslant\alpha^{\prime}\}
, which is the set of positions in
P^{\prime}
pointing to occurrences of
s
above
\alpha^{\prime}
.
We then define
\mathtt{C}(\alpha)
case-wise:

•

If
\alpha\in A_{r}
, then
\mathtt{C}(\alpha)=(\mathtt{tr}^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime}_{0})\cdot\mathtt{T}^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime}_{0}))_{\alpha^{\prime}_{0}\in A^{\prime}_{0}(al^{\prime},y)}
. for all
y\neq x
and
\mathtt{C}(\alpha)(x)=(\lfloor\alpha_{0}\rfloor\cdot\mathtt{T}^{\prime}(QR_{b}(\alpha_{0})))_{\alpha_{0}\in A(\alpha,x)}
. This latter definition is correct since
\lfloor\cdot\rfloor
is injective.

•

If
\overline{\alpha}\not\geqslant b
(
i.e.
,
\alpha\in A^{0}_{b}
) or
\alpha\in A_{s}
, then
\mathtt{C}(\alpha)=\mathtt{C}^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime})

•

If
\alpha=a\cdot 1
for some
a\in\mathtt{Rep}_{P^{\prime}}(b)
, then we set
\mathtt{C}(\alpha)=(\mathtt{tr}^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime}_{0})\cdot\mathtt{T}^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime}_{0}))_{\alpha^{\prime}_{0}\in A^{\prime}_{0}(al^{\prime},y)}
. for all
y\neq x
and
\mathtt{C}(\alpha)(x)=(\,)

We define
\mathtt{Exp}_{b}(P^{\prime},\lfloor\cdot\rfloor,t)
as the tree
P
labelled with
\mathtt{S}
-judgments such that
\mathtt{supp}(P)=A
and for all
\alpha\in A
,
P(\alpha)=\mathtt{C}(\alpha)\vdash t|_{\alpha}:\mathtt{T}(\alpha)
.
For instance, the construction of
A_{r}
depends on
t
. The construction of
A_{s}
depends on
\lfloor\cdot\rfloor
.

We have, for all
\alpha\in A\setminus\mathtt{Rep}_{P^{\prime}}(b)
and
\alpha^{\prime}=QR_{b}(\alpha)
:

\mathtt{T}(\alpha)=\mathtt{T}^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime})\hskip 14.22636pt\hskip 14.22636pt\text{(type preservation)}

Correction and monotonicity.

Let
\alpha\in A
. We set
\mathtt{Ch}(\alpha):=\{\alpha\cdot k\in A\,|\,k\in\mathbb{N}\}
(
\mathtt{Ch}
stands for “children”). Thus,
\mathtt{Ch}(\alpha)
corresponds to the premises of
\alpha
in
A
.
Let
\alpha^{\prime}\in A^{\prime}
. We set likewise
\mathtt{Ch}^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime}):=\{\alpha^{\prime}\cdot k\in A^{\prime}\,|\,k\in\mathbb{N}\}
. A case analysis shows that, if
\alpha\in A^{0}_{b}\cup A^{1}_{b}\cup A_{s}
and
\alpha^{\prime}=QR_{b}(\alpha)
, then
QR_{b}
induces a bijection from
\mathtt{Ch}(\alpha)
to
\mathtt{Ch}^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime})
. By type preservation, this implies that for all
\alpha\in A^{0}_{b}\cup A^{1}_{b}\cup A_{s}
,
\alpha
is given by a correct
\mathtt{S}
-rule.

We must also check that, for
\alpha\in A^{x}_{b}\cup\mathtt{Rep}_{P^{\prime}}(b)\cup\mathtt{Rep}_{P^{\prime}}(b)\cdot 1
,
\alpha
is correct: this easily follows from the definition of
\mathtt{C}
and
\mathtt{T}
. Thus,
P
is correct and concludes with
\mathtt{C}(\varepsilon)\vdash t:\mathtt{T}(\varepsilon)
,
i.e.
,
C\vdash t:T
, as expected.

To prove monotonicity,
i.e.
,
P^{\prime}_{1}\leqslant_{\infty}P^{\prime}_{2}
implies

\mathtt{Exp}_{b}(P^{\prime}_{1},\lfloor\cdot\rfloor,t)\leqslant_{\infty}\mathtt{Exp}_{b}(P^{\prime}_{2},\lfloor\cdot\rfloor,t)
, one needs to remark the two following crucial points: (1) the construction of
A_{s}
, which depends on
\lfloor\cdot\rfloor
, is monotonic (2) if
t(\alpha)=x
, then
(\lfloor\alpha\rfloor\cdot\mathtt{T}_{1}(\alpha))\leqslant_{\infty}(\lfloor\alpha\rfloor\cdot\mathtt{T}_{2}(\alpha))

because we assign the same axiom rule in the two singleton sequence types. This ensures that
\mathtt{C}_{1}(\alpha)\leqslant_{\infty}\mathtt{C}_{2}(\alpha)
in all cases.

Remark 12
.

•

The function
\lfloor\cdot\rfloor
does not need to be total on
\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0,1\}
, nor injective for the above construction to work. It only needs to be defined for the positions of the axiom rules that are created (
i.e.
,
\lfloor\cdot\rfloor
must be defined on
A^{x}_{b}
), so that an axiom track is assigned for each axiom rule that is created by expansion. And for all
\mathtt{Rep}_{P^{\prime}}(b)
,
\lfloor\cdot\rfloor
needs to be injective on
\{\alpha\in A^{x}_{b}~|~\alpha\geqslant a\}
(not on
\mathbb{N}^{*}
!), which is enough to ensure that no track conflict occurs.

•

Thus, the monotonicity of expansion thus admits a variant, without having to specify a function
\lfloor\cdot\rfloor
: if
\,\!{}^{f}\!P^{\prime}\leqslant P
type
t^{\prime}
and
t\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}t^{\prime}
, then there exists
\,\!{}^{f}\!P\leqslant P
typing
t
such that
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}P^{\prime}
and
\,\!{}^{f}\!P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}\,\!^{f}\!P
. For that, consider the function
\lfloor\cdot\rfloor
defined by
\lfloor\alpha\rfloor=\mathtt{tr}^{P}(\alpha)
for all
\alpha\in\mathtt{supp}(P)
such that
t(\alpha)
is the variable of the redex.

•

One way wonder how the expansion
P
depends on the choice of
\lfloor\cdot\rfloor
. It actually does not matter that much, because all the possible expansions of
P^{\prime}
are (in some sense)
isomorphic
, whatever the values of the new axiom tracks are.

C.4.
Quantitativity is stable under reducation and expansion.

We can now prove that quantitatitivity is stable under reduction and expansion, as stated in Remark
6
. This means that we did not lose any generality after Sec.
6
by considering only quantitative derivations.

Lemma 54
.

If
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}P^{\prime}
, then
P
is quantitative iff
P^{\prime}
is quantitative.

Proof.

•

Assume that
P\rhd C\vdash t:T
is not quantitative,
t|_{b}=(\lambda x.r)s
and that
t
respects Barendregt convention. Thus, there are
\alpha\in\mathtt{supp}(P)
,
y\in\mathscr{V}
and
k\geqslant 2
such that, for all
d\geqslant 0
, there is
\alpha_{0}\geqslant a
such that

|\alpha_{0}|\geqslant d
,
k\in\mathtt{supp}(\mathtt{C}(\alpha_{0})(y))
. Thus,
B:=\{\alpha_{0}\geqslant a~|~k\in\mathtt{supp}(\mathtt{C}(\alpha_{0})(x))\}
, the set of ascendants of
(\alpha_{0},y,k)
, defines (the postfix of) an infinite branch of
\mathtt{supp}(P)
. There are three cases: (1)
B
visits an occurrence of
s
(
i.e.
, there is
\alpha_{0}\in B
such that
\overline{\alpha_{0}}\geqslant b\cdot 1
) (2)
B
visits
r
but no occurrence of
s
(3)
B
does not visit the redex
(\lambda x.r)s
. By case analysis and using the definition of
\mathtt{C}
, we obtain an infinite branch
B^{\prime}
in the derivation reduct
P^{\prime}
which is in an occurrence of
s
in case (1), in
r
but does not visit an occurrence of
s
in case 2, is outside
r[s/x]
in case (3). In case (1), we may replace
(\alpha,y,k)
by one of its ascendant and assume that
\alpha
is in an occurrence of
s
without losing generality. Likewise, in case (2), we may assume that
(\alpha,y,k)
is in
r
.

•

The converse implication is proved by following back the steps of the above proof and considering the definition of
\mathtt{C}
.

∎

C.5.
Proof of the infinitary subject expansion property

We prove now infinitary subject expansion for system
\mathtt{S}
Proposition
42
.

As mentioned in Sec.
5.3
, performing an
expansion of a term inside a derivation requires that we choose new axiom tracks. We will
do this
uniformly
,
i.e.
, we fix an injection

\lfloor\cdot\rfloor
from
\mathbb{N}^{*}
to
\mathbb{N}-\{0,\,1\}
and any
axiom rule created at position
a
will use the axiom track value

\lfloor a\rfloor
. As we have seen (Sec.
C.3
), this ensures that expansion is monotonic.

Hypotheses.
We consider an
approximable

\mathtt{S}
-derivation
P^{\prime}\rhd C^{\prime}\vdash t^{\prime}:T^{\prime}
and a productive reduction path
t=t_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b_{0}}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}t_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b_{1}}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}t_{2}\ldots t_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b_{n}}}{{\rightarrow}}_{\!\beta}t_{n+1}\rightarrow^{\infty}t^{\prime}
. Thus,
\mathtt{ad}(b_{n})\rightarrow\infty
. We and set
A^{\prime}=\mathtt{supp}(P^{\prime})
.

Assume
\,\!{}^{f}\!P^{\prime}\leqslant P^{\prime}
. Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that, for all

n\geqslant N,~b_{n}\notin\overline{\,\!{}^{f}\!A^{\prime}}
with
\,\!{}^{f}\!A^{\prime}=\mathtt{supp}(\,\!^{f}\!P^{\prime})
. For
n\geqslant N
, we write
\,\!{}^{f}\!P^{\prime}(n)
for the derivation
replacing
t^{\prime}
by
t_{n}
in
\,\!{}^{f}\!P^{\prime}
. This derivation is correct
according to the subject substitution lemma (Lemma
33
),
since
t_{n}(\overline{a})=t^{\prime}(\overline{a})
for all
a\in\,\!^{f}\!A^{\prime}
.

We then write
\,\!{}^{f}\!P^{\prime}(n,~k)
(with
0\leqslant k\leqslant n
) the
derivation obtained by performing
k
expansions (w.r.t. our
reduction sequence and
\lfloor\cdot\rfloor
). Since
b_{n}
is not in
A
, we
observe that
\,\!{}^{f}\!P^{\prime}(n+1,\,1)=\,\!^{f}\!P^{\prime}(t_{n})
. Therefore, for all

n\geqslant N,~\,\!^{f}\!P^{\prime}(n,\,n)=\,\!^{f}\!P^{\prime}(N,\,N)
. Since we could
replace
N
by any
n\geqslant N
,
\,\!{}^{f}\!P
is morally
\,\!{}^{f}\!P^{\prime}(\infty,\,\infty)
. We write
P=\mathtt{init}(P^{\prime})
to refer to this
deterministic construction (which implicitly depends on
\lfloor\cdot\rfloor
).

We set
\mathscr{D}=\{\mathtt{init}(\,\!^{f}\!P^{\prime})~|~\,\!^{f}\!P^{\prime}\leqslant P^{\prime}\}
. Let us
show that
\mathscr{D}
is a directed set.

Let
\,\!{}^{f}\!P^{\prime}_{1},\,\,\!^{f}\!P^{\prime}_{2}\leqslant P^{\prime}
. We set

\,\!{}^{f}\!P^{\prime}=\mathop{\mathtt{sup}}(\,\!^{f}\!P^{\prime}_{1},\,\!^{f}\!P^{\prime}_{2})
, so that
\,\!{}^{f}\!P^{\prime}
is also finite. Let
N
be great enough so that
\forall n\geqslant N
,
b_{n}\notin\overline{\,\!{}^{f}\!A^{\prime}}
with
\,\!{}^{f}\!A^{\prime}=\mathtt{supp}(\,\!^{f}\!P^{\prime})
.

We have
\,\!{}^{f}\!P^{\prime}_{i}\leqslant\,\!^{f}\!P^{\prime}
, so
\,\!{}^{f}\!P_{i}^{\prime}(N)\leqslant\,\!^{f}\!P(N)
. Thus, by monotonicity of
uniform
expansion,
\,\!{}^{f}\!P^{\prime}_{i}(N,\,N)\leqslant\,\!^{f}\!P^{\prime}(N,\,N)

i.e.
,
\mathtt{init}(\,\!^{f}\!P_{i})\leqslant\mathtt{init}(\,\!^{f}\!P)
.

Since
\mathscr{D}
is directed, we can set
P=\mathop{\mathtt{sup}}_{\{\,\!^{f}\!P^{\prime}\,|\,\,\!^{f}\!P^{\prime}\leqslant P^{\prime}\}}\mathtt{init}(\,\!^{f}\!P^{\prime})
. Since for any
\,\!{}^{f}\!P^{\prime}\leqslant P
and the associated usual notations,

\,\!{}^{f}\!\mathtt{C}(\varepsilon)=\,\!^{f}\!\mathtt{C}^{\prime}(\varepsilon)
,
\,\!{}^{f}\!\mathtt{T}(\varepsilon)=\,\!^{f}\!\mathtt{T}^{\prime}(\varepsilon)
and, by Lemma
46
,
\mathtt{C}(\varepsilon),~\mathtt{C}^{\prime}(\varepsilon),~\mathtt{T}(\varepsilon),~\mathtt{T}^{\prime}(\varepsilon)
are the respective infinite joins of
\,\!{}^{f}\!\mathtt{C}(\varepsilon)
,
\,\!{}^{f}\!\mathtt{C}^{\prime}(\varepsilon)
,
\,\!{}^{f}\!\mathtt{T}(\varepsilon)
,
\,\!{}^{f}\!\mathtt{T}^{\prime}(\varepsilon)
when
\,\!{}^{f}\!P^{\prime}
ranges over
\mathtt{Approx}(P^{\prime})
,
we conclude that
\mathtt{C}(\varepsilon)=\mathtt{C}^{\prime}(\varepsilon)=C^{\prime}
and
\mathtt{T}(\varepsilon)=\mathtt{T}^{\prime}(\varepsilon)=T^{\prime}
. In particular,
C^{\prime}\vdash t:T^{\prime}
is approximably derivable. This concludes the proof of the infinitary subject expansion property.

C.6.
Equinecessity and Bipositions of Null Applicative Depth

One may wonder whether every biposition (in a quantitative derivation
P
) is equinecessary with a
root biposition

i.e.
, a biposition that is located in the judgment concluding
P
. Such a biposition is of the form
\mathtt{p}=(\varepsilon,c)
with
c\in\mathtt{supp}(\mathtt{T}^{P}(0))
or
\mathtt{p}=(\varepsilon,x,k\cdot c)
with
k\cdot c\in\mathtt{supp}(\mathtt{C}^{P}(0)(x))
.

This would imply that, for a derivation
P
to be approximable, it is enough to have: “
P
is quantitative and, for all
\,\!{}^{0}\!B\subseteq{\mathtt{bisupp}(P)}
finite set of
root
bipositions, there exists
\,\!{}^{f}\!P\leqslant P
such that
\,\!{}^{0}\!B\subseteq{\mathtt{bisupp}(\,\!^{f}\!P)}
”. We call this condition
root approximability
.

\mathtt{p}\in\,\!^{0}\!B
,

This is actually true in the finite case and so, for the approximable derivation. However, this is not true for
any
derivation. We exhibit a counter-example of this conjecture in this section
i.e.
, a derivation that root approximable but not approximable. We present this counter example with a
\mathscr{R}
-derivation whereas it should be a
\mathtt{S}
-derivation (since approximability is only an informal notion for System
\mathscr{R}
), but it is easier to understand that way. Corresponding
\mathtt{S}
-derivations are not difficult to define from our presentation.

The idea is to use a productive reduction path
t_{0}\rightarrow t_{1}\rightarrow t_{2}\rightarrow\ldots\rightarrow^{\infty}t^{\prime}
such that no reduction step is erasing but there is a variable
x\in\mathtt{fv}(t_{0})=\mathtt{fv}(t_{1})=\ldots
such that
x\notin\mathtt{fv}(t^{\prime})
(
i.e.
, there is an asymptotic erasure).

Let
\Delta_{*}=\lambda x.(\lambda z.y(x\,x\,z)
and
t=\Delta_{*}\,\Delta_{*}
, so that
t\rightarrow\lambda z.y(t\,z)
(see Fig.
16
). We have
t\equiv_{\!\beta}\mathtt{Y}\,(\lambda tx.(\lambda z.y(tx)))
.

t
\longrightarrow
y

t
z

\char 64\relax
\char 64\relax
\lambda z

Figure 16.
Reduction of
t

Note that
t\,f
converges to the term
t^{\prime}=y{{}^{\omega}}
, which does not contain
f
. Indeed,
t\,f\rightarrow(\lambda z.y(t\,z))f\rightarrow y(t\,f)
(non-erasing steps).

There are two derivations
\Psi_{1}
and
\Psi_{2}
respectively concluding with
y:[[o]\rightarrow o]_{\omega}\vdash\lambda z.y(t\,z):[o]\rightarrow o
and
y:[[o]\rightarrow o]_{\omega}\vdash\lambda z.y(t\,z):[\,]\rightarrow o
. They are obtained from
\Psi^{\prime}_{1}
and
\Psi^{\prime}_{2}
from Fig
16
by a one-step expansion:

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[o]\rightarrow o}
y

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[o]\rightarrow o}
t
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}o}
z

\char 64\relax
\char 64\relax
\lambda z

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[o]\rightarrow o}
\Psi^{\prime}_{1}\rhd y:[[o]\rightarrow o]_{\omega}\vdash\lambda z.y(t\,z):[o]\rightarrow o
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[o]\rightarrow o}
y

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[\,]\rightarrow o}
t

\char 64\relax
\char 64\relax
