{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[\,]\rightarrow o}
\Psi^{\prime}_{2}\rhd y:[[o]\rightarrow o]_{\omega}\vdash\lambda z.y(t\,z):[\,]\rightarrow o

Figure 17.
Two Derivations typing
t^{\prime}:=\lambda z.y(t\,z)

Let
\Pi_{\Omega}
a derivation concluding with
\vdash\Omega:o
. This derivation is unsound and is intuitively not approximable: it is impossible to find a finite derivation (of
\mathscr{R}_{0}
) concluding with
\vdash\Omega:o
.
Then using
\Psi_{1}
and
\Pi_{\Omega}
and an
\mathtt{app}
-rule we can build a derivation
\Pi_{1}
concluding with
y:[[o]\rightarrow o]_{\omega}\vdash t\,\Omega:o
, in which the subterm
\Omega
is typed. We can also build a derivation
\Pi_{2}
from
\Psi_{2}
that also concludes with
y:[[o]\rightarrow o]_{\omega}\vdash t\,\Omega:o
. This times, the subterm
\Omega
is not typed.

It is not difficult to see that
\Pi_{2}
is intuitively approximable, whereas
\Pi_{1}
is root approximable but not fully approximable. Roughly speaking, the subderivation of
\Pi_{1}
typing
\Omega
is the only non-approximable part of
\Pi_{1}
.
Indeed:

•

\Pi_{2}
is not approximable since it contains a subderivation typing the mute term
\Omega
.

•

Every (finite) approximation of
\Pi_{2}
is an approximation of
\Pi_{1}
. Thus, the join of the finite approximations of
\Pi_{1}
is actually
\Pi_{2}
.

This proves that
root
approximability is not equivalent to approximability, and that root approximability actually accepts derivations which are partially unsound.

Appendix D
Approximability of normal derivations

In this Appendix, we describe all the quantitative derivations typing normal forms in system
\mathtt{S}
and we prove that all of them are approximable.

D.1.
Called rank of a position inside a type in a derivation

We recall that the rank
\mathtt{rk}(a)
of
a\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
is defined by
\mathtt{rk}(a)=\max(\mathtt{ad}(a),\max(a))
(Definition
39
). We reuse the notation for constrain levels
\mathtt{clev}
defined in Sec.
7.1
and the notation
\mathtt{Call}(a)
define in Sec.
7.2
.

For each
a
in
A
and each position
c
in
\mathtt{Call}(a)
such that

\mathtt{Call}(a)(c)\neq X_{a^{\prime}}
(for all
a^{\prime}
), we define the numbers
\mathtt{cr}_{\mathtt{out}}(a,c)
and
\mathtt{cr}_{\mathtt{in}}(a,c)
by:

•

When
a
is a unconstrained node,
\mathtt{cr}_{\mathtt{out}}(a,c)
is
\mathtt{rk}(a)
and
\mathtt{cr}_{\mathtt{in}}(a,c)=\mathtt{rk}(c)
.

•

When
a
is a non-zero position: the respective values of
\mathtt{cr}_{\mathtt{out}}(a,c)
and
\mathtt{cr}_{\mathtt{in}}(a,c)
for the positions colored in red are
\mathtt{rk}(a)
and
\mathtt{rk}(c)
.

\mathtt{E}(\mathring{a})(x_{1})\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rightarrow}}\mathtt{E}(\mathring{a})(x_{2})\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rightarrow}}\ldots\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rightarrow}}\mathtt{E}(\mathring{a})(x_{n}){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rightarrow\mathtt{T}(\mathring{a})}

where
n=\mathtt{clev}(a)
.
In particular,
\mathtt{cr}_{\mathtt{out}}(a,1^{i})=\mathtt{ad}(a)
and
\mathtt{cr}_{\mathtt{in}}(a,1^{i})=0
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
..

•

When
a
is partial: the respective values of
\mathtt{cr}_{\mathtt{out}}(a,c)
and
\mathtt{cr}_{\mathtt{in}}(a,c)
for the positions
colored in red are
\mathtt{rk}(a)
and
\mathtt{rk}(c)
.

\mathtt{R}_{1}(a)\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rightarrow}}\ldots\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rightarrow}}\mathtt{R}_{n}(a){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rightarrow\mathtt{T}(\mathring{a})}

where
n=\mathtt{clev}(a)
.
In particular,
\mathtt{cr}_{\mathtt{out}}(a,1^{i})=\mathtt{ad}(a)
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
and
\mathtt{cr}_{\mathtt{out}}(a,1^{n}\cdot c)=\mathtt{ad}(a)
.

For each
a\in A
and each position
c
in
\mathtt{T}(a)
, we define

\mathtt{cr}_{\mathtt{out}}(a,c)
and
\mathtt{cr}_{\mathtt{in}}(a,c)
by extending
\mathtt{cr}_{\mathtt{out}}
and
\mathtt{cr}_{\mathtt{in}}
via substitution (this is formally done in the next section).

Again, for each
a\in A
, each variable
x
and each position

c
in
\mathtt{C}(a)(x)(c)
, we define
\mathtt{cr}_{\mathtt{out}}(a,x,c)
and
\mathtt{cr}_{\mathtt{in}}(a,x,c)
by extending
\mathtt{cr}_{\mathtt{out}}
via substitution.

The definition of
\mathtt{cr}_{\mathtt{out}}(a,c)
,
\mathtt{cr}_{\mathtt{out}}(a,x,c)
,
\mathtt{cr}_{\mathtt{in}}(a,c)
and
\mathtt{cr}_{\mathtt{in}}(a,x,c)
) are sound, because in
\mathtt{E}(a)(x)
and
\mathtt{R}_{i}(x)
, the
X_{a^{\prime}}
occur at depth
\geqslant 1
in
\mathtt{Call}(a^{\prime})
.

Definition 55
.

If
\mathtt{p}\in{\mathtt{bisupp}(P)}
, we define
called rank
of
\mathtt{p}
by

\mathtt{cr}(\mathtt{p})=\max(\mathtt{cr}_{\mathtt{out}}(\mathtt{p}),\,\mathtt{cr}_{\mathtt{in}}(\mathtt{p}))

D.2.
Truncation of rank
n

We present more formally the definitions of the last section and we recall that
\mathtt{rk}(a)=\max(\mathtt{ad}(a),\,\max(a))
for
a\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
.

As we saw above, a quantitative derivation typing a normal form can be reconstructed from its supports and the types assigned in the unconstrained positions. Thus, in a quantitative derivation
P
typing a normal form
t
such that
\mathtt{supp}(P)=A
, every
\mathtt{p}\in{\mathtt{bisupp}(P)}
(with
\mathtt{p}=(a,c)
or
\mathtt{p}=(a,x,c)
) must come from (so to say) an
(a^{\prime},c^{\prime})
with
a\geqslant a^{\prime}
. We call
a^{\prime}
the
calling outer position
and
c^{\prime}
the
calling inner position
of
\mathtt{p}
. Let us define them formally now.

For all
a\in A
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we set, by induction on
k
,
\mathtt{T}^{0}(a)=X_{a}
and

\mathtt{T}^{k+1}(a)=\mathtt{T}^{k}(a)[\mathtt{Call}(a^{\prime})/X_{a^{\prime}}]_{a^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
and for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we set
\mathtt{supp}_{*}(\mathtt{T}^{k}(a))=\{c\in\mathtt{supp}(\mathtt{T}^{k}(a))\,|\,\forall a^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{*},~\mathtt{T}^{k}(a)(c)\neq X_{a^{\prime}}\}
. Thus,
\mathtt{supp}(\mathtt{T}(a))=\cup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\mathtt{supp}_{*}(\mathtt{T}^{*}(a))
for all
a\in A
.

If
c\in\mathtt{supp}(\mathtt{T}(a))
, there is a minimal
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
c\in\mathtt{supp}_{*}(\mathtt{T}^{k}(a))
.
We denote it
\mathtt{cd}(a)(c)
(call-depth of
c
at pos.
a
).

When
k=\mathtt{cd}(a)(c)
, there are unique
c^{\prime}\in\mathtt{supp}(\mathtt{T}(a)),\;c^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
a^{\prime}\in A
such that

c=c^{\prime}\cdot c^{\prime\prime},\ \mathtt{T}^{k-1}(a)(c^{\prime})=X_{a^{\prime}}
(we have necessarily
a\leqslant a^{\prime}
since
a^{\prime}
is called by
a
),
c^{\prime\prime}\in\mathtt{supp}_{*}(\mathtt{Call}(a^{\prime}))
and
\mathtt{Call}(a^{\prime})(c^{\prime\prime})=\mathtt{T}(a)(c)
.
We write
a^{\prime}=\mathtt{cop}(a,c)
(
calling outer position
of
c
at position
a
),
c^{\prime}=\mathtt{cip}(a,c)
(
calling inner position
of
c
at position
a
)
and
c^{\prime\prime}=\mathtt{pf}(a,c)
(
postfix
of
c
at position
a
).
Then, we set
\mathtt{cr}_{\mathtt{out}}(a,c):=\mathtt{rk}(a^{\prime})
and
\mathtt{cr}_{\mathtt{in}}(a,c)=\mathtt{rk}(c^{\prime\prime})
.
Finally, we may define
\mathtt{cr}(a,c)
by
\mathtt{cr}(a,c)=\max(\mathtt{cr}_{\mathtt{out}}(a,c),\mathtt{cr}_{\mathtt{in}}(a,c))
. Implicitly,
\mathtt{cr}(a,c)
depends on
P
, but
P
is omitted from the notation.

•

We set
A_{n}=\{a\in A\,|\,\mathtt{rk}(a)\leqslant n\}
.

•

For all
\mathring{a}\in\mathring{A}_{n}
, we define
\mathring{\mathtt{T}}_{n}(\mathring{a})
by removing
all the positions
c
such that
\mathtt{rk}(c)>n
,
i.e.
,
\mathring{\mathtt{T}}_{n}(\mathring{a})
is the restriction of
\mathring{\mathtt{T}}(\mathring{a})
on
\{c\in\mathtt{supp}(\mathring{\mathtt{T}}(\mathring{a}))\,|\,\mathtt{rk}(c)\leqslant n\}
.

Since
t
is in
\Lambda^{001}
(and not in
\Lambda^{111}\setminus\Lambda^{001}
),
A_{n}
is finite. Since, for all
a\in A
,
\mathtt{T}(a)
is in
\mathtt{Typ}
(and not in
\mathtt{Typ}^{111}\setminus\mathtt{Typ}
), the type
\mathring{\mathtt{T}}_{n}(\mathring{a})
is finite for all
\mathring{a}\in\mathring{A}_{n}
.

We define
P_{n}
as the natural extension of
(A_{n},\mathring{\mathtt{T}}_{n})
. We retrieve contexts
\mathtt{C}_{n}
and types
\mathtt{T}_{n}
such that, for all
a\in A_{n}
,
P_{n}=\mathtt{C}_{n}(a)\vdash t|_{a}:\mathtt{T}_{n}(a)
.

Lemma 56
.

For all
k\in\mathbb{N},\;a\in A,\;c\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, we have
\mathtt{rk}(a)\leqslant n
and

c\in\mathtt{supp}(\mathtt{T}_{n}^{k}(a))
iff
c\in\mathtt{supp}(\mathtt{T}^{k}(a))
and

\mathtt{cr}(a,c)\leqslant n
.

In that case,
\mathtt{T}^{k}(a)(c)=\mathtt{T}_{n}^{k}(a)(c)
.

Proof.

By a simple but tedious induction on
k
. The notations
\mathtt{cop}_{n}
,
\mathtt{cip}_{n}
,
\mathtt{pf}_{n}
and
\mathtt{T}^{k}_{n}
for the
ad hoc
definitions of calling outer positions and so on for
P_{n}
.

•

Case
k=0
:

\Rightarrow
: if
\mathtt{rk}(a)\leqslant n
and
c\in\mathtt{supp}(\mathtt{T}_{n}^{0}(a))
, then
\mathtt{rk}(a)\leqslant n
and
c=\varepsilon
.
By definition,
\mathtt{cr}_{\mathtt{out}}(a,\varepsilon)=\mathtt{rk}(a)\leqslant n
and

\mathtt{cr}_{\mathtt{in}}(a)(\varepsilon)=\mathtt{rk}(\varepsilon)=0
. Thus,
\mathtt{cr}(a,c)\leqslant n
.

\Leftarrow
: conversely, if
c\in\mathtt{supp}(\mathtt{T}^{0}(a))
and
\mathtt{cr}(a,\,c)\leqslant n
, we have likewise
c=\varepsilon
and
\mathtt{cop}(a,\varepsilon)=a
, so
\mathtt{rk}(a)=\mathtt{cr}_{\mathtt{out}}(a,c)\leqslant n
. So
c=\varepsilon\in\mathtt{T}_{n}^{0}(a)
.

•

Case
k+1
:

\Rightarrow
: we assume that
\mathtt{rk}(a)\leqslant n
and
c\in\mathtt{supp}(\mathtt{T}^{k}_{n}(a))
.
We have two cases, whether
c\in\mathtt{supp}_{*}(\mathtt{T}^{k}_{n}(a))
or not. If
c\in\mathtt{supp}_{*}(\mathtt{T}^{k}_{n}(a))
, then
\mathtt{T}^{k+1}_{n}(a)(c)=\mathtt{T}^{k}_{n}(a)(c)
. By induction hypothesis, we have
\mathtt{T}^{k}_{n}(a)(c)=\mathtt{T}^{k}(a)(c)
. This implies
\mathtt{T}^{k+1}(a)(c)=\mathtt{T}^{k}(a)(c)
and also,
\mathtt{T}^{k+1}_{n}(a)(c)=\mathtt{T}^{k+1}(a)(c)
. Thus, let us assume
c\notin\mathtt{supp}_{*}(\mathtt{T}^{k}_{n}(a))
.

We have once again two cases, whether
c\in\mathtt{supp}(\mathtt{T}^{k}_{n}(a))
or not.

Assume first that
c\in\mathtt{supp}(\mathtt{T}^{k}_{n}(a))
. Since
c\notin\mathtt{supp}_{*}(\mathtt{T}^{k}_{n}(a))
, we have
\mathtt{T}_{n}^{k}(a)(c)=X_{a^{\prime}}
where
a^{\prime}=\mathtt{cop}(a,c)
. Then
\mathtt{T}^{k+1}_{n}(a)(c)=\mathtt{T}^{=}\mathtt{Call}_{n}(a^{\prime})(\varepsilon)
. Moreover, the induction hypothesisapplies to
(a,c)
and gives
\mathtt{cr}(a,c)\leqslant n
and
\mathtt{T}^{k}(a)(c)==\mathtt{T}^{k}_{n}(X_{a^{\prime}})
. Thus,
\mathtt{T}^{k+1}(a)(c)=\mathtt{Call}(a^{\prime})(\varepsilon)
and
\mathtt{rk}(a^{\prime})=\mathtt{cr}_{\mathtt{out}}(a,c)\leqslant n
. Since
a\leqslant a^{\prime}
, we have
\mathtt{rk}(a)\leqslant n
.
Since
\mathtt{rk}(\varepsilon)=0
, we have
\mathtt{Call}_{n}(a^{\prime})(\varepsilon)=\mathtt{Call}(a^{\prime})(\varepsilon)
. So
\mathtt{T}^{k+1}(a)(c)=\mathtt{T}^{k+1}_{n}(a)(\varepsilon)=\mathtt{Call}_{n}(a^{\prime})(\varepsilon)
.

We assume now that
c\notin\mathtt{supp}(\mathtt{T}^{k}_{n}(a))
. Since
c\in\mathtt{supp}(T^{k+1}_{n}(a))\setminus\mathtt{supp}(\mathtt{T}^{k}_{n}(a))
, there are unique
a^{\prime}\in A_{n}
,
c^{\prime\prime}\in\mathtt{supp}(T^{k}_{n}(a))
and
c^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
such that
c=c^{\prime}\cdot c^{\prime\prime}
,
\mathtt{T}^{k}_{n}(a)(c)=X_{a^{\prime}}
.
In particular,
\mathtt{T}^{k}_{n}(a)(c)=\mathtt{T}^{k}_{n}(a^{\prime})(c^{\prime\prime})
. We have two subcases:

–

Case
\mathtt{T}^{k+1}_{n}(a)(c)\neq X_{a^{\prime\prime}}
for all
a^{\prime\prime}
. In that case,
a^{\prime}=\mathtt{cop}_{n}(a,c)
,
c^{\prime}=\mathtt{cip}_{n}(a,c)
and
c^{\prime\prime}=\mathtt{pf}_{n}(a,c)
.
We have
c^{\prime\prime}\in\mathtt{supp}_{*}(\mathtt{Call}_{n}(a^{\prime}))\leqslant\mathtt{supp}(\mathtt{Call}(a^{\prime}))
. This entails in particular
\mathtt{T}^{k+1}(a)(c)=\mathtt{Call}_{n}(a^{\prime})(c)=\mathtt{T}^{k+1}_{n}(a,c)
.

Moreover, we have
\mathtt{cr}_{\mathtt{out}}(a,c)=\mathtt{rk}(a^{\prime})\leqslant n
since
a\in A_{n}
and
\mathtt{cr}_{\mathtt{in}}(a,c)=\mathtt{rk}(c^{\prime\prime})\leqslant n
since
c^{\prime\prime}\in\mathtt{supp}(\mathtt{T}_{n}(a^{\prime}))
. Thus,
\mathtt{cr}(a,c)\leqslant n
.

–

Case
\mathtt{T}^{k+1}_{n}(a)(c)=X_{a^{\prime\prime}}
for some
a^{\prime\prime}\geqslant a^{\prime}
. In that case,
a^{\prime\prime}=\mathtt{cop}_{n}(a,c)
,
c=\mathtt{cip}_{n}(a,c)
and
\mathtt{pf}_{n}(a,c)=\varepsilon
. Due to the form of
\mathtt{Call}_{n}(\_)
,
{}^{\prime\prime}=1^{i}\cdot\ell
for some
j<\mathtt{clev}(a^{\prime})
and
\ell=\lfloor a^{\prime}\rfloor\geqslant 2
. By induction hypothesis,
\mathtt{T}^{k}(a)(c^{\prime})=\mathtt{T}^{k}_{n}(a)(c^{\prime})
, so
\mathtt{T}^{k}(a)(c^{\prime})=X_{a^{\prime}}
. Since
c^{\prime\prime}=1^{i}\cdot\ell\in\mathtt{supp}(\mathtt{Call}_{n}(a^{\prime}))
and
\mathtt{Call}_{n}(a^{\prime})\leqslant\mathtt{Call}(a^{\prime})
, we also have
\mathtt{Call}(a^{\prime})(c^{\prime\prime})=X_{a^{\prime\prime}}
. Thus,
\mathtt{T}^{k+1}(a)(c)=X_{a^{\prime\prime}}=\mathtt{T}^{k+1}_{n}(a)(c)
.

Moreover,
\mathtt{cr}_{\mathtt{out}}(a,c)=\mathtt{rk}(a^{\prime\prime})\leqslant n
since
a^{\prime\prime}\in A_{n}
, and
\mathtt{cr}_{\mathtt{in}}(a,c)=\mathtt{rk}(\varepsilon)=0
, so
\mathtt{cr}(a,c)\leqslant n
.

\noindent\Leftarrow
: conversely, if
a\in A,\ c\in\mathtt{supp}(\mathtt{T}^{k+1}(a))
and
\mathtt{cr}(a,c)\leqslant n
, we assume that
a\notin\mathtt{supp}_{*}(\mathtt{T}^{k}(a))
(if
a\in\mathtt{supp}_{*}(\mathtt{T}^{k}(a))
, the case is straightforwardly handled by induction hypothesis).

Assume first that
c\in\mathtt{supp}(\mathtt{T}^{k}(a))
and
\mathtt{T}^{k}(a)=X_{a^{\prime}}
. Then
\mathtt{rk}(a^{\prime})=\mathtt{cr}_{\mathtt{out}}(a,c)\leqslant n
, so that
a^{\prime}\in A_{n}
, and
\mathtt{T}^{k+1}(a)=\mathtt{Call}_{n}(a^{\prime})(\varepsilon)
. By induction hypothesison
(a,c)
,
a\in A_{n}
,
c\in\mathtt{supp}(\mathtt{T}^{k}_{n}(a))
and
\mathtt{T}^{k}_{n}(a)(c)=X_{a^{\prime}}
, so
\mathtt{T}^{k+1}_{n}(\varepsilon)=\mathtt{Call}_{n}(a^{\prime})(\varepsilon)
. Since
\mathtt{Call}_{n}(a^{\prime})\leqslant\mathtt{Call}(a)
, we have
\mathtt{Call}_{n}(a^{\prime})=\mathtt{Call}(a^{\prime})(\varepsilon)
, which is equal to
\mathtt{T}^{k+1}(a)(c)
since
\mathtt{T}^{k}(a)(c)=X_{a^{\prime}}
. Thus,
\mathtt{T}^{k+1}_{n}(a)(cc)=\mathtt{T}^{k+1}(a)(c)
.

We assume now that
c\notin\mathtt{supp}(\mathtt{T}^{k}(a))
.
Since
c\in\mathtt{supp}(T^{k+1}(a))\setminus\mathtt{supp}(\mathtt{T}^{k}(a))
, there are unique
a^{\prime}\in A
,
c^{\prime}\in\mathtt{supp}(T^{k}(a))
and
c^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
such that
c=c^{\prime}\cdot c^{\prime\prime}
,
\mathtt{T}^{k}(a)(c)=X_{a^{\prime}}
. By induction hypothesison
c^{\prime}
,
\mathtt{rk}(a)\leqslant n
,
c^{\prime}\in\mathtt{supp}(T^{k}_{n}(a))
and
\mathtt{T}^{k}_{n}(a)(c^{\prime})=\mathtt{T}^{k}(a)(c)=X_{a^{\prime}}
.
In particular,
\mathtt{T}^{k}(a)(c)=\mathtt{T}^{k}(a^{\prime})(c^{\prime\prime})
. We have two subcases:

–

If
\mathtt{T}^{k+1}(a)(c)\neq X_{a^{\prime\prime}}
for all
a^{\prime\prime}
, then
a^{\prime}=\mathtt{cop}(a,c)
,
c^{\prime}=\mathtt{cip}(a,c)
and
c^{\prime\prime}=\mathtt{pf}(a,c)
. Moreover,
\mathtt{rk}(c^{\prime\prime})=\mathtt{cr}_{\mathtt{in}}(a,c)\leqslant\mathtt{cr}(a,c)\leqslant n
, so
{}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathtt{supp}(\mathtt{Call}_{n}(a^{\prime}))
. By definition of
\mathtt{T}^{k+1}_{n}(a)
,
\mathtt{T}^{k+1}=\mathtt{Call}_{n}(a^{\prime})(c^{\prime})
. Since
\mathtt{Call}_{n}(a^{\prime})\leqslant\mathtt{Call}(a^{\prime})
, we have
\mathtt{Call}_{n}(a^{\prime})(c^{\prime})=\mathtt{Call}(a)(c^{\prime})
, which is equal to
\mathtt{T}^{k+1}(a)(c)
. Thus,
\mathtt{T}^{k+1}_{n}(a)(c)=\mathtt{T}^{k+1}(a)(c)
.

–

If
\mathtt{T}^{k+1}(a)(c)=X_{a^{\prime\prime}}
for some
a^{\prime\prime}\geqslant a^{\prime}
. In that case,
a^{\prime\prime}=\mathtt{cop}(a,c)
,
c=\mathtt{cip}(a,c)
and
\mathtt{pf}(a,c)=\varepsilon
.
Due to the form of
\mathtt{Call}(\_)
,
{}^{\prime\prime}=1^{j}\cdot\ell
for some
j<\mathtt{clev}(a^{\prime})
and
\ell=\lfloor a^{\prime}\rfloor\geqslant 2
. By definition of
\mathtt{Call}_{n}(\_)
, we also have
1^{j}\in\mathtt{Call}_{n}(a^{\prime})
and
\mathtt{Call}_{n}(a^{\prime})(c^{\prime\prime})=X_{a^{\prime\prime}}
(
\mathtt{Call}_{n}(a^{\prime})
and
\mathtt{Call}(a^{\prime})
may only differ on positions
\geqslant 1^{\mathtt{clev}(a^{\prime})}
by construction). By definition of
T^{k+1}_{n}
, we have
T^{k+1}_{n}(a)(c)=\mathtt{Call}_{n}(a^{\prime})(X_{a^{\prime\prime}})
. In particular,
T^{k+1}_{n}(a)(c)=\mathtt{T}^{k+1}(a)(c)
.

∎

D.3.
A Complete Sequence of Derivation Approximations

We now prove Lemma
40
:

Lemma
.

If
P
is a quantitative derivation typing a normal form
t
, then
P
is approximable.

Proof.

Let
\,\!{}^{0}\!B\subset{\mathtt{bisupp}(P)}
a finite subset. We set
n=\max\{\mathtt{cr}(\mathtt{p})\,|\,\mathtt{p}\in B\}
. Then
\,\!{}^{0}\!B\subseteq{\mathtt{bisupp}(P_{n})}
.

In order to conclude, it is enough to prove that
P_{n}
is a
finite
derivation. For that, we notice that
A_{n}
is finite and, for all
a\in A_{n}
,
\mathtt{Call}_{n}(a)
is finite and
\mathtt{T}_{n}^{k+1}(a)=T_{n}^{n+1}(a)
for all
k\geqslant n+1
, since
\mathtt{T}^{n+1}_{n}(a)
does not contain any
X_{a^{\prime}}
. In particular,
\mathtt{T}_{n}(a)
is finite. Since
A_{n}
and all the
\mathtt{T}_{n}(a)
(
a
ranging over
A
) are finite,
P_{n}
is finite.
∎

Appendix E
Infinitary Multiset Types

We present here a definition of type assignment system
\mathscr{R}
,
which is an infinitary version of Gardner-de Carvalho’s system
\mathscr{R}_{0}
.

Intuitively, a multiset is a sequence in which we have the positions (the tracks) of the elements has been collapsed,
e.g.
, the multiset
[o_{1},o_{2},o_{1}]
may be seen indifferently as the collapse of the sequences
(3\cdot o_{1},5\cdot o_{2},7\cdot o_{2})
,
(2\cdot o_{1},8\cdot o_{2},10\cdot o_{2})
or
(3\cdot o_{2},5\cdot o_{1},7\cdot o_{2})
. Since there are nesting of multisets in the finite types from System
\mathscr{R}_{0}
, the types of
\mathscr{R}_{0}
may be seen as an inductive/nested collapse of finite types of System
\mathtt{S}
.

In order to build an infinitary version of
\mathscr{R}_{0}
whose types will feature infinitary nestings of multisets, it is natural to define those types as coinductive collapse of sequences.

E.1.
Types

Let
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
be two labelled trees or two sequences of labelled trees (in the sense of Sec.
4

). A
01-stable isomorphism
from
U_{1}
to
U_{2}
is a bijection
\phi
from
\mathtt{supp}(U_{1})
to
\mathtt{supp}(U_{2})
such that:

•

\phi
is monotonic for the prefix order.

•

If
a\cdot k\in\mathtt{supp}(U_{1})
with
k=0
or
k=1
, then
\phi(a\cdot k)=\phi(a)=k

•

For all
a\in\mathtt{supp}(U_{1})
,
U_{2}(\phi(a))=U_{1}(a)
.

Thus, a 01-stable isomorphism is either an isomorphism of labelled trees or an isomorphism of sequences, which preserves tracks 0 and 1. We write
U_{1}\equiv U_{2}
when there is a 01-stable isomorphism from
U_{1}
to
U_{2}
.
When
U
is a tree, the definition implies that
\phi(\varepsilon)=\varepsilon
.
If
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
are types (resp. sequence types),
\phi
is called a
type isomorphism
(resp.
sequence type isomorphism
).

Alternatively, we can define
U_{1}\equiv U_{2}
for types and sequence types by coinduction, without reference to 01-stable isomorphism:

•

o\equiv o

•

(S_{k})_{{k\in K}}\equiv(S^{\prime}_{k})_{{k\in K}^{\prime}}
if there is a bijection
\rho:K\rightarrow K^{\prime}
such that, for all
{k\in K}
,
S_{k}\equiv S^{\prime}_{\rho(k)}
.

•

(S_{k})_{{k\in K}}\rightarrow T\equiv(S^{\prime}_{k})_{{k\in K}^{\prime}}\rightarrow T^{\prime}
if
(S_{k})_{{k\in K}}\equiv(S^{\prime}_{k})_{{k\in K}^{\prime}}
and
T\equiv T^{\prime}
.

The set
\mathtt{Typ}_{\mathscr{R}}
of types of System
\mathscr{R}
is defined as the quotient set
\mathtt{Typ}/\equiv
.

If
U
is a
\mathtt{S}
-type or a sequence type, its equivalence class is written

\overline{U}
. We may now define coinductively the notation of the collapses of
\mathtt{S}
-types:

•

The equivalent class of a sequence type
F=(S_{k})_{{k\in K}}
is the
multiset type written
[\overline{S_{k}}]_{k\in\mathtt{Rt}(F)}

•

We write
\overline{F}\rightarrow\overline{T}
for
\overline{F\rightarrow T}
.

•

If
o
is a type variable,
\overline{o}
is the singleton
\{o\}
. In that case, we just write (abusively)
o
instead of
\overline{o}
or
\{o\}
.

Countable sum
+_{i\in I}\overline{F^{i}}
is defined on
the set of multisets types by using an arbitrary bijection
j
from the pairwise disjoint countable sum
\mathbb{N}\times(\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0,1\})
to
\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0,1\}
.

Let
(F^{i})_{{i\in I}}=((S^{i}_{k})_{{k\in K}(i)})_{{i\in I}}
a countable family of sequence type such that
I\subseteq\mathbb{N}
. We define
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j}}{{+}}_{{i\in I}}F^{i}
as the sequence type
F=(S_{k})_{{k\in K}}
such that
K=\{j(i,k)\,|\,i\in I,\,{k\in K}(i)\}
and, for all
{k\in K}
,
S_{k}
is
S^{i}_{k_{0}}
where
(i,k_{0})
is the unique pair such that
i\in I,\;k_{0}\in K(i)
and
k=j(i,k_{0})
. We may then prove that (1) if for all
{i\in I}
,
F^{i}\equiv G^{i}
, then
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j}}{{+}}_{{i\in I}}F^{i}\equiv\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j}}{{+}}_{{i\in I}}G^{i}
(2) the class of
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j}}{{+}}_{{i\in I}}F^{i}
does not depend on
j
. Thus,
we may define countable sum operator + on multiset types. It is routine work to prove that
+
infinitarily associative and commutative on multiset types, as expected.

E.2.
Typing rules

An
\mathscr{R}
-context
is a total function from the set of term variables
\mathscr{V}
to the set of infinitary multiset types. An
\mathscr{R}
-judgment
is a triple of the form
\Gamma\vdash t:\tau
where
G
is a
\mathscr{R}
-context,
t
a 001-term and
\tau
a
\mathscr{R}
-types. The set of
semi-rigid
derivations is the set of trees (labelled with
\mathscr{R}
-judgments) defined coinductively by the following rules:

\mathtt{ax}

\displaystyle x:[\tau]\vdash x:\tau

\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma;x:[\sigma_{i}]_{{i\in I}}\vdash t:\tau

\mathtt{abs}

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\lambda x.t:\,[\sigma_{i}]_{{i\in I}}\rightarrow\tau

\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash t:[\sigma_{i}]_{{i\in I}}\rightarrow\tau\hskip 14.22636pt(\Delta_{k}\vdash u:\sigma_{k})_{{k\in K}}

\mathtt{app}

\displaystyle\Gamma+_{{k\in K}}\Delta_{k}\vdash t\,u:\,\tau

Why do we say that these derivations are
semi-rigid
? Because the argument of derivations are still placed on argument tracks
{k\in K}
, while obviously, they should not matter when we work with multisets.
Let
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
be two semi-rigid derivations. We define the set of
\mathscr{R}
-derivation as the quotient set of that of semi-derivation by the relation
\equiv
.

An element of
\mathtt{Deriv}_{\mathscr{R}}
is usually written
\Pi
, whereas an element of
\mathtt{Deriv}_{*}
is written
P
(as for system
\mathtt{S}
). Notice the derivation
\Pi
and
\Pi^{\prime}
of Sec.
3.6
and Fig.
7
are objects of
\mathtt{Deriv}
.

E.3.
Quantitativity and Coinduction in System
\mathscr{R}

Let
\Gamma
be
any
context. Exactly as in Sec.
5.2
, we can use the infinite branch of
f^{\infty}

to give the following variant of derivation
\Pi^{\prime}
from Sec.
3.6
, which respects the rules
of system
\mathscr{R}
:

\displaystyle\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 48.72198pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=97.44397pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\mathtt{ax}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle f:[[o]\rightarrow o]\vdash f:[o]\rightarrow o$}}}}\enskip\penalty\hskip 14.22636pt\Pi^{\prime}_{\Gamma}\rhd f:[[o]\rightarrow o]_{\omega}+\Gamma\vdash f^{\infty}:o

\mathtt{app}

\displaystyle f:[[o]\rightarrow o]_{\omega}+\Gamma\vdash f^{\infty}:o

The notion of quantitativity (Definition
16
) can be adapted while considering multisets:

Definition 57
.

•

A semi-rigid derivation
P
is
quantitative
if, for all
a\in\mathtt{supp}(P),~\Gamma(a)(x)=[\tau(a^{\prime})]_{a^{\prime}\in\mathtt{Ax}^{P}_{a}{x}}
.

•

A
\mathscr{R}
-derivation
\Pi
is
quantitative
if any of its semi-rigid representatives is (in that case, all of them are quantitative).

In the next subsection, we show that a derivation
\Pi
from system
\mathscr{R}
can have both quantitative and not quantitative, approximable and not approximable representatives in System
\mathtt{S}
. It once again shows that rigid constructions allow a more fine-grained control than system
\mathscr{R}
does on derivations.

E.4.
Representatives and Dynamics

A
\mathtt{S}
-derivation
P

represents
a derivation
\Pi
if the semi-rigid derivation
P_{*}

defined by
\mathtt{supp}(P_{*})=\mathtt{supp}(P)
and
P_{*}(a)=\overline{C(a)}\vdash t|_{a}:\,\overline{T(a)}
, is a semi-rigid representative of
\Pi
.
We write
P_{1}\equiv_{\mathscr{R}}P_{2}
when two
\mathtt{S}
-derivations
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
both represent the same
\mathscr{R}
-derivation
\Pi
.

Proposition 58
.

If a
\mathtt{S}
-derivation
P
is quantitative, then the
\mathscr{R}
-derivation

\overline{P}
is quantitative in system
\mathscr{R}
.

Using natural extensions (Sec.
7.2
), it easy to prove:

Proposition 59
.

If
\Pi
is a quantitative derivation typing a 001-normal form, then, there is
a quantitative rigid derivation
P
such that
\overline{P}=\Pi
.

Proof.

Let
P(\ast)
be a semi-rigid derivation representing
\Pi
. We set
A=\mathtt{supp}(P(\ast))
and for all full position
a\in A
, we choose a representative
T(\mathring{a})
of
\tau(a)
. We apply then the canonical construction of Sec.
7.2
, which yields a rigid derivation
P
such that
P_{*}=P(\ast)
(we show that, for all
a\in A
,
T(a)
represents
\tau(a)
).
∎

We can actually prove that every quantitative
\mathscr{R}
-derivation can be represented with a
\mathtt{S}
-quantitative rigid derivation and that we can endow it with every possible infinitary reduction choice
[
Via18
]
.
However, a quantitative derivation can also have a not quantitative rigid
representative, as we see below with
\Pi^{\prime}
and
\tilde{P}^{\prime}
.

We omit again the right side of axiom rules,
e.g.
,
f:((2\cdot o)\rightarrow o)_{2}
stands for
f:((2\cdot o)\rightarrow o)_{2}\vdash f:(2\cdot o)\rightarrow o
. Moreover, outer tracks are indicated between red square brackets.

P^{\prime}_{2}={\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 36.72218pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\phantom{.}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=76.22215pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle f\!:\!(2\mathord{\cdot}(2\cdot o)\rightarrow o)\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[1]}$}}}}\enskip\penalty\ \enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 36.72218pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\phantom{.}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=76.22215pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle f\!:\!(3\mathord{\cdot}(2\cdot o)\rightarrow o)\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[1]}$}}}}\enskip\penalty\ P^{\prime}_{4}\rhd f\!:\!(k\mathord{\cdot}(2\cdot o)\rightarrow o)_{k\geqslant 4}\vdash f^{\infty}:o\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[2]}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=236.93047pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 52.59207pt\hbox{$\displaystyle f:(k\mathord{\cdot}(2\cdot o)\rightarrow o)_{k\geqslant 3}\vdash f^{\infty}:o\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[2]}$}}}}\enskip\penalty$}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=338.15262pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 109.31427pt\hbox{$\displaystyle f:(k\mathord{\cdot}(2\cdot o)\rightarrow o)_{k\geqslant 2}\vdash f^{\infty}:o$}}}}

\tilde{P}^{\prime}_{2}={\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 36.72218pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\phantom{.}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=76.22215pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle f\!:\!(2\mathord{\cdot}(2\cdot o)\rightarrow o)\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[1]}$}}}}\enskip\penalty\hskip 5.69046pt\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 36.72218pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\phantom{.}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=76.22215pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle f\!:\!(4\mathord{\cdot}(2\cdot o)\rightarrow o)\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[1]}$}}}}\enskip\penalty\hskip 5.69046pt\tilde{P}^{\prime}_{5}\rhd f\!:\!(k\mathord{\cdot}(2\cdot o)\rightarrow o)_{k=3\vee k\geqslant 5}\vdash f^{\infty}:o\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[2]}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=255.30157pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 61.77763pt\hbox{$\displaystyle f:(k\mathord{\cdot}(2\cdot o)\rightarrow o)_{k\geqslant 3}\vdash f^{\infty}:o\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[2]}$}}}}\enskip\penalty$}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=357.21419pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 118.84505pt\hbox{$\displaystyle f:(k\mathord{\cdot}(2\cdot o)\rightarrow o)_{k\geqslant 2}\vdash f^{\infty}:o$}}}}

\tilde{P}^{\prime}_{k}={\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 36.9826pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\phantom{.}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=76.743pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle f\!:\!(k\mathord{\cdot}(2\cdot o)\rightarrow o)\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[1]}$}}}}\enskip\penalty\hskip 14.22636pt\tilde{P}^{\prime}_{k+1}\rhd f\!:\!(i\mathord{\cdot}(2\cdot o)\rightarrow o)_{i=3\vee i\geqslant k+1}\vdash f^{\infty}:o\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[2]}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=280.21901pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 81.96373pt\hbox{$\displaystyle f:(i\mathord{\cdot}(2\cdot o)\rightarrow o)_{i\geqslant k}\vdash f^{\infty}:o$}}}}\hskip 14.22636pt\hskip 14.22636pt\text{(for $k\geqslant 5$)}

Figure 18.
Representing
\Pi^{\prime}

•
We define
{R_{o}}
by
{R_{o}}:=(k\cdot{R_{o}})_{k\geqslant 2}\rightarrow o
and
Let
P_{k}
(
k\geqslant 2
) and
P
be the following
\mathtt{S}
-derivations:

P_{k}={\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 36.9826pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\phantom{.}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule P={\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 10.89198pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty P_{2}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[1]}\hskip 14.22636pt(P_{k}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[k-1]})_{k\geqslant 3}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=121.71175pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle f:(k\mathord{\cdot}(2\cdot o)\rightarrow o)_{k\geqslant 2}\vdash\Delta_{f}\Delta_{f}$}}}}

• Let
\tilde{P}_{k}
(
k\geqslant 2
) and
\tilde{P}
be the following
\mathtt{S}
-derivations:

\tilde{P}_{k}={\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 41.70305pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\phantom{.}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=86.18388pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle f:(k\mathord{\cdot}(2\cdot o)\rightarrow o)~\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[1]}$}}}}\enskip\penalty\ \enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x:(3\mathord{\cdot}{R_{o}})~\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[1]}$}}}}\enskip\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip x:(2\mathord{\cdot}{R_{o}})~\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[2]}$}}}}\enskip\penalty(\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 32.08186pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\phantom{.}$}}\hskip height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=232.83195pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 65.85565pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x:(i\mathord{\cdot}{R_{o}})_{i\geqslant 2}\vdash \tilde{P}={\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\kern 0.0pt\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 8.99614pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\tilde{P}_{2}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[1]}\hskip 14.22636pt(\tilde{P}_{k}\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}[k-1]})_{k\geqslant 3}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\vskip 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt$$}\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=113.41313pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle f:((2\cdot o)\rightarrow o)_{k\geqslant 2}\vdash\Delta_{f}\Delta_{f}$}}}}

• The rigid derivations
P
and
\tilde{P}
both represent

\Pi
. Intuitively, subject reduction in
P
will consist in taking the
first argument
P_{3}
, placing it on the first occurrence of
x
in

f(x\,x)
(in
P_{2}
) and putting the other
P_{k}
(
k\geqslant 4
) in the
different axiom rules typing the second occurrence of
x
in the same
order,
i.e.
, , for
k\geqslant 3
,
P_{k}
is moved from track
k-1
to track
k-2
. There is a simple decrease on the track number and we can
go this way towards
P^{\prime}_{2}
typing
f^{\infty}
.

The rigid derivation
\tilde{P}
process the same way, except it will always skip
\tilde{P}_{3}
(
\tilde{P}_{3}
will remain on track 2). Morally, we
perform subject reduction “by-hand” while avoiding to ever place
\tilde{P}_{3}
in head position,
i.e.
,
\tilde{P}_{3}
is never “consumed” by reduction.

A computation shows that infinitary reductions performed in
P
and
\tilde{P}_{2}
yield respectively to
P^{\prime}_{2}
and
\tilde{P}^{\prime}_{2}
of Fig.
18
.

Thus,
P^{\prime}
and
\tilde{P}^{\prime}
both represent
\Pi^{\prime}
(from Sec.
3.6
), but
P^{\prime}
is quantitative whereas
\tilde{P}^{\prime}
is
not (the track
3
type assigned to
f
does not end in an axiom leaf). This proves that quantitativity is not stable up to the limit of a productive reduction path and that
\tilde{P}
is not approximable by Proposition
36
.

Moreover, by Proposition
41
,
P^{\prime}_{2}
is approximable. By infinitary subject expansion,
P_{2}
also is (actually, one may also use the
\mathtt{S}
-representatives of
\Pi_{n}
and
\Pi^{\prime}_{n}
in Fig.
7
to approximate
P
and
P^{\prime}
arbitrarily).

Conclusion.
Thus,
\Pi
and
\Pi^{\prime}
have both approximable and not approximable representatives. Actually,
\Pi
has a representative which is quantitative but not approximable. This proves that approximability cannot be defined in system
\mathscr{R}
.
